Master packet · GFiber v8 transformed to Amazon Leadership Principles · STAR format · math, solution design and probe questions intact

Amazon — Supply Chain Manager III (L6) — the MASTER packet

You. Rajesh Rishiyur Chandrasekaran
What this document is. The GFiber MASTER packet v8 (Sep 13, 2026 — 255 pages) transformed for the Amazon L6 SCM loop: the same eighteen stories in the same uniform shape — 60-second pitch → SITUATION / TASK (+ probing and challenge Q&A) → ACTION: gated STAR (five phases, each with entry · delivered · exit bar; + probing and challenge Q&A and the phase-by-phase deep-probe bank) → RESULTS quantified ($ · # · % · time · customer · quality; + probing and challenge Q&A) → business-impact model (KPI tree, numbers table, math) → full step-by-step derivation → solution flow (the system & data map) → sensitivity (low/base/high) → the spoken total-impact statement → the full "if challenged" answers — mapped to Amazon's sixteen Leadership Principles instead of a JD. §3 converts the twelve system-design prompts into design engagements told from the Elementum years, each keeping its stocks-and-flows map, first-principles invariants, solution design, system map, math and deep-dive probes. §4 is the numbers ledger — one canonical value per metric — and the never-say list. This document is self-contained: nothing requires going back to the source packet.
Relation to the loop packet. This is the depth book; amazon-l6-scm-loop-packet.pdf is the loop-mechanics book (interviewer tracker, AIQB 128-question bank, writing-exercise defense, video execution). The crosswalk — including the places where the two documents currently disagree — is in §1.3. Resolve those conflicts before the loop; never carry both versions of a number into the same building.
Voice, held everywhere. TPM / SAP integration program lead. "I" throughout. SAP teams and configurators built to the signed spec; I owned the data contract, the definitions, the gates, the signatures and the calls. Never claim to configure or deploy SAP. The only platform personally configured: Manhattan DOM. The MIT Supply Chain MicroMasters + Supply Chain Network Design certificate is an acquired toolkit, labeled wherever it appears.
Basis labels, used on every number. record — in the program record, say it flat · user anchor — a figure a named stakeholder stated, attributed · industry benchmark — a published rate, named · modeled — computed from stated inputs; say "modeled" in the same breath, never promote it to record.

0. How to use this packet in the loop

  1. Night before: §1 LP map and roster → §4 never-say list → skim each story's pitch line.
  2. In the room, unprompted: the 60-SECOND PITCH only. It ends on "Primary metric — X, before → after, $ impact." Stop there. The interviewer's probes are how they fill their STAR worksheet; silence after the pitch works for you.
  3. "Walk me through it": STAR. The Action is five gated phases — Discovery → Plan & Design → Build & Test → Cutover/Go-Live → Hypercare — each spoken as entry bar · what I delivered · exit bar.
  4. "How did you actually run it": the ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A. Answer the phase asked, then stop.
  5. "How would you design…": §3. Open with the stocks-and-flows map, state the invariants, then the design, then the metric. Program methodology lives only in §3's shared section — never inside an employer story.
  6. Numbers: §4. A record dollar is said flat. Where the record holds no dollar, say the operational metric and then the modeled figure with the word "modeled" in the same breath — each story's Business impact model carries the inputs, the math and the low / base / high range. Never promote a modeled number to record.
  7. Amazon loop mechanics: five 55-minute video interviews, each interviewer assigned 2–3 Leadership Principles, STAR worksheet, notes compared afterward. One question, one story — never the same story twice across the loop. The LP map below is the routing table.

Four stories open two LPs each (S10, S12, S13, S15): if both of a pair's LPs land in one loop, the second occurrence takes that LP's backup — never the same story twice.

2. The eighteen stories — pitch, gated STAR, phase probes, and the impact math

Every story now follows the Amazon worksheet shape: 60-second pitch (say first, unprompted; ends on the primary metric) → SITUATION / TASK with its probing and challenge questions answered → ACTION as five gated phases (entry · delivered · exit) with its probing and challenge questions answered, plus the phase-by-phase ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK (answer the phase asked, then stop) → RESULTS quantified as $ · # · % · time · customer · quality with its probing and challenge questions answered, backed by the business-impact model (KPI tree, numbers table, the math line by line), the full derivation, the solution flow, the sensitivity table, the spoken total-impact statement and the full "if challenged" answers. The GFiber fit line is replaced by the Amazon LP mapping and a "where this lands at Amazon" sentence. Dates and counts inside each story are the program record; anything labeled illustrative exists only to make the math complete.

S1 — Sephora S/4HANA ↔ ISM: one signed meaning of "available" across Store, Retail DC and Dotcom DC — policy before configuration

Amazon LPs: Earn Trust (backup) · Ownership (backup) · Think Big (third — the book-of-record migration) · Broad Responsibility (oversell is a customer harm). Crosswalk: loop packet S2 — same program, consistent. Recency: current role.

Honesty label. Integration program lead — no system reported to me; every configurator sat in another org. I owned the definitions, the asymmetry, the decrement and reconciliation contract, the signature chain and the council ask; engineers configured and coded to the FSD; planners executed the business acts.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Sephora was moving the inventory book of record from the in-house MCS to SAP S/4HANA, with ISM stood up beside it for the real-time view; Store, Retail DC and Dotcom DC each meant something different by 'available,' and most oversell bugs are vocabulary bugs. Task. As the integration program lead for inventory supply — no system reported to me — I had to define every bucket, its owning system, and how available-for-sale is computed and decremented in real time, and get Planning, Finance and operations to sign it before configuration. Action. Discovery: a bucket inventory with one proposed owner per bucket; the bar was every bucket owned. Plan & Design: the lower-of-two basis, the AFS equation, the real-time decrement contract with idempotent events, daily reconciliation to S/4 — FSD v1.1 → v1.2 → signed 6/5/25 by three orgs. Build & Test: node × channel × bucket-transition matrix; E2E Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30. Cutover: January planning go-live, then May execution; OMS reads published AFS, never computes; MCS retired as book of record. Hypercare: daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift; control owners on Negative AFS. Lesson: policy before configuration. Result. Primary metric: customer-facing oversell incidents — recurring across three node types → zero through two go-lives; one signed AFS per node per channel. $ impact: ≈$2.5M/yr — $1M measured MCS support cost retired, plus ≈$1.5M modeled (avoided oversell, dual-book reconciliation labor, legacy change premium); the record has no pre-state oversell dollar, so the $1.5M is my model and I say so."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Sephora moved the book of record from MCS to S/4HANA, with ISM beside it to see digital demand S/4 cannot see in real time — a web order placed, a BOPIS order packed, a POS sale rung but not yet posted. Three node types, three working definitions of "available." Merchandising wanted merchandise reserve visible, Finance wanted on-hand to tie to the book, dotcom wanted in-transit promisable. Nobody owned the definition; I owned no system.

T — Own the integration design and data contract of inventory supply: which buckets exist, which system owns each, how available-for-sale is computed per node, and how it feeds promising, replenishment and Finance.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

Most oversell bugs are vocabulary bugs: Store, Retail DC and Dotcom DC each meant something different by “available,” and Sephora was moving the inventory book of record from MCS to S/4HANA with ISM beside it for the real-time view. The goal, written with the VP Supply Chain in the sponsor memo: one signed AFS per node per channel, one Financial On-Hand, zero customer-impacting incidents.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

Scope was the integration design and data contract of inventory supply — which buckets exist, which system owns each, how available-for-sale is computed and decremented in real time, and how it feeds promising, replenishment and Finance. The challenges: three node types with three working definitions, Merchandising, Finance and dotcom each wanting a different truth, nobody owning the definition — and I owned no system.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Recurring vocabulary-driven oversells — a customer harm, not a ledger error — plus two books reconciled by hand, the MCS run cost continuing, and a legacy change premium on an undocumented stack. Vocabulary drift — a bucket without an owner — sat at the top of my risk register.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Earn Trust?

Because the deliverable was one signed truth across three orgs that did not report to me — Supply Chain Strategy, Inventory Management and FSC Inventory Accounting each signed the FSD that carried the definitions. One signature would have left two orgs free to disagree later.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Earn Trust?

S14 — the $2.5M OMS↔WMS discrepancy, transparency over a quiet fix — is my primary Earn Trust story; S3 (Finance and Ops on one number) and S5 (the council) carry the same LP. This story is the Earn Trust backup in my map.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is from my current role at Sephora — the FSD was signed 6/5/25 and the two go-lives ran January and May — so it is the most recent record I have for this principle.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (record dates: kickoff 3/29 → council 5/15 → LOE 7/2 → sign-off 7/31 → FSD 8/14 → peak freeze Oct–Dec; ISM FSD v1.1 3/5/25 → v1.2 5/16/25 → signed 6/5/25; E2E Inbound 11/22 · Dotcom 12/20 · Retail 1/30; issues log 836 rows / 85.4% closed. The dated gate chain, the issues log and the rollback triggers are program-level artifacts shared with S2/S5 — one umbrella program; if two of these stories go to one loop, say so):

  1. Discovery — profiled both books (MCS and S/4) for SKU overlap and order profiles per node; node-by-node sessions with ~15 functions; built the bucket inventory with one proposed owning system per bucket. Exit bar: every bucket owned by exactly one system; business sessions closed 4/30; open questions carried with owner and date.
  2. Plan & Design — wrote the lower-of-two basis, the AFS/FOH equations, the real-time decrement contract (event → bucket delta, idempotency key per event), the daily ISM-vs-MB52 reconciliation into FSD v1.1 → v1.2, the RAID log and the council pack (S5). Exit bar: FSD signed 6/5/25 by Supply Chain Strategy, Inventory Management and FSC Inventory Accounting with zero open "who owns this bucket" rows.
  3. Build & Test — authored the node × channel × bucket-transition matrix with before/after assertions; SIT on the interface contracts before UAT; published the E2E chain dates. Exit bar: 0 open contract defects in SIT; E2E green 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30; Blocker + Critical open = 0 at go/no-go.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — wrote the go/no-go recommendation from counted rows, the publish-switch plan (OMS reads ISM AFS, never computes) and the reserve-copy invariants; assigned control-screen roles. Exit bar: January go-live (reserve rules live against demand), May go-live (execution); no SKU with two published AFS values; Negative AFS clean at T+1.
  5. Hypercare / Close — daily drift report (ISM vs MB52), Negative AFS / AFS Correction audit review, hypercare exit memo and FSD as-built note. Exit bar: drift = 0 SKUs on consecutive days; blockers/day under threshold; exited into the monthly release train.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

The core is the asymmetric lower-of-two supply basis — Basis = min(S4_Avail, WMS_OnHand) — with AFS = Basis minus every committed-or-sold-not-posted bucket, a real-time decrement contract keyed by idempotent events, and daily ISM-vs-MB52 reconciliation at tolerance zero. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

Integration program lead — no system reported to me and every configurator sat in another org. I owned the definitions, the asymmetry, the decrement and reconciliation contract, the signature chain and the council ask; engineers configured and coded to the FSD; planners executed the business acts.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Taking the definition nobody owned and getting it signed before configuration — the lower-of-two basis, the AFS equation and the real-time decrement contract written into an FSD three orgs signed on 6/5/25. Policy before configuration is the unique value.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Three orgs each defending a different truth about “available,” and sixty people to move without authority. I pre-wired every row of the systems-impacted table with its owner's level-of-effort before the council, ran working sessions to one table instead of arbitrating in meetings, and converted objections into FSD rows — Merchandising's merch-reserve ask was priced and answered with visibility without promisability.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the bucket inventory, the equations, the decrement contract, the node × channel × bucket-transition matrix design, the go/no-go recommendation and the signature chain. The team's: SAP teams and configurators built to the signed spec; planners confirmed in UAT; control owners ran the Negative AFS and AFS Correction screens after handoff.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Epics were slotted by business criticality × effort, and a story could not enter QA without a matrix cell reference. Buy-in came from pre-wiring: nobody learned their cost in the council room, and the council was asked for three decisions rather than a review.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

Merchandising wanted merch reserve promisable — I priced it (unpickable stock sold) and the no went into the FSD as a row, not a meeting minute. I also held the asymmetry itself: oversell is a customer defect; undersell is a planning variance Inventory Control chases through the Negative AFS and AFS Correction screens.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: recurring vocabulary-driven oversells → zero through two go-lives; one signed AFS per node per channel; MCS retired. $ impact: ≈$2.5M/yr ($1M user anchor + ≈$1.5M modeled — model below).

  • $ — ≈$2.5M/yr — $1M MCS run/support cost retired (user anchor) + ≈$1.5M modeled (avoided oversell, dual-book reconciliation labor, legacy change premium); the record has no pre-state oversell dollar, so the $1.5M is my model and I say so.
  • # — Oversell incidents recurring across three node types → zero through two go-lives (record); dual-book reconciliation ≈4 FTE by hand → ≈1 FTE reading the drift report (modeled).
  • % — Issues log 85.4% of 836 rows closed (program-level artifact shared with S2/S5); the oversell rate behind the ≈$0.9M row is 0.10% of orders — modeled, benchmark 0.05–0.25%; the record movement is incidents → zero.
  • Time — Dated gate chain held: FSD v1.1 3/5/25 → v1.2 5/16/25 → signed 6/5/25; E2E Inbound 11/22 · Dotcom 12/20 · Retail 1/30; January planning and May execution go-lives (record dates).
  • Customer — Customer-facing oversell incidents zero through two go-lives; the asymmetry treats oversell as a customer defect and undersell as a planning variance (record).
  • Quality — One signed AFS per node per channel; no SKU with two published AFS values; daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift = 0 SKUs on consecutive days at hypercare exit (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Oversell was the customer-facing defect the whole program existed to kill, so incidents-to-zero and one signed AFS per node per channel lead. The other results that mattered: MCS retired as book of record, reconciliation moved from argument to a daily drift report with a named owner, and the E2E chain landing on its dates.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

There is no year-over-year revenue record here — the ≈$2.5M/yr is $1M of measured MCS run cost retired (user anchor) plus ≈$1.5M modeled against the pre-state baseline, not a YoY revenue change. The record movement is incidents: recurring → zero through two go-lives.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

The asymmetry is a priced trade: a positive out-of-balance reduces supply and a surplus stays unpromised — accepting visible undersell to make oversell zero by construction. Merch reserve got visibility without promisability, because unpickable stock sold was never worth the sale.

Probing — I'm concerned about the 0.10% oversell rate — tell me more.

I don't know it to the decimal — the record says incidents were recurring and went to zero; the rate is a benchmark set deliberately low, a twentieth of a typical e-commerce inventory-cancel rate. First thing I'd measure: the “item unavailable after order” cancel-reason code for the twelve months before January.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Policy before configuration is the lesson that held. Differently: the in-transit rule (S4) — back-test a ruleset against history before publishing it.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Every fulfillment promise rests on one published availability number per node that downstream systems read and never recompute — at Amazon scale that is the ATP/promise seam between IPC-style planning and the order pipeline: one definition, one owner, policy signed before code.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The signed criteria were one AFS per node per channel, one Financial On-Hand and zero customer-impacting incidents — all three held through both go-lives, with drift at zero SKUs on consecutive days at hypercare exit and MCS retired as book of record.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (15). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When every bucket on the table had exactly one proposed owning system and no open "who owns this" row. Sessions closed 4/30; the exit memo listed remaining questions with an owner and a date, not a hope.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote them with the VP Supply Chain in the sponsor memo — one signed AFS per node per channel, one Financial On-Hand, zero customer-impacting incidents. Supply Chain Strategy, Inventory Management and FSC Inventory Accounting signed the FSD that carried them.

What did you profile before designing?

Both books — MCS and S/4 — for SKU overlap and order profiles per node type, so the design argument started from their own data, not opinions about it.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Three at the top: vocabulary drift (a bucket without an owner), feed latency from WMS/OMS/POS, and override abuse on AFS-changing screens. Each had a trigger, a mitigation and an owner — mine was drift.

How did you handle an objection at the design review?

Merchandising wanted merch reserve promisable. I priced it — unpickable stock sold — and offered visibility without promisability. The no went into the FSD as a row, not into a meeting minute.

Why three signatories, not one?

Each owned a different truth: Strategy owned policy, Inventory Management owned execution, Inventory Accounting owned the book. One signature would have left two orgs free to disagree later.

Build & Test — How did you sequence stories in Jira?

Epics per module — reserve module, AFS engine, SNB, control screens — slotted by business criticality × effort; a story could not enter QA without a matrix cell reference.

How were UAT entrance criteria set and who could fail a build?

Entrance: SIT contract defects closed, test data handed off, environments ready. QA owned the gate and could fail a build; planners confirmed in UAT because QA had already demoed each matrix family during build.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The dated E2E days — Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30. Business and engineering ran the full flow in one room; every finding got a severity and owner before we broke.

Cutover — What made you willing to say go?

Counted rows: Blocker + Critical at zero, E2E green, the monitor for the interim copy built and tested, rollback triggers armed. Never confidence in the room.

What were your rollback triggers and did any fire?

Sync below 99% for four consecutive hours, or order-flow p95 above twice budget for an hour — consumers revert to the prior publish. Armed for both go-lives; never fired.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over?

Daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift and blockers/day under threshold on consecutive days, and the control owners — Inventory Control, Internal Audit, Finance — running the Negative AFS report without me. Then the design entered the monthly release train.

What did hypercare hand over, and to whom?

The control-owner map: Negative AFS to Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance; AFS Correction to the ISM app team; the daily drift report to Inventory Control. Every AFS-changing act had a screen, a role and an audit row before I stepped back.

Cross-phase — You owned no system; how did you get sixty people to sign?

Pre-wired every row of the systems-impacted table with its owner's level-of-effort before the council, ran working sessions to one table instead of arbitrating in meetings, and asked the council for three decisions rather than a review. A no that lives in a document survives; a no that lives in a meeting gets re-litigated weekly.

Why lower-of-two and not an average? · What would you do differently?

Oversell is a customer defect; undersell is a planning variance Inventory Control chases through the Negative AFS and AFS Correction screens — the asymmetry is the policy. Differently: the in-transit rule (S4) — back-test a ruleset against history before publishing it.

Where this lands at Amazon: every fulfillment promise rests on one published availability number per node that downstream systems read and never recompute — the exact contract I held the OMS to. At Amazon scale the same discipline is the ATP/promise seam between IPC-style planning and the order pipeline: one definition, one owner, policy signed before code.

Gross margin + customer trust (primary outcome)

+- Dual-book reconciliation labor (stock: FTE) -> 1 FTE + drift report
|    (loop) more drift -> more manual recon -> slower fixes -> more drift
+- Legacy run cost (MCS support, $/yr) -> retired
+- Tech-debt change premium (changes/yr x extra hrs x rate) -> S/4 + ISM
+- Book accuracy -> unexplained shrink / audit-finding risk
+- Time-to-market for a new node or channel (weeks) -> a matrix row

If challenged. "How do you know it was a tenth of a percent?" — "I don't, to the decimal. The record says incidents were recurring and went to zero; the rate is a benchmark set deliberately low — a twentieth of a typical e-commerce inventory-cancel rate. First thing I'd measure: the 'item unavailable after order' cancel-reason code for the twelve months before January." "Does the $1M already include your change premium?" — "It might. If enhancement labor sits inside it, I drop the row and the headline is about $2.2M. I'd split the MCS cost center into run vs change tickets for one fiscal year." "Three FTE sounds like a guess." — "It's a benchmark count. What's in the record is the mechanism: reconciliation moved from argument to a daily drift report with tolerance zero and a named owner. I'd size it with a two-week time study — how I sized the Austria effort at Elementum."

The math — full derivation, step by step

Supply basis (asymmetric lower-of-two). For SKU i at node n: OOB_in = S4_Avail_in −
  WMS_OnHand_in (out-of-balance; + = book above floor, − = floor above book) Basis_in =
  min(S4_Avail_in, WMS_OnHand_in) — a positive OOB reduces supply; a negative OOB is never
  promised until S/4 books it.
Available-for-sale. AFS_in = Basis_in − PickRes_in − EcomPick_in − EcomPack_in − SNB_in , where
  every subtrahend is a committed-or-sold-not-posted bucket. Merchandise Reserve and Lost/Found
  are outside S4_Avail by construction, so they never appear in the subtraction — no
  double-subtract path exists.
Financial On-Hand (DC). FOH_n = Avail_n + MerchRes_n + LostFound_n (physical truth for Finance;
  AFS is the promising truth for channels).
Real-time decrement (the ISM contract). Between two S/4 postings, ISM keeps AFS current from
  events, never from re-reading the book: AFS_in(t) = AFS_in(t_0) − Σ_{e ∈ (t_0, t]} Δ_e , where
  each event e (OMS commit / pick / pack / cancel, POS sale line) maps to exactly one bucket
  delta by the decision-rules table. Every event carries an idempotency key ( source ×
  OrderLine- or-Txn × line × event type ); a replayed key is a no-op, a reversal must reference
  its original, so Σ Δ_e is the same whether an event arrives once or five times. Per-key
  ordering is per SKU × node, so a pick can never be applied before its commit.
Reconciliation (daily, tolerance 0). Drift_in = ISM.S4_Avail_in − MB52_in per SKU × SLoc at the
  daily snapshot; Drift ≠ 0 is a Sev, not a planning variance. Month-end: Σ_n FOH_n ties to MB5L
  quantity and value. SNB is the reconciling bucket between the two books: S4_Avail_in(after
  posting) = S4_Avail_in(before) − SNB_matched_in , so ISM decrements SNB on the matched
  aggregate posting only (S3) and SNB can never go negative.
Worked example (consistent with v105 Part 4 Ex. 1). S/4 Available 1,000 units; WMS on-hand 980 →
  OOB = +20 → Basis = 980. Picking Reserve 200, Ecom Pick 120, Ecom Pack 60, SNB 40 (Σ = 420) →
  AFS = 980 − 420 = 560. Flip the floor: WMS 1,020 → OOB = −20 → Basis = min(1,000, 1,020) =
  1,000 → AFS = 580, not 600 — the 20-unit surplus stays unpromised. Decrement example
  (illustrative): a store rings 3 units at 10:02 → SNB 40 → 43, AFS 560 → 557 within the 60 s
  publish budget; the POS feed replays the same transaction at 10:04 → same key, no-op, AFS
  stays 557; the aggregate posting lands in S/4 overnight for 43 units → SNB 43 → 0, S4_Avail
  1,000 → 957, and the next-morning drift check reads 957 − MB52 957 = 0 . Sanity: AFS ≤ Basis ≤
  both books; never a negative published number (Negative AFS is a report, not a value channels
  read). The store-node walk is the same with Basis = S4_Avail (no WMS floor) and SNB as the
  only sold-not-posted bucket; the DC walks (Retail DC, Dotcom DC) carry the WMS floor and the
  OMS buckets. Comingle pool partitioning — the nine-reserve priority walk — is S2's math.
Oversell exposure (why asymmetry). Expected oversell units if surplus were promised: E[oversell]
  = Σ_i P(WMS surplus is phantom) × surplus_i . With no measured phantom rate at design time,
  the policy sets that probability to 1 (promise nothing unbooked) and lets the Negative AFS /
  AFS Correction screens chase the under-sell — a priced choice: under-sell is a planning
  variance; over-sell is a customer defect.

Solution flow — the system & data map

 [Aptos POS]      [OMS Yantriks]      [BlueYonder WMS]     [FRAME planning]
  sale line        commit/pick/pack/   on-hand by item x    channel reserves,
  (Kafka topic)    cancel events       loc x status         HUD holds
    | +SNB,          | bucket delta,     | WMS_OnHand,        | reserve input,
    | real-time      | real-time         | <=15 min feed       | nightly + HUD

     ^  MB52 snapshot, daily      |  published AFS per      |  FOH + SNB,
     |  + MSEG 101/311/601/701/   |  SKU x node x channel,  |  after match
     |  702 as events, real-time  |  publish-only           v  cycle
 +----------------------+           v                   [S/4 FI / Finance]
 | SAP S/4HANA MM-IM    |     [OMS / dotcom / stores]    MB5L tie, month-end
 | [BoR] physical book  |      read AFS, never compute
 | MARD / MSEG / MB52   |

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Conservatively, about two and a half million dollars a year. One million of that is measured: the MCS support cost that went away when S/4 became the book of record. About nine hundred thousand is avoided oversell: on roughly thirty million dotcom orders, a tenth of a percent going wrong at about thirty dollars each, and the record shows those incidents went to zero through both go-lives. About three hundred thousand is reconciliation labor, three analysts who stopped reconciling two books by hand, and about three hundred thousand is the change premium we stopped paying on an undocumented stack. The first number is measured; the other three are my model, and I can show you every input."

S2 — Sephora UDC: two warehouses in one building → one comingle pool, coupled with a BlueYonder upgrade and robotics behind a published gate

Amazon LPs: Insist on the Highest Standards (primary — the 98% gate) · Deliver Results · Have Backbone (the throttle) · Earth's Best Employer (the floor protected). Crosswalk: loop packet S11 + S24 — consistent. Recency: current role.

Honesty label. I owned discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging and the gates; BlueYonder, Locus and the platform teams built and configured; DC Ops ran the floor. The +15% is Sephora's signed number; every dollar is my model on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Sephora's Utah DC ran as two systemic warehouses in one building, with a BlueYonder upgrade and Locus robotics on the same path and zero customer-facing incidents allowed. Task. I owned discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging, and the gates — a dozen core systems, 20+ touched, 60+ stakeholders. Action. Discovery: both books profiled; systems-impacted table with owners. Plan & Design: one comingle pool, three planning identities, two go-lives so each carried one defect class. Build & Test: node × channel × bucket matrix; robotics gate published at 98% throughput with a throttle built in advance. Cutover: six-step May choreography — clear negatives, transfer AFS to Uncommitted, scan-and-reserve, zero-then-publish so nothing was sellable in two places. Hypercare: week two hit 94; I throttled bots to ~30% of volume, root-caused zone/slotting logic, full volume week three. Lesson: a gate that cannot stop the program is decoration — mine did. Result. Primary metric: DC processing efficiency — baseline → +15% after one comingle pool replaced two systemic warehouses; customer-facing incidents zero through both go-lives. $ impact: ≈$1.6M/yr modeled (labor at +15%, pooling carry, fewer split shipments, self-transfer handling removed) — the +15% is the signed number; the dollars are my model."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The Utah DC ran as two systemic warehouses — Dotcom 801 (selling location 800) and Retail 901 — transferring stock to itself and paying duplicate handling for zero customer value. The ask: one omni/comingle facility, with a BlueYonder WMS 8.2 → 2023.1 upgrade and Locus robotics on the same critical path, a dozen core systems (20+ touched), an Oct–Dec peak freeze, zero customer-facing incidents allowed.

T — Discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging, and the acceptance gates; 60+ stakeholders on the steering path.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

The Utah DC ran as two systemic warehouses in one building — Dotcom 801 and Retail 901 — transferring stock to itself and paying duplicate handling for zero customer value. The signed objective: one omni/comingle facility, with a BlueYonder WMS 8.2 → 2023.1 upgrade and Locus robotics on the same critical path, zero customer-facing incidents allowed, peak untouched.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

I owned discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging and the acceptance gates — a dozen core systems, 20+ touched, 60+ stakeholders on the steering path. The challenges: a WMS upgrade and a robotics ramp on the same path, an Oct–Dec peak freeze, and two live truths during the interim.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Continued duplicate handling and split shipments from the DC shipping to itself; and once the program ran, the register's top risks were two live truths during the interim, a robotics ramp with no stop condition, and a peak collision — each given a trigger, a mitigation and an owner before sign-off.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

The 98% robotics gate was set from the pre-ramp baseline and the incident budget before the event — and when week two hit 94, it fired: I throttled bots to ~30% of volume rather than let the ramp erode the floor. A gate that cannot stop the program is decoration; mine did.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

S18 — the 0.5% reconciliation tolerance published with Finance before mock one — is this LP's backup, and S9's ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, held twice, is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is my current role — the January and May go-lives and the Locus ramp are the most recent record I have for this principle.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

The core mechanics: the 900/901 location and inventory model with three planning identities over one physical location, the nine-reserve priority walk partitioning the comingle pool, and the six-step May choreography with zero-then-publish so no SKU was sellable in two places at once. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging and the gates; BlueYonder, Locus and the platform teams built and configured; DC Ops ran the floor. The +15% is Sephora's signed number; every dollar is my model on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

The design that let one physical pool serve both channels without blurring anyone's lens — three planning identities over one location — plus the published 98% robotics gate with a throttle dial built in advance, and the staged two-go-live timeline so each go-live carried one defect class.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

The Locus RTL slipped three weeks — escalated the week it surfaced, re-slotted openly, and I refused to compress UAT to absorb it. Digital worried a virtual Retail DC would blur their forecasting lens — I kept three planning identities over one physical location, so the concern became the design, not an argument.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Theirs: BlueYonder, Locus and the platform teams built and configured; DC Ops ran the floor and measured the +15% post-stabilization.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

~30 build tracks under one log, stories slotted by business criticality × effort, Blocker + Critical open = 0 as the gate. Buy-in: steering (DC Ops, IT, Supply Chain Strategy) signed the success criteria in the council pack, and DC Ops owned the robotics gate and the dial before the ramp — the rule fired, I didn't improvise.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

I challenged the single-go-live instinct — two go-lives so we never hunted two defect classes in the same week — and held the comingle-reserve improvements out of the release to protect zero incidents, priced as a later enhancement at steering.

R — Primary metric: processing efficiency +15% (record, Sephora signed); zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives; robotics gate held at 94 vs 98, full volume week three. $ impact: ≈$1.6M/yr modeled + ≈$2.2M one-time safety-stock release (model below).

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY revenue figure — the +15% is processing efficiency (units per labor-hour) against the pre-UDC baseline, Sephora-signed, and the ≈$1.6M/yr is my model on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC, not a revenue change.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

The comingle-reserve improvements were traded out of the release to protect zero incidents — priced as a later enhancement at steering. The throttle traded a week of robot volume for a root-caused ramp, and for four months every report had to declare which system of record it read — the interim's price.

Probing — I'm concerned about the labor number behind the modeled ≈$0.9M — tell me more.

The fifteen percent is measured; the headcount and the $28 loaded rate are my assumptions. Pull the DC's actual direct hours for the two quarters before and after and multiply by payroll — that turns the model into a record in one afternoon.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

A gate that cannot stop the program is decoration — mine did, at 94. Differently: staff the interim reporting workstream on day one, not month three.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

This is an FC launch/retrofit with automation on the critical path — publish the ramp gate and the throttle before the robots arrive, stage go-lives so each carries one defect class, and hold peak sacred. Zero-then-publish is the same discipline as never letting one unit be promisable from two nodes at once.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The steering charter asked for one omni facility, self-transfers eliminated, zero customer-facing incidents and peak untouched — all four delivered, with +15% efficiency measured by DC Ops post-stabilization and the robotics at full volume week three.

Two live truths during the interim, a robotics ramp with no stop condition, and a peak collision. Each had a trigger, a mitigation and an owner — the shadow monitor, the 98% gate with a throttle dial, and the freeze in the pack.

The burn-down, not the room: Blocker + Critical at zero, mock 3 inside the 0.5% tolerance (the discipline S18 established, reused here), runbook rehearsed, rollback armed. I would have said no-go on one open Blocker; I never had to.

Sync < 99% for four consecutive hours, or order-flow p95 > 2× budget for one hour — per-interface rollback behind them. Armed for both go-lives; never fired.

343 tasks, 48,885 task-minutes — about 815 hours — May 1 to Jul 1, each task with an owner, duration and predecessor, and step 4 (zero-then-publish) as the one irreversible instant.

The Locus ramp at week 2: 94 against a published 98. I throttled to ~30% for a week. DC Ops took it well because the gate and the dial were theirs before the ramp — the rule fired, I didn't improvise.

One go-live would mean hunting two defect classes in one week. Differently: staff the interim reporting workstream on day one, not month three — for four months every report had to declare which system of record it read.

The zero-then-publish invariant is the same discipline as never letting one unit be promisable from two nodes at once.

poolable SS = $50M avg inv x 25% SS share x 60% overlap = $7.5M
pooling factor (2 equal pools -> 1) = 1 - 1/sqrt(2) = 0.293 -> release $2.2M one-time
carry = $2.2M x 10% = $0.22M/yr · splits = 45,000 x $10 = $0.45M/yr
self-transfer = 260K x $0.80 x 50% (rest already in labor row) = $0.1M/yr
total = 0.88 + 0.22 + 0.45 + 0.10 = ~$1.6M/yr
Sensitivity: low ~$1.1M · base ~$1.6M · high ~$2.4M (FTE 100-150, $24-32/h, capital 8-12%)

If challenged. "The labor number is the biggest — how firm?" — "The fifteen percent is measured; the headcount and the $28 loaded rate are my assumptions. Pull the DC's actual direct hours for the two quarters before and after and multiply by payroll — that turns the model into a record in one afternoon." "Pooling sounds like a textbook claim." — "It is, and I labeled it. √2 pooling assumes two roughly equal independent pools on the same SKUs. Measure SKU-level on-hand on the last day of 801 vs the first stable month of 901; the release is whatever the difference actually is." "Split shipments — evidence?" — "The softest row. Parcels per dotcom order from the ship system before and after May, times the carrier's per-parcel cost. If the point didn't move, I drop the row and the total is about $1.1M."

The math — full derivation, step by step

Throughput gate. T = pick-accurate completed tasks / tasks attempted per week. Gate published
  before ramp: T ≥ 0.98 (stretch 0.99). Week 2 observed 0.94 → gate failed by 4 pts.
Throttle rule. Bot share of volume s drops from 100% to s_throttle until root cause is cleared:
  s_throttle = 30% (dial built in advance). Manual capacity must cover (1 − s) × V_week ; with
  V_week = weekly units, manual load = 0.70 × V. Illustrative: V = 100,000 units/week → bots
  30,000, manual 70,000 — the floor staffing check is 70,000 / (units per labor-hour × hours
  available) ≤ 1 .
Incident budget. Zero customer-facing incidents allowed
any defect that could reach a customer must be caught at a gate: P(customer incident) ≈ Σ_gates
  (1 − catch_rate_g) × P(defect_g) . Staging two go-lives halves the defect classes live at
  once, so the joint probability of two interacting classes in one week is removed by
  construction.
Interim monitor. |AFS_900 − AFS_801| > 0 on any SKU after the copy  Sev. Tolerance is 0 units,
  not a percentage, because 900 is defined as a copy of 801.
Rollback triggers. sync_rate = synced records / expected records ; fire if sync_rate < 0.99 for
  4 consecutive hours, or order-flow p95 latency > 2 × budget for 1 hour. Illustrative: budget
  p95 = 30 s  trigger at > 60 s sustained 60 min. Comingle reserve partition (nine reserve
  types, strict priority — replicated 100% from Canada). The single physical pool P on 901 is
  partitioned for both channels by a priority walk over reserve types r_k , k = 1..9 (Held
  first, then the DOT and Retail hard reserves, then the softer channel and launch reserves,
  then Uncommitted as the remainder): alloc_k = min(r_k, P − Σ_{j<k} alloc_j) , Uncommitted = P
  − Σ_k alloc_k . The walk stops when the pool is exhausted, so the lowest tier is truncated
  first and no channel can starve another out of order. Worked example (v105 figures, split
  illustrative): pool 4,900 − Held 50 − DOT hard 1,550 − Retail hard 2,700 = Uncommitted 600.
  Sanity: Σ alloc_k ≤ P , Uncommitted ≥ 0 . Why not tune it for the US: the Canada engine was
  already proven; a second engine is a second defect class in the same release — the fence in
  the council pack (S5). The comingle-reserve improvements were the scope I traded for zero
  incidents. S1 reads these reserves as a subtrahend input to AFS; the walk itself lives here.
Cutover invariants (six-step May choreography). Σ AFS_801 (before) = Σ Uncommitted_901 (after)
  per SKU; negatives on 801 cleared first ( AFS_801 < 0 ⇒ set 0 with audit ) because a
  transferred negative is invented inventory; Yantriks zero-then- publish: AFS_pub(800) := 0
  before AFS_pub(900) := AFS_901 , so at no instant is a SKU sellable on both.
Result metric. Processing efficiency +15% (canonical; definition per program = units processed
  per labor-hour vs pre-UDC baseline).
Issues log: 836 rows, 85.4% closed; post-go-live spike 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4 (weekly open
  counts) — a geometric burn-down with ratio ≈ 0.3–0.7 per week.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Conservatively, about one point six million a year: roughly nine hundred thousand of labor, because the same volume takes thirteen percent fewer hours at plus fifteen percent efficiency; about two hundred thousand a year of carry on the two point two million of safety stock one pool releases versus two; about four hundred fifty thousand from fewer split shipments; and about a hundred thousand from the DC no longer shipping to itself. The plus fifteen percent and the zero incidents are measured and signed by Sephora; every dollar figure is my model on an illustrative hundred-thousand-units-a-week DC, and I will tell you which input each one rests on."

S3 — Sephora S/4HANA: Shipped-Not-Billed and de-aggregation — Finance and Operations on one Financial On-Hand number

Amazon LPs: Earn Trust (backup — Finance and Ops on one number) · Dive Deep (the grain problem) · Invent and Simplify. Crosswalk: not in the loop packet — safe anywhere. Recency: current role.

Honesty label. The flow, controls and ownership map were mine; S/4 and OMS engineers implemented. The record has no canonical dollar for the pre-state drift; the ≈$0.6M/yr is my model and I say so.
60-second pitch

"Situation. S/4 posts sales as aggregates while POS sends lines, so between sale and posting units were gone but the book was not decremented — Finance and operations reconciled by argument. Task. Define how the real-time layer tracks that gap so promising, replenishment and Finance agree, under a zero-incident constraint. Action. Discovery: profiled the posting-vs-line grain and named the phantom-availability window. Plan & Design: SNB incremented by POS/OMS/WMS events, decremented only on matched S/4 aggregate; de-aggregation pro-rates COGS per line; failure direction under-states, never over-states. Build & Test: Aggregation# join in the matrix; control owner per bucket. Cutover: publish Financial On-Hand and updated SNB back to S/4. Hypercare: unmatched postings age on a report, not as phantom AFS. Lesson: design the failure direction before the happy path. Result. Primary metric: Financial On-Hand definitions — two (Finance book vs operations count) → one shared FOH, availability decremented at sale/ship rather than at posting; zero incidents. $ impact: ≈$0.6M/yr modeled (phantom-availability cancels avoided + Finance reconciliation labor + month-end close time) — one FOH is the signed result; the record has no pre-state drift dollar and I am not claiming one."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — S/4 posts sales as aggregates — one posting per Aggregation# with total quantity and COGS; the Aptos POS sends transaction lines with an Order#. Between the sale and the posting, units are gone but the book is not decremented — the phantom-availability window — and Finance and Operations read different on-hand numbers and reconcile by argument.

T — Define how ISM tracks the gap in real time and reconciles it so promising, replenishment and Finance agree, with the program's zero-incident constraint applying to the book as much as to the customer.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

S/4 posts sales as aggregates while the Aptos POS sends lines, so between the sale and the posting units were gone but the book was not decremented — the phantom-availability window — and Finance and Operations read different on-hand numbers and reconciled by argument. The goal: one Financial On-Hand, AFS decremented at sale rather than at posting, with a safe failure direction.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

Define how ISM tracks the gap in real time and reconciles it so promising, replenishment and Finance agree, with the program's zero-incident constraint applying to the book as much as to the customer. The challenges: the grain mismatch (one posting per Aggregation# vs transaction lines), a possible double-subtraction path, and unmatched postings accumulating silently.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

The phantom-availability window stays open — units sold but still showing available — with two books reconciled by argument, a bridge spreadsheet at every month-end close, and the gap hiding as oversell if an estimate ever decremented the book.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Earn Trust?

Because the result is Finance and Operations trusting one number — Financial On-Hand truths went from two to one, signed inside the FSD on 6/5/25. Finance wanted their own reconciliation; I gave them the real report to own instead — a parallel spreadsheet dies when its owner owns the real report.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Earn Trust?

S14 — the $2.5M discrepancy — is my primary Earn Trust story, and S1, one signed “available,” is the other backup from the same program family.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Current role — SNB rode the ISM program's dated chain to the May execution go-live, so this is among the most recent records I have for this principle.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (SNB rode the ISM program's dated chain — FSD signed 6/5/25; E2E 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30; May execution go-live):

  1. Discovery — problem statement (the phantom-availability window), the event inventory (POS flash sale, omni pending, ship-to-home confirm, S/4 Aggregation# posting), the who-reads-which-number map. Exit bar: Finance and Ops agree on the description of the gap and which events move units before posting; Internal Audit named as reviewer.
  2. Plan & Design — SNB bucket definition (increment on events, decrement only on matched posting); join spec Aggregation# ↔ Aptos header ↔ lines; per-line COGS pro-rata de-aggregation; the failure-direction statement (unmatched ⇒ SNB stays high); control-owner map into the FSD. Exit bar: signed inside the FSD 6/5/25; Finance accepts one FOH; WAC$ confirmed S/4-owned, ISM visibility-only.
  3. Build & Test — de-aggregation test family (n lines; missing line; duplicate Aptos header); the SNB-never-negative assertion; unmatched-posting aging report spec. Exit bar: in SIT every posting matches or ages visibly; Σ per-line COGS = posting COGS to the cent in every cell; UAT = confirmation by Inventory Accounting.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — riding the May go-live: SNB opening balance reconciled to unposted sales at the snapshot; SNB publish back to S/4 enabled. Exit bar: aging report empty or explained at T+1; no negative SNB; Finance reads FOH from ISM.
  5. Hypercare / Close — daily aging review with Inventory Accounting; control-owner handoff in the exit memo. Exit bar: aging report owned by Finance without me; month-end FOH ties to MB5L (mechanism canonical; days-to-tie not in record).

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

SNB increments on POS/OMS/WMS events and decrements only on a matched S/4 aggregate posting; de-aggregation pro-rates COGS per line so Σ per-line COGS equals the posting COGS to the cent; the failure direction under-states availability by construction. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

The flow, controls and ownership map were mine; S/4 and OMS engineers implemented. The record has no canonical dollar for the pre-state drift; the ≈$0.6M/yr is my model and I say so.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Designing the failure direction before the happy path — an unmatched posting keeps SNB high, so AFS under-states and Replenishment over-orders visibly instead of overselling invisibly — and making the aging report, with a named Finance owner, the real product.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Finance objecting at design review — I showed the same number arriving from the physical side first and gave them the aging report to own. The grain problem — aggregates vs lines — was solved by the Aggregation# ↔ Aptos header ↔ lines join and exact pro-rata COGS allocation, with the last line absorbing rounding so the sum ties to the cent.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the SNB bucket definition, the join spec, the de-aggregation rule, the failure-direction statement, the control-owner map and the test families. Theirs: S/4 and OMS engineers implemented; Inventory Accounting confirmed in UAT and took over the aging report.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Sequenced by event source — POS flash sales, then omni pending, then ship-to-home confirmations — each added to SIT only when its increments and matched decrements reconciled. Buy-in: FSC Inventory Accounting signed inside the FSD on 6/5/25, and UAT was confirmation because QA had demoed each case during build.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

I refused real-time per-line posting to S/4 — throughput and posting cadence are why S/4 posts aggregates; ISM is the real-time ledger and S/4 stays settlement authority. And I refused a Finance-derived SNB: that is two numbers, and the whole point was one.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: FOH truths 2 → 1; AFS decremented at sale/ship; over-sell from unmatched postings = 0 by construction; zero incidents. $ impact: ≈$0.6M/yr modeled.

  • $ — ≈$0.6M/yr modeled (phantom-availability cancels avoided + Finance reconciliation labor + month-end close time); the record has no pre-state drift dollar and I am not claiming one.
  • # — Financial On-Hand truths 2 → 1 (record, FSD signed 6/5/25); over-sell from unmatched postings = 0 by construction.
  • % — Design KPI: match rate ≥ 99.5% within 24h; the modeled phantom-cancel rate is ~0.10 pt of orders → ~0 (modeled, labeled).
  • Time — AFS decrement point moved from the S/4 posting to the sale/ship event (record); rode the May execution go-live; aging report empty or explained at T+1 (record exit bar).
  • Customer — The failure direction protects the customer: an unmatched posting under-states availability — under-sell, never over-sell (record mechanism).
  • Quality — Σ per-line COGS = posting COGS to the cent in every cell; SNB never negative; month-end FOH ties to MB5L through the ISM number, not a bridge spreadsheet (record mechanism; days-to-tie not in record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

FOH truths 2 → 1 is the headline because reconciliation-by-argument was the disease; the decrement point moving to sale/ship and over-sell at zero by construction are what the customer and the book feel. Also important: the aging report owned by Finance without me, and month-end tying to MB5L without a bridge.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY figure exists — the ≈$0.6M/yr is modeled (cancels avoided, reconciliation labor, close time) against the pre-state, and the record has no pre-state drift dollar; I am not claiming one. The record movement is two on-hand truths becoming one.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

Decrementing only on a matched posting moves cost to Replenishment deliberately: an unmatched posting under-states AFS and shows up as an aged row Finance clears the same day — small and visible, priced that way on purpose. The price of one book is a matching cycle to maintain.

Probing — I'm concerned about the cancel rate — it looks borrowed from S1 — tell me more.

Different window: S1's rate is the vocabulary problem across node types; this is the posting-lag window inside one node. If the room wants one number, I keep S1's and drop this row — the mechanism still stands on the record.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Design the failure direction before the happy path. Differently: Internal Audit in discovery, not review — the day Inventory Accounting asked me why a posting had aged before I noticed it, the control had an owner.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Every fulfillment event moves units before Finance posts them — the same window at a vastly larger grain. The transferable design: one real-time ledger, the financial system as settlement authority, a safe failure direction chosen before the happy path, and a control report with a named owner instead of a bridge spreadsheet.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The signed criterion was one Financial On-Hand with AFS decremented at sale, not posting, and a safe failure direction — delivered: FOH 2 → 1, over-sell from unmatched postings zero by construction, aging report empty or explained at T+1, zero incidents.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (14). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When Finance and Operations agreed on the description of the gap — units gone, book not decremented, between sale and posting — and on the exact events that move units before posting. Agreement on the description came before any design.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote "one Financial On-Hand for Finance and Operations; AFS decremented at sale, not posting; safe failure direction." FSC Inventory Accounting signed it inside the FSD on 6/5/25 with Strategy and Inventory Management.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at sign-off?

A double-subtraction path, unmatched postings accumulating silently, and late or duplicate POS lines. Mitigations: designed out by construction, an aging report Finance owns, idempotency keys with a late-arrival flag.

How did you handle Finance objecting at the design review?

They wanted their own reconciliation. I showed the same number arriving from the physical side first and gave them the report to own. A parallel spreadsheet dies when its owner owns the real report.

Why decrement only on a matched posting?

An unmatched posting keeps SNB high, so AFS under-states and Replenishment over-orders visibly. Decrementing on an estimate would hide the gap as oversell — the failure direction was designed before the happy path.

Build & Test — How did you sequence the stories?

By event source: POS flash sales, then omni pending, then ship-to-home confirmations — each added to SIT only when its increments and matched decrements reconciled. The de-aggregation family ran on every source.

What was your definition of done?

Matrix cell green, the "SNB never negative" assertion, and Σ per-line COGS equal to the posting COGS to the cent. A story without the failure-direction assertion was not done.

Who could fail a build in UAT?

Inventory Accounting. Their entrance criterion was "every SIT case ties to the cent"; they confirmed rather than discovered because QA had demoed each case during build.

Cutover — What was the cutover risk specific to SNB?

The opening balance — sales made but not yet posted at the snapshot. I reconciled SNB's opening balance to unposted sales before the publish switch; the T+1 aging report was the go/no-go read for the matching cycle.

What were your rollback triggers?

Aging report growing without explanation, or any negative SNB at T+1 — rollback was cheap because S/4 stayed settlement authority; ISM republishes SNB from the event log. Neither fired.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over?

When Finance ran the aging and Negative AFS reports without me and month-end FOH tied to MB5L through the ISM number, not a bridge spreadsheet. I don't have a canonical days-to-tie figure and won't invent one.

What did the first weeks teach you?

That the aging report is the real product — the day Inventory Accounting asked me why a posting had aged before I noticed it, the control had an owner. Do-differently: Internal Audit in discovery, not review.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date?

Real-time per-line posting to S/4 — never in scope, and I held that line. The price was a matching cycle to maintain; the benefit was one book.

Why not post every line to S/4? · Why not let Finance derive SNB themselves?

Throughput and posting cadence — S/4 posts aggregates for a reason; ISM is the real-time ledger, S/4 stays the settlement authority. And a Finance-derived SNB is two numbers; the whole point was one.

Where this lands at Amazon: every fulfillment event moves units before Finance posts them — the same window at a vastly larger grain. The transferable design: one real-time ledger, the financial system as settlement authority, a safe failure direction chosen before the happy path, and a control report with a named owner instead of a bridge spreadsheet.

The math — full derivation, step by step

Shipped-Not-Billed ledger. For SKU i at node n: SNB_in(t) = Σ sold-or-shipped-not-posted units −
  Σ matched-posting units . Increment on POS flash sale, OMS omni pending sale, WMS ship
  confirm; decrement only on a matched S/4 aggregate posting. De-aggregation (pro-rata COGS).
  Aggregate posting P with total qty q_P and total COGS C_P ; line i with q_i : COGS_i = C_P ×
  q_i / q_P , with Σ_i q_i = q_P and Σ_i COGS_i = C_P (allocation is exact; last line absorbs
  rounding so the sum ties to the cent).
Phantom-availability window. W_i = t_post − t_sale ; exposure per SKU ≈ sales rate × W units.
  The design makes the exposure zero by decrementing AFS at t_sale via SNB instead of at t_post
  .
Safe failure direction. If a posting never matches: SNB stays high  AFS = Basis − … − SNB is
  lower than truth  under-sell (visible over-ordering by Replenishment), never over-sell.
  Formally: AFS_reported ≤ AFS_true for all unmatched states.
Worked example (illustrative — no canonical drift $ exists; "illustrative, not program
    data").
  Aggregate posting P: 5 lines, q_P = 12 , C_P = $300.00 . Lines q = 3, 2, 4, 1, 2 → COGS =
  75.00, 50.00, 100.00, 25.00, 50.00 (Σ = 300.00 ). SNB before match = 12 (from POS events);
  after match SNB = 0. Suppose one line (q = 2) arrives late: SNB = 2 after matching 10; AFS
  under-states by 2 units until the line lands; the unmatched-aging report shows it at 24h.
  Sanity: SNB ≥ 0 always; a decrement beyond the matched quantity is rejected.
Reconciliation KPI. match_rate = matched postings / postings received per day (target ≥ 99.5%
  within 24h); SNB_aging_p95 in hours; FOH_ISM − FOH_S4 = 0 at month-end per SKU × node.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Conservatively, about six hundred thousand a year: roughly four hundred fifty thousand of cancels that no longer happen because availability drops at the sale instead of at the posting; about a hundred forty thousand of Finance hours that were going into a bridge spreadsheet; and about fifty thousand a year of month-end close time. What is measured is that Finance and Operations went from two on-hand numbers to one, decremented at sale or ship, with zero incidents and the failure direction pointing at under-sell. Every dollar is my model on an illustrative thirty-million-order scale; the record has no pre-state drift dollar and I am not claiming one."

If challenged — the full answers.

The cancel number is three quarters of the total and you say the record has no drift figure.

Right, so it is the hardest number. It rests on a tenth of a point of orders being cancelled for phantom availability before the fix. What I would measure is the cancel-reason code for out-of-stock-after-order, per week, before and after the May go-live, times Digital's own cost per cancel; whatever that shows replaces my assumption.

Finance hours are soft, nobody logs spreadsheet time.

Agreed. Four people at eight hours a week is what Inventory Accounting described, not what a timesheet says. I would firm it up by counting the manual journal entries and bridge lines per month before and after; that is in the posting log and it is not opinion.

Isn't the safe failure direction just moving cost to Replenishment?

Yes, and I priced it that way on purpose: an unmatched posting under-states AFS and shows up as an aged row Finance clears the same day, so the over-order is small and visible. The measure is the aging report's p95 in hours and the match rate inside twenty-four hours; if match rate holds above ninety-nine and a half, the Replenishment side rounds to zero.

S4 — Sephora S/4HANA: the in-transit promise rule I got wrong — fixed through the signed policy, not a side configuration

Amazon LPs: Are Right, A Lot (the failure question — third) · Customer Obsession (backup) · Earn Trust (owning the miss) · Learn and Be Curious. Crosswalk: not in the loop packet — safe anywhere. The designated policy-failure story. Recency: current role.

Honesty label. The v1 rule was mine; the indictment came from my own program's reports. Lane σ ≈ 0.3 day and the ~0.49-day window are the record's promise math; the record carries no lane counts and no dollar for the conversion lost while I was wrong — anything of that kind is labeled illustrative, and I will not invent the loss figure.
60-second pitch

"Situation. My first ISM ruleset barred every channel from promising units in transit; Aging In-Transit showed dotcom starved of fast movers one day from the building. Task. Correct my own policy without weakening oversell protection, through the same three signatories. Action. Discovery: conversion-loss evidence from the aging and replenishment reports — that was the entry bar to reopen the policy. Plan & Design: promise math — lane σ ≈ 0.3 day, ~0.49-day window ≈ 95% coverage; Promisable = in-transit × 1[ETA ≤ window] × 1[on-time ≥ floor]; Aging In-Transit as kill switch. Build & Test: back-test against receiving history; regression through the node × channel matrix. Cutover: released on the normal monthly cadence as a signed FSD change, not a side config. Hypercare: kill switch armed; never fired. Lesson: both sides of a rule get a number before it ships. Result. Primary metric: sellable units on qualifying in-transit lanes — starved → restored inside a per-lane promise window, oversell increase = zero, kill switch never fired. $ impact: ≈$0.5M/yr modeled margin recovered — the record has no dollar for the conversion lost while I was wrong, and I will not invent one."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — In the first ruleset I authored for the ISM availability model, no channel could promise against units in transit. It felt safe. The Aging In-Transit and Replenishment reports showed dotcom starved of fast-moving units reliably one day from the building, with conversion loss on those SKUs.

T — The policy was mine and I had written it conservatively; the task was to correct it without weakening oversell protection, and to take the miss back through the same signatories.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

My first ISM ruleset barred every channel from promising units in transit, and the Aging In-Transit and Replenishment reports showed dotcom starved of fast movers reliably one day from the building, with conversion loss on those SKUs. The goal: correct my own policy without weakening oversell protection, through the same three signatories.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

Scope was one rule in the availability model I had authored — whether and when in-transit units are promisable. The challenges: the policy was mine, it was signed, and both blanket answers were wrong — “always counts” oversells unreliable lanes, “never counts” strands good units.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Left standing, the rule kept starving dotcom of fast movers one day out, with conversion loss; fixed carelessly, the risks were oversell on an unreliable lane, aging shipments getting promised, and on-hold receipts being counted — each given an automatic mitigation and a named owner.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Are Right, A Lot?

Because the LP is about being right through data, not never being wrong — my own program's reports indicted my rule, and I corrected it with promise math (lane σ ≈ 0.3 day, a ~0.49-day window ≈ 95% coverage) through the same signatures. It is my designated policy-failure story.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Are Right, A Lot?

S10 is the primary — “the noise said carriers; the data said measurement” — and S8, measured lanes vs brochure, is the backup.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is current-role Sephora — the fix released on the ISM program's normal monthly cadence — so it is a recent record, and the lesson it produced set the 98% robotics gate (S2) and the 0.5% tolerance (S18).

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery — built the evidence pack from the Aging In-Transit and Replenishment reports (fast movers reliably one day out, conversion loss), stated that the v1 rule was mine, reframed the problem as promise math rather than "make in-transit count." Exit bar: Dotcom, Inventory Planning and I agree the undersell is real and is the rule's doing.
  2. Plan & Design — wrote the rule — Promisable = In-transit × 1[ETA ≤ window(lane)] × 1[on-time ≥ floor], window per lane from receiving history, Aging In-Transit as kill switch — rejected both blanket options in writing, carried it as an FSD change. Exit bar: signed by the same three signatories as v1.
  3. Build & Test — owned the back-test of the ruleset against receiving history, four regression cells added to the matrix (qualifying lane / non-qualifying lane / aging breach / on-hold receipt), the release note. Exit bar: back-test shows no oversell on qualifying lanes; matrix green; QA gate passed.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — released on the normal cadence with the lane qualification list published and monitor thresholds set — no hotfix, no side configuration. Exit bar: sellable units restored on qualifying lanes; zero oversell traced to the window.
  5. Hypercare / Close — kill-switch watch (aging per lane), oversell watch, close-out to the signatories with the miss named as mine plus the mechanism note. Exit bar: kill switch never fired; lesson institutionalized — the Locus 98% gate (S2) and the 0.5% tolerance (S18) were both set from data before the event.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

Promisable = In-transit × 1[ETA ≤ window(lane)] × 1[on-time ≥ floor], with the window from lane receiving history — w = z × σ = 1.645 × 0.3 ≈ 0.49 day for ~95% coverage — and Aging In-Transit as the kill switch. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

The v1 rule was mine and so was the fix — I built the evidence pack, wrote the corrected rule, owned the back-test against receiving history, and carried it as an FSD change through the same three signatories. The indictment came from my own program's reports.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Naming the miss as mine to the signatories before proposing a fix — owning it first is what made re-signing possible without a fight — and reframing the problem as promise math rather than “make in-transit count.”

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

“Don't touch a signed policy” — answered with the undersell number and the promise math, then changed through the same signatures rather than a side configuration. The design risks — oversell on an unreliable lane, aging shipments, on-hold receipts — got the on-time floor, the kill switch and the reserve logic.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

The rule, the evidence pack, the back-test and the four regression cells were mine; Dotcom and Inventory Planning agreed the undersell was real and the rule's doing; the signatories re-signed; Inventory Control owns the standing kill-switch review weekly.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Lane by lane, not all-or-nothing — a lane whose on-time rate sat below the floor stayed unpromisable, and Dotcom accepted because the alternative was all-or-nothing. Buy-in came from the evidence pack and from rejecting both blanket options in writing.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

I challenged my own signed policy — and refused to ship the fix as a hotfix or side configuration: the error was in the policy, so the fix had to be too, in the governed artifact with regression.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: starved lanes → restored inside the window; oversell increase = 0; kill switch never fired. $ impact: ≈$0.5M/yr modeled.

  • $ — ≈$0.5M/yr modeled margin recovered (inputs illustrative: 300 starved units/day × 60% conversion × 40% lost-sale share × $20 margin); the record carries no dollar for the conversion lost while the v1 rule stood, and I will not invent one.

  • Time — Released on the normal monthly cadence as a signed FSD change — no hotfix, no side configuration (record).
  • Customer — Starved fast movers one day from the building → sellable inside the window; oversell increase = zero (record).
