Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Starved lanes restored with oversell increase = 0 is the pair that matters — the fix had to recover sales without weakening protection, and the kill switch never firing is the proof. The institutional result matters as much: both sides of every rule now get a number before it ships.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY — the ≈$0.5M/yr is a modeled recovery run-rate on illustrative volumes, and the record carries no dollar for what my first rule cost while it stood, so I do not claim one. The record movement is starved → restored with oversell at zero.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

The window accepts a priced, per-lane oversell exposure — ~95% coverage, the on-time floor, the kill switch — in exchange for restoring sellable units; lanes below the floor stayed unpromisable. Speed was traded for governance: the normal release train, not a hotfix.

Probing — I'm concerned about the 300 starved units a day — tell me more.

Correct — it is my hardest input; it assumes the Aging In-Transit report's pattern held across the qualifying lanes every day. To firm it up I would pull the daily count of units within the window on qualifying lanes for the first quarter after the release; that count is in the report, and it replaces my three hundred.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Both sides of a rule get a number before it ships — the 98% robotics gate (S2) and the 0.5% tolerance (S18) were both set from data before the event because of this. Differently: back-test against history before publishing v1 — one week of measurement would have skipped the starvation.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Inventory in transit between FCs, or inbound from a vendor, is exactly this promising decision at network scale — and this is the failure answer every Bar Raiser wants: a real policy error, named as mine to the same signatories, fixed with math, institutionalized.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The task was to correct the policy without weakening oversell protection — delivered: sellable units restored inside a back-tested half-day window, oversell increase zero, kill switch never fired, signed by the same three signatories as v1.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know this was a policy defect and not a data defect?

The reports showed fast movers reliably one day out with conversion loss — the lanes were on time; the rule forbade promising against them. Data was fine; my rule was the cause.

Who did you tell first?

The three signatories of the v1 rule, with my name on the miss, before proposing a fix. Owning it first is what made re-signing possible without a fight.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register for the fix?

Oversell on an unreliable lane, aging shipments getting promised, on-hold receipts being counted. Each had an automatic mitigation — floor exclusion, kill switch, reserve logic — and a named owner.

How did you handle "don't touch a signed policy"?

By showing the undersell number and the promise math: σ about 0.3 day, a ~0.49-day window buys ~95% coverage. Then I changed the policy through the same signatures rather than a side configuration.

Why reject both blanket answers?

"Always counts" oversells unreliable lanes; "never counts" strands good units. Both sides of a rule get a number now — that was the lesson.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done?

Back-test against receiving history attached, regression cells green for all four cases, QA's gate passed. No back-test, no QA entry.

How did you test it without a bug bash?

A rule change doesn't need a room; it needs history. I back-tested against receiving history first — the step I had skipped in v1 — then ran the matrix.

Cutover — Why the normal release and not a hotfix?

A policy fix belongs in the governed artifact with regression. A hotfix would have made the fix as untraceable as the error.

What were your rollback triggers?

Any oversell traced to the window, or aging above threshold on a qualified lane — rollback is per lane: window to zero. Neither fired.

Hypercare — How did you decide it was closed?

One release cycle with the kill switch silent and no oversell increase, then a one-page close-out to the signatories. The lesson outlived the fix: the 98% robotics gate and the 0.5% tolerance were both set from data before the event.

What stayed on the RAID log after close?

One standing row: the kill-switch threshold per lane, owned by Inventory Control, reviewed on the normal cadence. A rule that qualifies lanes automatically needs one human who reads the aging report every week.

Cross-phase — Which wave did you hold? What did you refuse?

Lanes, not waves — a lane whose on-time rate sat below the floor stayed unpromisable; Dotcom accepted lane-by-lane because the alternative was all-or-nothing. Refused: shipping it as a side configuration. The error was in the policy; the fix had to be too.

Why didn't you catch it in design? · What changed in how you work?

I optimized for the defect I feared (oversell) and never priced the one I couldn't see (undersell). Now both sides of every rule get a number, and I test a ruleset against history before publishing — one week of measurement would have skipped the starvation.

Where this lands at Amazon: inventory in transit between FCs, or inbound from a vendor, is exactly this promising decision at network scale. And the failure answer every Bar Raiser wants: a real policy error, named as mine to the same signatories, fixed with math, institutionalized as "both sides of a rule get a number."

The math — full derivation, step by step

Promise rule. Promisable_in = InTransit_in × 1[ETA_lane ≤ promise_window_lane] × 1[OTR_lane ≥
  floor] , where OTR_lane = on-time rate over the trailing receiving history and floor is the
  per-lane admission threshold. Window from lane variability (MIT toolkit label: normal
  approximation on lane transit). For lane transit time L ~ (μ_L, σ_L) , a window that covers a
  fraction α of arrivals beyond the ETA is w = z_α × σ_L . Canonical: measured lane σ ≈ 0.3 day;
  z_0.95 = 1.645 → w = 1.645 × 0.3 ≈ 0.49 day  ~95% coverage on qualifying lanes. Check: P(L ≤
  ETA + 0.49) = Φ(0.49/0.3) = Φ(1.63) ≈ 0.95 . Units: days. What "unpromisable" claims.
    Setting
  Promisable = 0 for all in-transit is the limit σ_L → ∞ (or OTR → 0 ) — a claim of infinite
  variability. Measured σ contradicts it. Both errors priced (the lesson). Under-sell cost per
  lane-day ≈ starved units × conversion loss × margin ; over-sell cost ≈ late units × P(late) ×
  (cancel/expedite cost) . Illustrative ("not program data"): a SKU with 100 units one
    day out,
  60% would convert at $20 margin → $1,200/day of under-sell; with OTR 97% the over-sell
  exposure is 100 × 0.03 × $15 expedite = $45/day. The asymmetry is what the "safe" rule
  ignored.
Kill switch. Aging_lane = units in transit longer than k × p90_lane ; lane drops out of
  promisability if Aging_lane > threshold (e.g., any unit > 2 × p90 or OTR falls below
    floor for
  the trailing window). Sample-size sanity for OTR (toolkit). To estimate OTR ≈ 0.95 within ±5%
  at 95% CI: n = z² p(1−p)/e² = 3.84 × 0.0475 / 0.0025 ≈ 73 receipts per lane before the lane
  qualifies — illustrative.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Conservatively, about half a million a year of margin recovered: roughly three hundred fast-mover units a day that were sitting one day from the building and unsellable, of which about sixty percent would have converted and about forty percent of those were truly lost rather than substituted, at about twenty dollars of margin each. If challenged — the full answers.

Three hundred units a day is your number, not the report's.

Correct, it is the hardest input. It assumes the Aging In-Transit report's pattern held across the qualifying lanes every day. To firm it up I would pull the daily count of units within the window on qualifying lanes for the first quarter after the release; that count is in the report, and it replaces my three hundred.

How do you know those sales were lost and not delayed a day?

I do not, which is why I only take forty percent of them, the middle of the published range for a stockout turning into a lost sale. The measure is conversion on the affected SKUs the week before and the week after the release, from Digital's own funnel; if it moved less than that, the number shrinks with it.

You net oversell at zero, is that convenient?

It is the record: zero oversell incidents traced to the window and a kill switch that never fired. But I would keep measuring it, because a lane's on-time rate can decay. The standing check is the aging report per lane against two times its ninetieth percentile, owned by Inventory Control weekly.

S5 — Sephora: the architecture council without owning any system — three decisions and a fence

Amazon LPs: Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit (third) · Earn Trust (influence without authority) · Think Big (architecture governance). Crosswalk: not in the loop packet — safe anywhere. Recency: current role. Same program family as S1/S2 — the governance story; don't pair with either for one interviewer.

Honesty label. I owned none of the systems; the design decision was the deliverable. Zero escalations and the dated gate chain are the record; the dollars are my model of what governance avoided.
60-second pitch

"Situation. The UDC location and inventory model had to clear architecture council — a challenge gate — with ~80 named participants across ~15 functions, and I owned none of those systems. Task. Take the design from discovery through council to sign-off and FSD without a surprise escalation. Action. Hypercare: fence held in writing for the whole program; a second live warehouse became a monitored shadow copy, not an argument. Lesson: a no that lives in a document survives. Result. Primary metric: surprise escalations on a 60+ stakeholder design — the norm on prior programs → zero; the dated gate chain held. $ impact: ≈$0.5M one-time modeled (avoided rework on ~3 escalations that never happened + six weeks of benefit not deferred + a fenced second reserve engine not built)."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The UDC location and inventory model had to clear the architecture council — a challenge gate, not a formality — with a dozen core systems (20+ touched) and ~80 named participants across ~15 functions on the impact table, and I owned none of those systems.

T — Take the design from discovery (kickoff 3/29, sessions 4/30) through the council (5/15) to sign-off (7/31) and FSD (8/14) without a surprise escalation.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

The UDC location and inventory model had to clear the architecture council — a challenge gate, not a formality — with ~80 named participants across ~15 functions and a dozen core systems (20+ touched), and I owned none of those systems. The goal: discovery through council to sign-off and FSD without a surprise escalation.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

Scope: take the design from discovery (kickoff 3/29, sessions closed 4/30) through the council (5/15) to sign-off (7/31) and FSD (8/14). The challenges: no authority over any impacted system, ~80 stakeholders, and the prior norm of designs this size being re-litigated.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

The register's top three: surprise LOE in the room, a second live warehouse becoming a second truth, and scope re-litigated after sign-off — each with a mitigation: the private pre-wire with the 7/2 LOE deadline, the monitored shadow copy, and the fence.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit?

Because the refusals were written and held — reserve math untouched, MCS decommission out of scope — and the council was asked for three separable decisions rather than a blessing. Saying no in a signed document and holding it for the program's life is the backbone; committing to the council's decisions is the commit.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit?

S9 — 597 vs 1,000, taken two levels up — is the primary; S14, refusing the quiet fix, is the backup.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Current role — the 2025 gate chain (council 5/15, sign-off 7/31, FSD 8/14) — so this is among the most recent records I have for this principle.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases — here the phases are governance milestones; the cutover is the council decision itself:

  1. Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — profiled both books (SKU overlap, order profiles); sessions per function with the profile on the table — "design" deliberately not argued yet; systems-impacted table with a provisional owner on every row; stakeholder map (~80 names / ~15 functions). Exit bar: every impacted team in a session, every row owned.
  2. Plan & Design → council (5/15) — council pack as three separable decisions (three planning identities over one location; January planning / May execution timeline; peak-freeze boundary) plus the out-of-scope fence (reserve math untouched; MCS decommission belongs to Global ERP); every owner pre-briefed privately. Exit bar: council challenge held 5/15; decisions taken or dated; no surprise objection.
  3. Build & Test → LOE (7/2) — one LOE template to one deadline across every impacted owner; each open concern converted into design or a dated RAID item (the second-live-warehouse concern → a monitored shadow copy). Exit bar: LOE signed 7/2 by every impacted owner; no owner learns their cost after this date.
  4. Cutover → sign-off (7/31), FSD (8/14) — assembled the sign-off pack (decisions, fence, LOE, risks); published FSD 8/14 with discovery results attached. Exit bar: signed 7/31; FSD 8/14; fence lines in writing.
  5. Hypercare / Close — enforced the fence (scope requests answered from the pack, in writing); applied the peak-freeze boundary Oct–Dec; handed the program into the build (S2). Exit bar: zero surprise escalations for the program's life; both scope lines held in writing.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

The mechanism is decision decomposition — three separable decisions give partial-approval outcomes a bundled “approve the design” cannot — plus 100% pre-wire coverage (every impacted row with a signed LOE before the council) and the shadow-copy design that turned the two-truths risk into a detectable Sev. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned none of the systems; the design decision was the deliverable. The profiling, the council pack, the three-decision structure, the fence and the LOE campaign were mine; system owners signed their own rows; the council took the decisions.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

The pre-wire: one LOE template, one deadline, one private conversation per owner before the council, so nobody learned their cost in the room. The result — zero surprise escalations on a 60+ stakeholder design against a norm everyone in that building recognized.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Getting sixty-plus people to sign LOE I didn't control — solved by the template, the deadline and the private conversations; signatures landed 7/2. The “second truth” objection — converted into the design as a monitored shadow copy, because converting a legitimate concern costs less than winning it.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the profiling of both books, the systems-impacted table, the council pack, the decision structure, the fence and the written scope answers afterwards. Theirs: each system owner produced and signed their own LOE; the council took the three decisions.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Profiling first — their numbers, not my opinion — with design deliberately not argued in the sessions. Buy-in was manufactured before the room: every row owned, every owner pre-briefed privately, and the council asked for three separable decisions it could take one at a time.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

I challenged the “approve the design” bundle itself — separability was the design — and refused retuning the reserve math and folding in the MCS decommission. Both refusals written, signed, held; later scope requests answered from the pack in writing.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: surprise escalations → 0; gate chain 5/15 → 7/31 → 8/14 held with zero re-litigation. $ impact: ≈$0.5M one-time modeled.

  • $ — ≈$0.5M one-time modeled — avoided rework on ~3 escalations that never happened (benchmark 2–4), six weeks of benefit not deferred, and a fenced second reserve engine not built; the dollars are my model of what did not happen.
  • # — ~80 named participants across ~15 functions; a dozen core systems (20+ touched); three separable decisions plus the out-of-scope fence (record).
  • % — Pre-wire coverage 100% — every impacted row with a signed LOE by 7/2, before the council sign-off (record).
  • Time — Gate chain kickoff 3/29 → sessions 4/30 → council 5/15 → LOE 7/2 → sign-off 7/31 → FSD 8/14 — held to the day, council-to-sign-off with zero re-litigation (record).
  • Quality — Surprise escalations → 0 for the program's life; fence breaches in the log: 0; both scope lines held in writing (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Zero surprise escalations and the dated chain holding — 5/15 → 7/31 → 8/14 with zero re-litigation — are the record for a governance program whose deliverable was the decision itself. Also important: fence breaches at zero and the program handed clean into the build (S2).

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY — the ≈$0.5M is one-time and entirely modeled: avoided rework on escalations that never happened, six weeks of benefit not deferred, and a fenced engine not built. I hold those loosely and say so.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

The fence itself was the trade: reserve-math retuning and the MCS decommission were refused to keep the release to one defect class; the second-live-warehouse concern cost a monitored shadow copy instead of an argument.

Probing — I'm concerned that you're pricing escalations that never happened — tell me more.

Correct, and labeled: the ~3 is a benchmark for designs this size and the rework hours are illustrative. The record is zero escalations against a norm everyone in that building recognized, and a gate chain that held to the day — if the room won't price prevention, keep the record and drop the dollars; the mechanism is the point.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

A no that lives in a document survives; a no that lives in a meeting gets re-litigated weekly. The fence held because it was signed — and answered from, in writing, every time.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Influence without authority is the L6 job description — pre-wire every owner's cost before the review, ask the forum for separable decisions rather than a blessing, fence what you will not do, and answer scope creep from the signed document.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The task was discovery through council to sign-off and FSD without a surprise escalation — delivered exactly: zero escalations for the program's life, both scope lines held in writing, and the chain held to the day.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done? Why make it a data exercise?

Every impacted team had been in a session on its own profiled data; every row had a provisional owner; sessions closed 4/30. Profiling is disarming — it is their numbers, not my opinion; SKU overlap and order profiles settled arguments before they started.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at the council?

Surprise LOE in the room, a second live warehouse becoming a second truth, and scope re-litigated after sign-off. Mitigations: private pre-wire with a 7/2 LOE deadline, the monitored shadow copy, the fence.

How did you handle a partner who objected at the review?

The "second truth" objection became the design. When a concern is legitimate, converting it costs less than winning it.

Why exactly three decisions?

Separability. Architecture, staged timeline, freeze boundary — a no on any one leaves the others standing. A single "approve the design" would have been all-or-nothing.

Build (LOE) — How did you get sixty-plus people to sign LOE you didn't control?

One template, one deadline, one private conversation per owner before the council so nobody learned their cost in the room. Signatures landed 7/2.

What was your definition of done for a row?

Owner named, LOE signed, concern converted to design or dated. A row with an open concern did not go into the sign-off pack.

Cutover — What would have made you delay the 7/31 sign-off?

An open row. There were none — which is what the pre-wire buys.

What was your rollback if the council said no?

Separability. A no on the timeline would have sent us back with the architecture intact; nothing had been configured yet by design — policy before configuration.

Hypercare — How did you keep scope from creeping back?

The fence lived in the signed pack; every later request was answered from it in writing. A no in a document survives; a no in a meeting gets re-litigated weekly.

What did "hypercare" mean for a governance program?

Answering every scope request from the signed pack, in writing, for the program's life — and applying the peak-freeze boundary Oct–Dec as the ruling. Zero surprise escalations was the exit metric, and it held.

Cross-phase — What did you escalate? What did you refuse?

Escalated nothing — the pre-wire is the escalation-prevention mechanism; the freeze boundary went to the council as a decision. Refused: retuning reserve math and folding in the MCS decommission. Both refusals written, signed, held.

What if the council had said no?

The three decisions were separable — a no on the timeline would not have unwound the architecture. That's why I asked for three, not one.

Where this lands at Amazon: influence without authority is the L6 job description. The mechanism transfers whole — pre-wire every owner's cost before the review, ask the forum for separable decisions rather than a blessing, fence what you will not do, and answer scope creep from the signed document.

The math — full derivation, step by step

Decision decomposition. A council "review" is one binary outcome on a bundle; three
    separable
  decisions D = {architecture, timeline, peak boundary} give 2³ = 8 outcomes, of which any
  partial approval preserves progress. Expected recoverable progress: with independent approval
  probabilities p_a, p_t, p_b , E[decisions approved] = p_a + p_t + p_b vs a bundle's p_a ×
    p_t
  × p_b . Illustrative: 0.9 each → 2.7 of 3 expected vs 0.73 chance of the whole bundle.
Pre-wiring coverage. coverage = rows with signed LOE before council / total impacted rows ;
  target 100% (LOE signed 7/2 ahead of 7/31 sign-off). Surprise escalations = rows whose owner
  first learned cost in the room ; result 0.
Gate chain. Kickoff 3/29 → business sessions 4/30 → council challenge 5/15 → LOE 7/2 → sign-off
  7/31 → FSD 8/14: 138 days discovery-to-spec; council-to-sign-off 77 days with zero
  re-litigation.
Impact table size. ~80 named participants across ~15 functions, a dozen core systems (20+
  touched)  impacted-row grain = system × process step; LOE per row in person-days, summed per
  system owner for the council pack. Two-truths risk (design, not argument). During the interim,
  two live books create disagreement probability per SKU per day; the shadow-900 copy set with
  |AFS_900 − AFS_801| = 0 monitor makes disagreement a detectable Sev rather than a latent truth
  conflict.

   v                         v                        v

 | Discovery profiling (me): A n B, A - B, B - A  -> SKU overlap report      |
 | Systems-impacted table: system x process step x change x owner x LOE      |
 +---------------------------------------------------------------------------+
   ^  LOE (person-days), risks, signed 7/2      |  council pack, one read
   |  per row -- before the council             v  (<= 30 min)
 [System owners: WMS, OMS, ESB,            [Architecture council] -> decision
  Finance/SAP, Planning IT, Locus, Proship]  register: D1 architecture,
                                       D2 staged timeline, D3 peak boundary
 [Out-of-scope fence] -- reserve math (Canada replica), MCS decommission
                         (Global ERP / S/4 program) --> written in the pack

                     AFS}, nightly, drift = 0 --> monitor spec -> S2 build
 SAP S/4HANA [BoR: finance master data] sits behind "Finance/SAP" as an owner
 row (cost center / company code inheritance) - no S/4 interface built here.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Conservatively, about half a million, one time, of cost this governance avoided: roughly three hundred thousand of rework on the two or three escalations that never happened because every owner had signed their effort before the council; about a hundred thousand of the comingle benefit arriving six weeks sooner than it would have after those re-litigations; and about a hundred thousand for the second reserve engine the fence kept out of the release. What is measured is zero surprise escalations and the dated chain holding, council to sign-off to spec. The dollars are my model of what did not happen, so I hold them loosely and I say so."

If challenged — the full answers.

You are pricing things that did not happen.

Yes, and that is the hardest part of any governance number. It rests on a norm of two to four re-litigations for a design with sixty-plus stakeholders and two weeks of core-team rework each. To firm it up I would take the prior two programs of this size at Sephora and count the scope re-openings and the weeks each cost from their own RAID logs; that replaces my norm with theirs.

A hundred thousand of schedule protection borrows S2's number.

It does, deliberately, and it is labelled that way. The assumption is six weeks of slip avoided against a benefit worth about seventeen thousand a week. The measure is simple: the FSD landed on the fourteenth of August against the plan, so slip avoided is the plan minus the actual, and the weekly value comes from the DC's measured efficiency once it is priced.

The fence saving is just work you chose not to do.

Right, so I count it once and small. The assumption is two engineers for twelve weeks. What would firm it up is the actual estimate the engineering team gave when they proposed the US-tuned engine; if their number was different, mine changes, but the point stands that a second engine was a second defect class in the same release.

S6 — Elementum: eight SAP data domains into one real-time supply/demand view — a shortage visible a lead-time early

Amazon LPs: Deliver Results (third) · Earn Trust (the two-signal release rule) · Dive Deep · Think Big (one view over eight domains). Crosswalk: loop packet S5 — consistent (70→85%, $2M, both-errors rule). Different program from S7 (the six-site ingest) and S8 (the $1.5M lanes) — never merge the dollars or the timelines.

Honesty label. I owned the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery; the client's SAP teams and our integration engineers built the feeds; planners owned the forecast — I owned its measurement and the release rule. I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it.
60-second pitch

"Situation. A six-site manufacturer learned about component shortages when the line stopped; eight SAP domains never met, so 'which orders are at risk' had no data path. Task. I took the definitions, the data-integrity program, and the delivery. Action. Discovery: profiled all eight domains; signed gap findings. Plan & Design: supply = on-hand + production plan + released planned orders only; grain SKU × location × date; shortage alert = days-of-cover vs lane lead-time p90. Build & Test: inventory and production first — numbers planners already believed; daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52. Cutover: shortage-alert product live in 3 months behind data-quality gates; nobody mandated off a spreadsheet. Hypercare: safety-stock release only where forecast error AND lead-time error both improved, signed with planning and Finance. Lesson: define supply honestly before any screen. Result. Primary metric: forecast accuracy on scoped class-A SKUs — 70% → 85%. $ impact: $2M of safety stock released under the both-errors-improved rule (record, one-time working capital), with ≈$0.8M/yr modeled recurring value around it — separate from the $1.5M lane program; never add them."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — A strategic manufacturing customer with six sites (mix of make-to-order and make-to-stock): planners learned about a component shortage when the line stopped; S&OP ran off private spreadsheets; shipment data was 60% complete; supplier, material, BOM, inventory, production, transportation, sales-order and planning data all lived in SAP and site systems but never met. Leadership's question — "which customer orders are at risk this quarter?" — had no data path.

T — Nobody owned the seam; there was no spec, no agreed grain, no definition of "supply," no data-quality bar. I chose all four, as owner of the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

A six-site manufacturer learned about component shortages when the line stopped; S&OP ran off private spreadsheets; supplier, material, BOM, inventory, production, transportation, sales-order and planning data all lived in SAP and site systems but never met — so leadership's question, “which customer orders are at risk this quarter?,” had no data path. The goal: a real-time supply/demand view with honest definitions and a shortage visible a lead-time early.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

Nobody owned the seam; there was no spec, no agreed grain, no definition of “supply,” no data-quality bar — I chose all four, as owner of the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery. Challenges: shipment data 60% complete, six sites mixing make-to-order and make-to-stock, and planner trust.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Line-down surprises continuing — shortages discovered when the line stopped — expedites instead of re-plans, buffers sized on noise, and S&OP steered from private spreadsheets.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Deliver Results?

Because the results landed behind gates and signatures: forecast accuracy 70 → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs, the shortage alert live in three months, and $2M of safety stock out under the both-errors-improved rule Planning and Finance signed. Delivering the number honestly — released planned orders only — is the point.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Deliver Results?

S11 — 7 → 68 behind graduation gates — is the primary Deliver Results story; S18 (8 months, both hubs, gate cleared) is the backup, and S2's comingle launch the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is from the Elementum years — older. The most recent same-LP record is S2's comingle launch in my current role (zero incidents, +15% signed); S11 at Flexport sits between.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (canonical plan: Discovery 4–6 wks → master-data foundation 4–6 wks parallel → domain waves 8–12 wks → views live 4 wks → scale & hypercare 4 wks; the 3-month figure is the shortage-alert product; the six-site ingest is S7's separate 5-month program):

  1. Discovery (4–6 wks) — profiled every SAP domain (completeness, latency, joinability) to my own gap templates; wrote the definitions memo (grain SKU × location × date; supply = on-hand + production plan + released planned orders only) and the metrics tree. When Planning asked to count unfirmed MRP orders as supply because it looked better, I refused: unfirmed orders breathe every run. Exit bar: signed gap findings per domain; scope frozen in writing; planners agree the supply definition before any screen.
  2. Plan & Design (parallel) — master-data foundation plan; MPN mapping worklist with its coverage gate; BOM extract and phantom clean-up plan; domain-wave sequence by trust; DQ scorecard spec; the 10-step onboarding playbook. Exit bar: master-data gates green; unmapped-MPN list under threshold with a named owner; wave order signed by domain owners.
  3. Build & Test (8–12 wks) — domain waves behind published DQ gates; shortage-alert rule specified (days-of-cover vs lane LT p90); daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52; weekly root-cause loop with false alarms worked as defects. Exit bar: per-domain completeness ≥ gate; reconciliation passing daily; false alarms trending down; pilot planners using the alert.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (4 wks) — views live for pilot planners with the at-risk list replacing the Monday spreadsheet; go/no-go read from the scorecards. Exit bar: planners answer "where is my product" from the platform in the live S&OP meeting — not from a spreadsheet.
  5. Hypercare / Close (4 wks) — exception workbench staffed; weekly root-cause reviews; the safety-stock release rule signed with Planning and Finance. Exit bar: blockers/day under threshold two consecutive weeks; release applied only where forecast AND lead-time error both improved.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

Supply defined as on-hand + production plan + released planned orders only (unfirmed MRP orders breathe every run), grain SKU × location × date, PAB = supply − demand with multi-level BOM explosion, and the alert at days-of-cover ≤ lane lead-time p90. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery; the client's SAP teams and our integration engineers built the feeds; planners owned the forecast — I owned its measurement and the release rule. I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

The honest definitions — refusing to count unfirmed MRP orders as supply because it looked better — and the two-signal release rule: buffer out only where forecast error and lead-time error both improved. That rule is why the release survived audit.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Planner trust, and the MPN mapping worklist that gated longer than planned. Trust was built by sequencing waves by trust — inventory and production first, numbers planners already believed — and working false alarms as defects; the MPN slip went to the sponsor as a re-sequenced date with the coverage number attached.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the definitions memo, the metrics tree, the DQ scorecard spec, the wave sequence, the release rule and the 10-step onboarding playbook. Theirs: SAP teams and integration engineers built the feeds; domain owners held sign-off authority over their own gates; planners owned the forecast.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Waves by trust, class-A SKUs before the tail, every feed behind a published DQ gate — below the gate, the feed got fixed, not automated. Buy-in: domain owners could fail their own wave — I designed it so the business could say no to its own data — and nobody was mandated off the spreadsheet.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

Planning asked to count unfirmed MRP orders as supply because it looked better; I refused — unfirmed orders change every run, amplify noise and inflate supply. And the safety-stock release was refused as a blanket act and agreed as a rule.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: forecast accuracy 70% → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs (record). $2M safety stock released under the both-errors rule (record, one-time); planner effort −50%; shortage alert live in 3 months; adoption 8×.

  • $ — $2M safety stock released (record, one-time working capital, under the both-errors-improved rule signed with Planning and Finance) + ≈$0.8M/yr modeled recurring (carry, expedites pre-empted, planner capacity) — never added to S8's $1.5M.
  • # — Eight SAP data domains into one view across six sites; shortage-alert product live in 3 months; adoption 8× (record).
  • % — Forecast accuracy 70% → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs (record); planner effort −50% (record); shipment data 60% complete at the start.
  • Time — Shortage alert fires at days-of-cover ≤ lane lead-time p90 — a lead-time early instead of at line-down (record mechanism); views live in 3 months.
  • Customer — “Which customer orders are at risk this quarter?” answered from the platform in the live S&OP meeting, not a spreadsheet (record exit bar).
  • Quality — Daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52; false alarms classified weekly to root cause and burned down as defects (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Forecast accuracy 70 → 85% is the record headline and the $2M release is the signed, audit-surviving consequence; planner effort −50% and the alert live in three months are what the planners felt. Adoption 8× mattered because trust was the real product.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY revenue figure — the 70 → 85% is forecast accuracy on scoped class-A SKUs against the pre-state, and the $2M is a one-time working-capital release, not an annual saving; the recurring value I model is ≈$0.8M/yr around it, labeled.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

The lane tail and the MPN tail were deferred behind the same gates — priced in coverage, not count. Rollback stayed free because the spreadsheet was never banned, and the release rule traded speed of release for a release that survived audit.

Probing — I'm concerned about the expedites-avoided figure — tell me more.

The softest row — about sixty expedites a year pre-empted at a five-thousand-dollar premium, both illustrative. To firm it I would pull the expedited-freight ledger for the six sites for the twelve months before and after the alert went live and count the premium directly.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Define supply honestly before any screen. Differently: start the MPN-mapping worklist on day one — it is never “done” and it gated more than I planned.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Supply/demand visibility built honestly — define supply before the screen, measure MAPE and bias at the decision grain, alert a lead-time early, and release buffer only when both error signals improve. The two-signal release rule is the part most rooms have never seen.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

Leadership's question was “which customer orders are at risk this quarter?” — answered a lead-time early from the platform in the live S&OP meeting, with accuracy 70 → 85%, the alert live in three months, and the $2M release under the signed rule.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (14). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

Every domain had a signed gap finding on its own profiled data and the metrics tree was agreed. Four to six weeks; scope frozen in writing at the exit.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote the metrics tree — OTIF, inventory dollars, MAPE/bias, lane lead-time accuracy, completeness, latency, adoption — and the domain owners with sign-off authority signed their gates.

What did you define before any screen?

Grain — SKU × location × date — and supply: on-hand plus production plan plus released planned orders only. Planners accepted the definition first; the view was built to it.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at sign-off?

MPN mapping never done, carrier EDI capability spread, planner trust, and SAP extract load — carried openly with a mitigation and owner each. MPN became a standing metric rather than a task.

How did you sequence the waves?

By trust: inventory and production first because planners already believed those numbers, then purchasing/ASN, then transportation with the best lanes first. Class-A SKUs before the tail.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a feed?

Its DQ scorecard — completeness, latency, accuracy vs source, joinability — above the published gate, and daily reconciliation passing. Below the gate, the feed got fixed, not automated.

How did you treat false alarms?

As defects: classified weekly to a root cause — mapping gap, master-data defect, carrier capability, pipeline bug — owned and burned down. Adoption followed planners watching their reported defects die.

Who could fail a wave?

The domain owner — they held sign-off authority over their gate. I designed it so the business could say no to its own data.

Cutover — What was the go-live gate for the views?

Planners answering "where is my product" from the platform in the live S&OP meeting rather than from a spreadsheet. The shortage alert was live in three months.

What was your rollback?

The spreadsheet — still there, never banned. Reversibility was free because I never mandated anyone off it.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over? What did it hand over?

Blockers/day under threshold two consecutive weeks, adoption targets met, the exception workbench staffed. Handover: the weekly root-cause review to domain owners, the onboarding playbook (profile → map → test file → parallel run → scorecard → live), and the MPN exception queue as a standing metric.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

The lane tail and the MPN tail — deferred behind the same gates, priced in coverage, not count. Escalated the MPN worklist gating longer than planned — to the sponsor as a re-sequenced date with the coverage number attached.

Who owned the forecast?

The planners. I owned the accuracy measurement (MAPE/bias per SKU family), input integrity and the release rule. I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it.

Why released planned orders only? · Do differently?

Unfirmed MRP orders change every run; planning against them amplifies noise and inflates supply. Differently: start the MPN-mapping worklist on day one — it is never "done" and gated more than I planned.

Where this lands at Amazon: this is supply/demand visibility built honestly — define supply before the screen, measure MAPE and bias at the decision grain, alert a lead-time early, and release buffer only when both error signals improve. The two-signal release rule is the part most rooms have never seen.

planner capacity = 12 planners x 40% recon share x -50% = 2.4 FTE x $120K = $0.3M/yr
  (capacity redeployed to root cause, not headcount removed)
total modeled recurring = ~$0.8M/yr; range $0.5-1.2M (WACC 8-12%, premium $3-8K)

If challenged. "The $2M — measured how?" — "It's the record: the release Finance signed, applied only on SKU families where forecast error and lead-time error had both demonstrably improved. That rule is why the release survived audit — capital came out on evidence, not on a better-looking average." "150 shortage events?" — "Illustrative volume — six sites at roughly 25 escalations a year. The record is the mechanism: the alert fires at days-of-cover ≤ lane p90, which is the last moment a re-plan is cheaper than a flight."

The math — full derivation, step by step

Supply and demand at grain SKU × location × date. supply(t) = on_hand + Σ in-transit arriving ≤
  t + Σ production finishing ≤ t + Σ PO lines landing ≤ t + Σ RELEASED planned orders finishing
  ≤ t (unfirmed MRP planned orders excluded). demand(t) = Σ sales-order schedule lines due ≤ t +
  Σ DC replenishment due ≤ t + forecast net of orders (outside the frozen horizon only) . PAB(t)
  = supply(t) − demand(t) ; a negative PAB is a dated shortage.
Multi-level BOM explosion. demand(c) = Σ_p demand(p) × qty_per(p,c) / (1 − scrap_pc) , recursed
  by low-level code; where-used is the inverse map c → {p} .
Shortage alert (rule, not report). DoC_c = PAB-positive horizon in days (days until PAB(c) <
    0);
  alert if DoC_c ≤ LT_p90(lane_c) — a lead-time early. Illustrative: component with on-hand
  1,200, consumption 100/day → DoC = 12 days; lane p90 = 14 days  alert now (fix window 12 − 14
  < 0: expedite or re-promise); with p90 = 9 days  no alert (3-day slack).
Forecast accuracy. MAPE = (1/n) Σ |A_t − F_t| / A_t over periods with A_t > 0 ; WMAPE = Σ|A−F|
    /
  ΣA ; bias = Σ(F − A) / ΣA . Canonical: accuracy (1 − MAPE) 70% → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs
  (error 30% → 15%, halved). Illustrative check: 4 weeks A = 100, 120, 80, 100; F = 130, 110,
  100, 90 → |e| = 30, 10, 20, 10 → MAPE = (0.30 + 0.083 + 0.25 + 0.10)/4 = 18.3%; WMAPE = 70/400
  = 17.5%; bias = (430 − 400)/400 = +7.5% (over-forecasting).
Release rule (safety stock). Release buffer for SKU i only if MAPE_i(after) < MAPE_i(before)
    and
  LTacc_i(after) > LTacc_i(before) ; released amount from SS = z·σ_d·√L (demand-driven) or
  z·d·σ_L (lead-time-driven), toolkit form. Illustrative: d = 100/day, σ_L 4 → 2 days, z = 2.33
  (99%)  SS 932 → 466 units released 466. Canonical program total: $2M released under the rule
  (never added to S8's $1.5M).
Worked peg (v105 §5.12, illustrative). ROUTER-100 at DC-East: on-hand 400; in-transit 600 lands
  day 5; production 800 day 9. Demand 500 day 3, 700 day 7, 400 day 10. PAB(3) = 400 − 500 =
  −100; PAB(7) = 1,000 − 1,200 = −200 (day-9 supply is too late; fix window 2 days); PAB(10) =
  1,800 − 1,600 = +200 .

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured number is two million dollars of safety stock released, and that is record, signed with planning and Finance under the both-errors-improved rule. Around it I model roughly eight hundred thousand a year of recurring value: about two hundred thousand in carry cost on the capital that came out, about three hundred thousand in expedite premium the shortage alert pre-empts because it fires a lead-time early instead of at line-down, and about three hundred thousand of planner capacity from the fifty percent effort cut. The two million and the fifty percent are measured; the three recurring pieces are my model on benchmark rates, and I keep them separate from the one-and-a-half million in the lane program."

If challenged — the full answers.

The expedite figure is the softest

It rests on about sixty expedites a year pre-empted at a five thousand dollar premium, both illustrative; to firm it I would pull the expedited-freight ledger for the six sites for the twelve months before and after the alert went live and count the premium directly.

Planner capacity is not cash

Agreed, it is two point four FTE of time redeployed, not headcount removed; the fifty percent is measured, the forty percent share of the week is my assumption, and a two-week time study on the S&OP prep cycle would replace it.

Why not claim the whole two million every year

Because a release is a one-time working-capital event; the recurring value is the carry at eight to twelve percent, and I would rather say two hundred thousand a year honestly than two million a year wrongly.

S7 — Elementum: making a contract manufacturer's BOM safe to plan on — phantoms, revision overlaps, one supply equation

Amazon LPs: Learn and Be Curious (primary — learned CM data by math) · Dive Deep · Earn Trust (external partner). Crosswalk: loop packet S6 — consistent. Guardrails: "six sites," never "6 NA sites"; no "500+ product families"; 60→95% belongs to S9; no tolerance value — the record doesn't carry one.

Honesty label. I owned the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan and the weekly Flextronics call; engineers built the normalization layer and quarantine to the spec; the commercial relationship belonged to the customer's procurement — my leverage was evidence. The $1.5M/580K belongs to the p99 program this backbone enabled (S8).
60-second pitch

"Situation. Flextronics supplied the BOM our component-demand visibility depended on; phantoms double-time demand if ingested as stocked, and overlapping revisions rewrite history if you auto-load one. Task. I owned ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan, and the weekly Flextronics call. Action. Discovery: sampled finished goods, exploded BOMs, compared component counts to Flextronics MRP — phantoms found by math. Plan & Design: phantom flatten with an explicit marker; effective-date quarantine, human disposition, never auto-load; supply equation signed. Build & Test: mapping tables owned by named site super-users; per-site reconciliation is the go-live gate. Cutover: three waves / six sites / 5 months; wave 3's hostile ERP used snapshot-diffing — 6 weeks vs a 3-week average, priced up front. Hypercare: the weekly call became standing, framed as their protection. Lesson: reconcile to the source's math, not its documentation. Result. Primary metric: contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade BOM/supply visibility — 0 → 6 in 3 waves / 5 months, zero auto-loaded revision overlaps. $ impact: ≈$0.3M/yr modeled — the six sites and zero overlaps are measured; the dollars are my model; the $1.5M belongs to S8."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Same manufacturer; Flextronics supplied the BOM and planner data that component-demand visibility depended on. Two traps documentation never confesses: phantom assemblies (logical groupings that pass demand through without stocking — ingest them as stocked and component need-dates get double-timed) and revision overlaps (two BOM revisions partially true for weeks — auto-load one and you rewrite demand history). Six site systems carried six definitions of yield, lead time and order status; MTO and MTS had different ATP semantics.

T — Own the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan — and the chair of the weekly Flextronics call.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

Flextronics supplied the BOM our component-demand visibility depended on, and two traps documentation never confesses — phantom assemblies and revision overlaps — manufacture phantom demand if ingested naively: phantoms double-time component need-dates, and an auto-loaded overlap rewrites demand history. The goal: contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade BOM and supply visibility, with zero auto-loaded overlaps.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

I owned the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan and the chair of the weekly Flextronics call. Challenges: six site systems with six definitions of yield, lead time and order status; MTO and MTS with different ATP semantics; one hostile legacy ERP with flat files and no change-data-capture; and no commercial leverage — evidence was my only lever.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Phantoms ingested as stocked double-time component demand — pulled early and counted twice — feeding false shortages and expedites acted on; an auto-loaded revision overlap rewrites demand for orders released under the prior revision. Both traps were named with evidence in the discovery memo.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Learn and Be Curious?

Because I learned the CM's data by math, not meetings — sampled finished goods, exploded their BOMs in our model, compared component counts to Flextronics' own MRP, and their engineers confirmed the phantom signature the mismatches revealed. Reconcile to the source's math, not its documentation.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Learn and Be Curious?

S16 — learning the stack to build the Snowflake pipeline — is the backup; the MIT toolkit (labeled) and E7 forecasting are the standing third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. For a current-role record of the same curiosity, S4 is the one: my own program's reports indicted my rule and I learned the promise math to fix it.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (canonical: wave 1 = two cleanest high-volume sites; wave 2 = three sites on parameterized maps; wave 3 = the hostile legacy ERP, flat files, no change-data-capture):

  1. Discovery — sampled finished goods and exploded them against Flextronics MRP (the phantom signature found by math); catalogued the six-site definition inventory (yield, lead time, order-status semantics); wrote the MTO/MTS tagging rule and a discovery memo naming the two traps. Exit bar: Flextronics engineering confirms the phantom signature; six definitions catalogued; wave order proposed.
  2. Plan & Design — supply equation (on-hand + production plan + released planned orders); ingest requirements (phantom flatten with marker; effective-date quarantine queue with human disposition — never auto-load); the reconciliation report as the per-site gate; the 2/3/1 wave plan; mapping-table ownership model; weekly call charter. Exit bar: requirements signed by customer planning; Flextronics staffs the weekly call; wave economics accepted.
  3. Build & Test — held engineers to the spec for normalization and quarantine; reconciliation report per site; parameterized mapping templates for wave 2; snapshot-diff design for wave 3 (consecutive full extracts, checksums, a completeness flag on every extract). Exit bar: wave-1 reconciliation within tolerance; zero auto-loaded overlaps in test; completeness flag on every extract.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (per site) — each site's go-live ran off its matched reconciliation report, mapping tables handed to a named super-user, disposition queue live. The wave-3 duration went to customer leadership up front as a priced wait. Exit bar: the reconciliation report was the gate, not a date on a slide — six sites live over three waves; hardest site 6 weeks vs the 3-week average, absorbed in plan.
  5. Hypercare / Close — weekly Flextronics DQ/disposition call left standing (it had become theirs — the forum that protected their feed's reputation); the wave-2 yield gap fixed in the mapping row, model untouched; completeness flag written as a rule in the ingest layer. Exit bar: zero auto-loaded revision overlaps; phantom double-timing eliminated at source; backbone handed to the 200-lane program (S8).

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

Phantom detection by count_model vs count_MRP on sampled finished goods; phantom flatten with an explicit marker so need-dates offset by the FG lead time only; effective-date quarantine with human disposition so overlap error is zero by construction; snapshot-diffing with checksums and a completeness flag for the no-CDC site. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan and the weekly Flextronics call; engineers built the normalization layer and quarantine to the spec; the commercial relationship belonged to the customer's procurement — my leverage was evidence.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Finding the phantoms by math and designing the two mechanisms — the marker and the quarantine — that made the traps impossible rather than policed. And the reconciliation report as the per-site gate, not a date on a slide.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

An external supplier with no obligation to care — solved by making the weekly call theirs: it protected Flextronics from blame for misses their feed didn't cause. The hostile wave-3 ERP — snapshot-diffing at six weeks against a three-week average, priced up front so leadership took it as the plan, not a slip.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the sampling-and-explosion analysis, the ingest requirements, the supply equation, the 2/3/1 wave plan, the gate and the call charter. Theirs: engineers built normalization and quarantine; named site super-users owned the mapping tables; Flextronics staffed the call and confirmed the signature.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Waves by cleanliness — the two cleanest high-volume sites first, so a failure in wave 1 cost two sites of credibility, not six. Buy-in: requirements signed by customer planning, Flextronics staffing the call because it was their protection, and the wave economics accepted up front.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

I refused auto-loading revisions, forcing one lead-time semantics across sites, and going live on a date instead of a matched report. ATP and lead-time meaning genuinely differ between MTO and MTS; one forced semantics lies about both.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: CM sites with planning-grade BOM 0 → 6 in 3 waves / 5 months; zero auto-loaded overlaps; hardest site in plan. $ impact: ≈$0.3M/yr modeled.

  • $ — ≈$0.3M/yr modeled (manual BOM reconciliation labor, expedites fired at phantom shortages, overlap rework avoided); the record has no dollar for this story — the $1.5M belongs to S8, the program this backbone enabled.
  • # — Contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade BOM 0 → 6, in 3 waves (record); zero auto-loaded revision overlaps — ever (record).
  • Time — 5 months for six sites; hardest site 6 weeks vs the 3-week average — priced up front and absorbed in plan (record).
  • Customer — Phantom double-timing eliminated at source, so planners stopped chasing false shortages that were never real; the weekly Flextronics call left standing as their forum (record).
  • Quality — The reconciliation report was the per-site go-live gate, not a date on a slide; mapping tables handed to named site super-users; completeness flag written as a rule in the ingest layer (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Sites 0 → 6 with zero auto-loaded overlaps is the record that matters — planning-grade data where there was none, in five months. The downstream dollar deliberately lives elsewhere: this backbone enabled the p99 lane program, and the $1.5M belongs to S8.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY — the ≈$0.3M/yr is modeled (reconciliation labor, false-shortage expedites, overlap rework), deliberately small because the big dollar belongs to the program this backbone enabled, and I won't double-count it. The record movement is sites 0 → 6 and overlaps at zero.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

I traded speed, never quality: three waves over five months instead of a big bang, so one surprise could not spend six sites — and the hostile site ran six weeks against a three-week average, priced up front.

Probing — I'm concerned about the 360 false shortages — tell me more.

Illustrative volume at the “phantom shows a shortage of 100” pattern, scaled. To firm it I would join the expedite ledger to the shortage alerts for the year before ingest and count how many alerts had no real component gap behind them.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Reconcile to the source's math, not its documentation. Differently: put the completeness flag on the extract from wave 1 — the unflagged partial extract is the failure that looks like thousands of phantom deletions.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Vendor and manufacturer master data at scale carries the same two traps — a phantom level in a kit BOM and an effectivity overlap on an engineering change both manufacture phantom demand. Detect by the source's own math, quarantine with a human disposition, and let the reconciliation report — not a date — open each wave.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The ask was planning-grade BOM and supply visibility at the CM sites — delivered 0 → 6 in three waves and five months, zero auto-loaded overlaps, the hardest site absorbed in plan, and the backbone handed to the 200-lane program (S8).

Discovery — How did you find the phantoms?

The two traps — phantoms and revision overlaps — named with evidence, the six sites' definitions catalogued, and Flextronics confirming the signature.

Phantoms ingested as stocked, revision overlaps auto-loaded, and partial extracts consumed. The first two had mechanisms from day one — marker and quarantine; the third got its completeness flag properly at wave 3, my do-differently.

By making the call theirs: it protected Flextronics from blame for misses their feed didn't cause. Evidence was my only leverage — I owned no commercial relationship.

Extracts flowing, a named super-user for every mapping table, and the reconciliation report built for that site. No super-user, no wave slot.

Reconciliation report matched within the published tolerance — the report was the gate, not a date on a slide. I won't quote the tolerance value; the record doesn't carry it.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The wave-2 yield gap: several points between their report and ours. Planners, Flextronics and engineers in one session traced it to engineering-sample pulls netted on their side versus gross started-units on ours — fixed in one mapping row.

Wave 3 ran six weeks against a three-week average because the no-CDC ERP needed snapshot-diffing. I had priced it up front, so leadership took it as the plan, not a slip.

W1: the normalization layer, quarantine, and the first two sites. W2: parameterized mapping tables and three more sites. W3: snapshot-diffing and the hardest site. Each wave's mapping tables stayed with a named super-user.

Six sites live behind matched reports, zero auto-loaded overlaps, the weekly call running without me. Left behind: a named super-user per mapping table, the quarantine queue with human disposition, the completeness-flag contract, the call as their forum. The model stayed standard; the mappings stayed theirs.

Nowhere on quality; I traded speed — three waves over five months instead of a big bang, so one surprise could not spend six sites. Refused: auto-loading revisions, forcing one lead-time semantics, going live on a date instead of a matched report.

Why not force one lead-time semantics across sites?

ATP and lead-time meaning genuinely differ between MTO and MTS; one forced semantics lies about both.

Do differently?

Put the completeness flag on the extract from wave 1 — the unflagged partial extract is the failure that looks like thousands of phantom deletions.

Where this lands at Amazon: vendor and manufacturer master data at scale carries the same two traps — a phantom level in a kit BOM and an effectivity overlap on an engineering change both manufacture phantom demand. The transferable rules: detect by the source's own math, quarantine with a human disposition, and let the reconciliation report — not a date — open each wave.

The math — full derivation, step by step

Phantom double-timing. For finished good FG with lead time LT_FG and phantom sub-assembly SA
  (special procurement 50, never stocked) with nominal LT_SA : correct component need-date =
  due_FG − LT_FG (pass-through). Naive ingest (SA as stocked) yields due_FG − LT_FG − LT_SA and
  a second requirement at the SA level
demand offset twice and counted twice. Detection: count_model(c | FG) ≠ count_MRP(c | FG)
  systematically for components under SA.
Worked example (Rune §7 tree, illustrative quantities). FG-1000 → SA-200 (phantom) → COMP-A ×2,
  COMP-B ×1; FG-1000 → COMP-C ×4. Order: 100 FG due day 20; LT_FG = 5, nominal LT_SA = 3.
  Correct: COMP-A 200 due day 15; COMP-B 100 due day 15; COMP-C 400 due day 15. Naive: COMP-A
  200 due day 12 plus an SA-200 requirement of 100 at day 15 that MRP tries to "stock" —
  components appear 3 days early and SA shows a phantom shortage of 100. Flattened with marker:
  qty-per(FG, COMP-A) = 1 × 2 = 2, need-date offset = LT_FG only .
Revision overlap. Revisions R1 (valid-to d1 ) and R2 (valid-from d0 < d1 ): for t ∈ [d0, d1]
  both are partially true. Auto-load rule pick(R2) rewrites demand for orders released under R1
  history error = Σ_orders∈[d0,d1] |qty_per_R2 − qty_per_R1| × qty . Quarantine: hold both;
  human dispositions per order/effectivity; error = 0 by construction. Yield gross vs net
  (wave-2 gap). yield_site = good / started_net where started_net excludes engineering-sample
  pulls; yield_model = good / started_gross . Gap = yield_site − yield_model = good ×
  (1/started_net − 1/started_gross) — "several points"; fixed in the mapping row
    (define started
  = net of sample pulls), model untouched. Snapshot-diffing (no CDC). Δ_t = extract_t −
  extract_{t−1} (row-level by key); accept only if checksum_t valid and completeness_flag_t =
  rows_t / expected_t ≥ 1 − ε . A partial file with 40% of rows looks like 60% deletions — the
  flag prevents consumption.
Wave economics. Waves W1 = 2 sites, W2 = 3, W3 = 1 hostile; average 3 weeks/site, hostile 6
  weeks  2×3 + 3×3 + 6 = 21 weeks ≈ 5 months  (sequential upper bound; parallelism inside waves
  absorbed the hostile site "in plan"). Failure in W1 costs 2 sites of credibility, not
    6.
Reconciliation gate. Per site: |component_count_model − component_count_MRP| / count_MRP ≤ τ on
  the sampled FG set, and yield/LT vs the site's own reports within tolerance — the report is
  the gate.

It is; the honest replacement is the disposition-queue log we left behind, which timestamps every human decision, so a month of that log against the pre-ingest planner timesheets would give you the real before and after.
