SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The six-site manufacturer's logistics manager asked for a "lead-time variability report." The real problem: inventory planning ran on carrier-brochure lead times — every buffer sized on a variability nobody had measured; too much buffer on reliable lanes, too little on fat-tailed ones.

T — Nobody owned the seam between transportation reality and planning parameters; I took it.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

Inventory planning ran on carrier-brochure lead times — every buffer sized on a variability nobody had measured: too much buffer on reliable lanes, too little on fat-tailed ones. The goal: measured lanes replace catalog values, a promise at a defensible tail, and buffer released only on evidence.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

The logistics manager asked for a “lead-time variability report”; the real problem was the parameter, and nobody owned the seam between transportation reality and planning parameters — I took it. Scope: 200 lanes by site, lane and mode. The challenge: the average hides the tail, and a buffer cut before evidence is a stockout waiting.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Keep planning on brochures and you hold too much on reliable lanes and stock out on fat-tailed ones; cut buffer without evidence and the stockout is yours. And an unmaintained promise decays into a new brochure — which is why every parameter got a drift flag.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Dive Deep?

Because the whole story is refusing the surface request — a report — and measuring the underlying distributions: mean, spread and tail per lane, production cycle plus inbound transit vs actual, with p99 defined cumulatively because a right-skewed lane's normal shortcut understates the tail.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Dive Deep?

S10 — Austria, where I measured the measurement — is the backup; S3 (the grain problem) and S7 (the phantom math) are thirds.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. The current-role story with the same LP is S3 — aggregates de-aggregated to the cent — and S10 is the same era's backup.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (dependency: the six-site canonical model (S7) — six definitions cannot produce one σ; lane-cohort counts and week markers are not in the record):

  1. Discovery — reframed the report request as a parameter problem; scoped 200 lanes by site/lane/mode with success criteria (measured lanes replace catalog values; buffer released only on evidence); showed one right-skewed lane where the average hid the tail. Exit bar: planning and logistics agree the problem is the parameter, not the report.
  2. Plan & Design — wrote the requirement to the data team (mean, spread, tail per lane; production cycle + inbound transit vs actual), the p99 promise policy, the release rule as a signed planning act (fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves — refused as a blanket release, agreed as a rule), the stockout-and-expedite watch, the drift flag and refresh cadence. On p95 vs p99 I showed the asymmetry per lane and left the percentile choice to planning — their choice, my frame. Exit bar: policy signed by planning; Finance agrees the release rule in the room; data team accepts the spec.
  3. Build & Test — data team built the lane distributions and tracked lead-time accuracy per lane; I showed the asymmetry per lane (line-down cost vs carrying cost). Exit bar: lead-time accuracy 99% on the 200-lane set — the bar I would not endorse a cut below.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — planners executed the buffer release under the signed rule, fat-tail lanes excluded until their source process moved, the watch armed first. Exit bar: 580K units / ~$1.5M released; no stockout in the watch window.
  5. Hypercare / Close — read the stockout-and-expedite watch, set drift flags per lane, handed the refresh cadence to planning. Exit bar: watch clean; the promise stays measured rather than decaying into a new brochure.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

p99 per lane defined cumulatively — the smallest d with P(L ≤ d) ≥ 0.99, not μ + 2.33σ unless the lane is normal — lead-time accuracy gated at 99% on the 200-lane set, and SS = z × d × σ_L sizing under the release rule (toolkit label: how I explain it now with the MIT inventory-theory toolkit, not a program artifact). The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned the requirement (measured lanes, not brochures), the p99 promise policy and the release rule; the data team built the distributions; planners sized the buffers; Finance signed the release.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Reframing a report request into a promise-and-buffer policy, and writing the release rule as a signed planning act with the exception inside it — fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves — so the rule did the refusing when Finance pushed for a broader release.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Finance pushing for a broader release — answered with the rule they had signed and the lanes' asymmetry. The percentile fight never happened: I showed each lane's line-down cost against carrying cost and left the choice to planning; they chose p99.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the requirement to the data team (mean, spread, tail per lane), the promise policy, the release rule, the 99% endorsement bar, the stockout-and-expedite watch and the drift-flag design. Theirs: the data team built the distributions and tracked accuracy; planners executed the release; Finance signed each lane.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Scope was 200 lanes by site, lane and mode with success criteria written at discovery; the release went lane by lane under the signed rule with the watch armed first. Buy-in: planning signed the policy and Finance agreed the release rule in the same room, before any cut.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

I challenged the brochure itself — showed one right-skewed lane where the average hid the tail — and refused both averaging away a tail and endorsing a cut below 99% accuracy. Both refusals were written into the act before anyone asked.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: lead-time accuracy → 99% on 200 lanes at the p99 promise. $ impact: 580K units / ~$1.5M released without a stockout (record, one-time); ≈$0.6M/yr modeled recurring around it.

  • $ — 580K units / ~$1.5M of safety stock released without a stockout (record, one-time working capital); ≈$0.6M/yr modeled recurring around it (carry at a benchmark 20% all-in rate + expedites avoided) — a different program from S6's $2M; never add them.
  • # — 200 lanes measured by site, lane and mode; 580K units released (record).
  • % — Lead-time accuracy 99% on the 200-lane set at the p99 promise — the bar I would not endorse a cut below (record).
  • Customer — Service held: zero stockouts on released lanes through the watch window (record).
  • Quality — Release only under the signed rule — fat-tail lanes kept buffer until the source process moved; drift flags and a refresh cadence keep the promise measured, never a new brochure (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

The release — 580K units / ~$1.5M without a stockout — is the record that proves the promise, and 99% lead-time accuracy on 200 lanes is the bar that made it safe. Service held is the result planners feel; a release that causes a stockout is worse than no release.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY — the ~$1.5M is a one-time working-capital release, booked once as a signed planning act, not an annual saving; what recurs is my model (≈$0.3M carry at a benchmark rate plus avoided expedites), and I never add this program to S6's $2M.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

Fat-tail lanes kept their buffer until the source process moved — capital stayed tied up where the tail said it must — and I would not endorse a cut below the 99% bar, trading release speed for a release with zero stockouts in the watch.

Probing — I'm concerned about the expedite number — tell me more.

The softest — it assumes ten thousand shipments a year on the two hundred lanes and one late arrival in ten forcing an expedite before p99. To firm it I would pull the expedite ledger by lane for the year before and after the release and compare directly.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Fat tails keep their buffer, and I owned the rule, not the model as a day job. And give every parameter a drift flag so the promise never decays into a new brochure.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Inbound lead-time variability is half of every buffer and every promise — measure the lane's own distribution, promise at the tail you can defend, release capital only on evidence with the exception written into the rule, and give every parameter a drift flag.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The success criteria from discovery were: measured lanes replace catalog values, buffer released only on evidence, fat tails keep buffer. All three held — 99% accuracy at the p99 promise, 580K units / ~$1.5M out, no stockout in the watch window.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When planning and logistics agreed the problem was the parameter, not the report — brochure lead times sizing every buffer — and the 200-lane scope by site, lane and mode was written. The canonical model from the six-site program made one σ possible.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote them — measured lanes replace catalog values; buffer released only on evidence; fat tails keep buffer. Planning signed the policy; Finance agreed the release rule in the same room.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

The average hiding the tail, a buffer cut before evidence, and the promise decaying into a new brochure. Mitigations: cumulative p99 per lane, a 99% accuracy bar before endorsement, drift flags with a refresh cadence.

How did you handle Finance pushing for a broader release?

With the rule they had signed — fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves. I showed the lanes and the asymmetry; the rule did the refusing.

Why p99 and not p95?

Per lane, the cost of a line-down on a fat-tail lane dwarfed the carrying cost of the extra day. I showed the asymmetry and let planning choose; they chose p99.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a lane?

Distribution built from actuals — mean, spread, tail — accuracy tracked, p99 published, drift flag set. Below 99% accuracy on the set, I did not endorse a release.

Who built the distributions?

The data team, to my requirement. I owned the requirement, the promise policy and the release rule; planners sized the buffers; Finance signed. The formula is how I explain it now with the MIT toolkit — not a program artifact.

Cutover — What made you willing to endorse the release?

Lead-time accuracy at 99% on the 200-lane set and the watch armed. Then planners released under the rule, lane by lane, with fat tails excluded.

What was your rollback trigger?

Any stockout or expedite on a released lane during the watch — buffer restored. It never triggered.

Hypercare — How did you know it was closed?

The watch stayed clean, 580K units and about $1.5M were out without a stockout, and planning owned the refresh cadence and drift flags. The promise stayed measured.

What did you hand to planning at close?

The drift flag per lane with its refresh cadence, the p99 promise per lane, and the release rule as a signed act they could re-apply. The stockout-and-expedite watch became a standing report.

Cross-phase — Which lanes did you hold and how did the business take it?

The fat-tail lanes — buffer kept until the source process moved. Finance accepted because the exception was in the rule they signed, not a judgment I made afterwards.

What did you refuse?

Averaging away a tail, and endorsing a cut below 99% accuracy. Both were written into the act before anyone asked.

Where this lands at Amazon: inbound lead-time variability is half of every buffer and every promise. The transferable discipline: measure the lane's own distribution, promise at the tail you can defend, release capital only on evidence with the exception written into the rule, and give every parameter a drift flag so the promise never decays into a new brochure.

The math — full derivation, step by step

(Toolkit label: MIT Supply Chain MicroMasters inventory theory — how I explain the requirement
  now; the data team built the distributions, planners sized buffers, Finance signed the
  release.)
Measured lane distribution. For lane l, actual transit L_l from shipment history (production
  cycle + inbound transit vs predicted).
Statistics: μ_L, σ_L, p50, p90, p99 . p99 defined cumulatively. p99_l = min{ d : P(L_l ≤ d) ≥
  0.99 } — the smallest d with 99% of shipments arriving by d. Not μ + 2.33σ unless the lane is
  normal; a right-skewed lane's normal shortcut understates the tail (illustrative from the
  record: by 3.4 days).
Lead-time accuracy. LTacc_l = 1 − |actual − promised| / promised per shipment, aggregated per
  lane; gate 99% on the 200-lane set before endorsing any cut.
Safety stock (toolkit forms). Demand-driven: SS = z × σ_d × √L . Lead-time-driven: SS = z × d ×
  σ_L . Combined: SS = z × √(L σ_d² + d² σ_L²) . z = 2.33 at 99% service, 1.65 at 95%.
  Illustrative worked example ("illustrative, not program data"). Lane with d = 500
    units/day,
  brochure σ_L assumed 5 days (folklore), measured σ_L = 1.2 days; z = 2.33. Brochure SS = 2.33
  × 500 × 5 = 5,825 units; measured SS = 2.33 × 500 × 1.2 = 1,398 units  release 4,427 units on
  this lane if forecast error also improved (release rule). Fat-tail lane: measured σ_L = 6.5
  days (worse than brochure)  SS rises to 7,573 — buffer kept until the source process moves.
  Sanity: units = units/day × days ; SS decreases with σ_L, never with wishful averaging.
Canonical program result. 580K units / ~$1.5M released across 200 lanes at 99% lead-time
  accuracy, service held (implied ≈ $2.59/unit — derived; low-value components). Different
  program from S6's $2M — never add.
Drift flag. Recompute p99 per lane on a refresh cadence; flag if |p99_new − p99_old| > τ or if
  LTacc falls below floor  buffer decision re-opened; the promise stays measured, never decays
  into a new brochure.
Cost asymmetry (why p99). Cost_line_down × P(L > promise) vs holding cost × extra days × d ; on
  fat-tail lanes the first term dominates  planners pick p99 per lane after seeing both numbers.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured result is five hundred eighty thousand units, about a million and a half dollars of safety stock, released at ninety-nine percent lead-time accuracy with zero stockouts on the watch; that is record and Finance signed it. What recurs around it I model at roughly six hundred thousand a year: about three hundred thousand in carrying cost on capital we no longer hold, and about three hundred thousand in expedites we no longer pay because the promise sits at the ninety-ninth percentile instead of a brochure number. If challenged — the full answers.

The expedite number is the softest

It assumes ten thousand shipments a year on the two hundred lanes and that one late arrival in ten forced an expedite before p99; to firm it I would pull the expedite ledger by lane for the year before and after the release and compare directly.

Twenty percent carrying cost is high

Fifteen to twenty-five is the textbook range and I use the middle; capital alone is eight to twelve, so if Finance wants capital only the carry is about a hundred and fifty thousand and I would say that instead.

Why is the million and a half not an annual saving

Because it is a one-time working-capital release, booked once as a signed planning act; what recurs is the carry and the avoided expedites, and I would rather be right at six hundred thousand a year than wrong at a million and a half.

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The customer's Global Head of Supply Chain wanted end-to-end inbound visibility on 1,000 global lanes in two months — scope, schedule and quality all fixed. Lanes differed by orders of magnitude in spend, volume and criticality; carriers differed in EDI capability; completeness was 60% and high-value lanes were effectively blind.

T — Own the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

The customer's Global Head of Supply Chain wanted end-to-end inbound visibility on 1,000 global lanes in two months — scope, schedule and quality all fixed — while shipment-data completeness sat at 60% and high-value lanes were effectively blind. The goal: visibility that was actually trustworthy, at the spend that mattered first.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor. Challenges: lanes differing by orders of magnitude in spend, volume and criticality; carriers differing in EDI capability; and a fixed triangle of scope, schedule and quality that could not all hold.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — 1,000 lanes at 60% completeness in two months would have shipped exactly that. And without a floor for carriers without EDI, the long tail gets priced out of the standard.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit?

Because I took the re-scope — 597 lanes first, not 1,000 — two levels up to the Global Head of Supply Chain with the spend curve and the lane owners' validation attached, then committed to delivering the remainder behind the same gate. And the gate held in public: one cohort failed twice on a timestamp format and I held it both times.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit?

S14 — refusing the quiet fix — is the backup; S5 (the council without owning a system) and S2 (the throttle) are the thirds.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery — built the spend Pareto and lane scoring model (spend × criticality × mode × volume) and the carrier EDI-capability survey; pre-validated the 597 / ~200 / remainder scope with Transportation and Procurement before taking it up. Exit bar: lane owners cannot contradict the cut; the executive accepts the phased plan in spend share, not lane count.
  2. Plan & Design — wrote the onboarding standard (document set + XML/portal floor — "the standard was the data, not the transport"), the ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, the per-lane scorecard spec (events received / expected; 997 rate; tracking-number joinability), the harness design. Exit bar: standard signed by Transportation; gate published to carriers before cohort 1; harness templates exist.
  3. Build & Test — held engineers to the harness (per-partner mapping templates, test scripts, DQ checklist), test files per carrier, a parallel run per lane, scorecards live. Exit bar: each cohort's lanes clear ≥95% valid ASN in test before activation.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (per cohort) — activated cohorts only off the published gate; published the scorecards; recorded the hold decision when a cohort failed. Exit bar: cohort active only after the gate; a failing cohort held — twice, on a timestamp format.
  5. Hypercare / Close — weekly completeness scorecards; harness reused for later waves; method written up as the sourcing SOP (spend file in → phased plan out). Exit bar: completeness 60% → 95%; 100% visibility on high-value lanes; remaining waves behind the same gate.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor; engineers built the maps and harness to the standard; Transportation owned the carrier relationships.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Pre-validating the 597 cut with Transportation and Procurement so no lane owner could contradict it in the leadership meeting, and publishing the ≥95% gate to carriers before cohort one — a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Carriers unable to produce valid ASNs — answered with the XML/portal floor (“the standard was the data, not the transport”) and a hold rule; the failing cohort's timestamp format — held twice, fixed, then activated; bespoke maps per partner — avoided by the parameterized harness.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the spend Pareto and lane scoring model, the carrier EDI-capability survey, the onboarding standard, the gate, the scorecard spec and the hold decisions. Theirs: engineers built the mapping templates, test scripts and harness; Transportation held the carrier relationships and accepted the holds.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Spend share, not lane count — 597 lanes carrying 80% of spend first, ~200 next, the remainder after, each behind the same gate. Buy-in: pre-validation with the lane owners first, then the executive accepted the phased plan off the curve.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

The 1,000-in-two-months ask itself — challenged two levels up with the spend curve, deferring ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend, priced. And I refused activating any cohort that failed the gate — twice, on the same cohort.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: completeness 60% → 95%; 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one; freight −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record); adoption 8×.

  • $ — Freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months on the onboarded lanes (record); my three-way split (routing-guide compliance, mode discipline, expedites and detention) decomposes the record, never adds to it; ≈$0.4M/yr modeled document-handling labor on top.
  • # — 597 of 1,000 lanes = 80% of freight spend live in month one; ~200 the next wave; the remainder behind the same gate (record).
  • % — Completeness 60% → 95%; the ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate — held twice on a cohort that failed on a timestamp format (record).
  • Time — 597 lanes live in month one; the record $2M landed within six months (record).
  • Customer — 100% visibility on high-value lanes; small carriers kept inside the standard by the XML/portal floor instead of being priced out (record).
  • Quality — The gate is the product — automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers; adoption 8×; the method written up as the sourcing SOP (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Completeness 60 → 95% is the record the whole program was for, and freight −10% ≈ $2M in six months is what the executive funded the next wave on. The 597 = 80% of spend live in month one is the proof the re-scope was right.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the $2M is the record as measured: minus ten percent on the onboarded lanes' spend in six months, kept as stated rather than doubled into a run-rate. My three-way split of it decomposes the record, never adds to it.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend were deferred so the 597 could go live at the highest data quality in month one — priced, and delivered later behind the same gate. Quality was never traded: a lane that slipped below its scorecard went back to “fix,” not “live.”

Probing — I'm concerned about your split of the $2M — tell me more.

The split is a judgment on how the ten points divide; to firm it I would tag every invoice line on the onboarded lanes by cause — off-guide carrier, mode change, expedite — for the six months and let the ledger set the shares.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — the gate is the product. Differently: the harness from cohort one, not cohort two.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Vendor and carrier onboarding at scale is a Pareto plus a gate — spend share first, a published data-quality bar a partner must clear before activation, and a floor (portal/templates) so the long tail is kept inside the standard rather than priced out of it.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The ask was 1,000 lanes in two months; the delivery was 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one at ≥95% valid ASN, completeness 60 → 95%, the remaining waves behind the same gate — and the executive accepted the phased plan in spend share, not lane count.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the spend Pareto was pre-validated by Transportation and Procurement and the scope proposal — 597 first, ~200 next, remainder after — could not be contradicted by any lane owner in the leadership meeting.

Who signed the scope?

The Global Head of Supply Chain accepted it after seeing the curve; the lane owners had already validated it. I priced the deferral in spend share, not lane count.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Carriers unable to produce valid ASNs, the scope being contradicted in the room, and bespoke maps per partner. Mitigations: XML/portal floor plus a hold rule, pre-validation, and a parameterized harness.

What was the entry bar for a cohort?

Standard and gate published to its carriers, mapping templates instantiated, test files in hand, a scorecard per lane. Then ≥95% valid ASN in test before activation.

How did you set the standard?

850/855/856/940/945/214/997 on X12, with XML or portal templates as the floor for carriers without EDI. The standard was the data, not the transport.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a lane?

Valid-ASN rate ≥95%, 997 acknowledgments flowing, tracking numbers joinable, completeness on its scorecard. Below that, the lane was fixed, not automated.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The cohort test day: every carrier's file through the harness in one sitting; defects classified as format, timestamp, missing segment or join key, each with an owner. That is where the timestamp defect surfaced.

Cutover — Which cohort did you hold and how did the business take it?

One cohort failed twice on a carrier timestamp format; I held it both times. Transportation accepted because the 95% gate was published before cohort one — a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion.

What did each wave deliver?

Wave 1: 597 lanes, 80% of spend, live in month one. Wave 2: ~200 lanes on a harness that made the work mostly configuration. Wave 3: the tail, including non-EDI carriers on templates.

Hypercare — How did you know you were done? What did you watch?

Completeness 60% → 95%, 100% visibility on high-value lanes, the harness reused without me, the method adopted as the sourcing SOP. Watched weekly: per-lane completeness, the 997 rate, tracking-number joinability. A lane that slipped below its scorecard went back to "fix," not "live."

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

Deferred ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend so the 597 could go live at the highest data quality in month one — priced, and delivered later behind the same gate. Escalated the re-scope to the Global Head of Supply Chain — two levels up — with the spend curve and the lane owners' validation attached. Refused: activating a cohort that failed the gate.

Did you deliver the remaining 400? · Do differently?

Yes — later waves, same gate; the first 597 carried the spend and the decision. Differently: the harness from cohort one, not cohort two.

Where this lands at Amazon: vendor and carrier onboarding at scale is a Pareto plus a gate: spend share first, a published data-quality bar a partner must clear before activation, and a floor (portal/templates) so the long tail is kept inside the standard rather than priced out of it. The sentence that travels: automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers.

The math — full derivation, step by step

Spend Pareto. Sort lanes by annual freight spend s_l descending; cumulative share S(k) = Σ_{l≤k}
  s_l / Σ_all s_l .
Canonical: 597 of 1,000 lanes  S(597) = 80% of spend. Priority score per lane (spreadsheet):
  score_l = f(forwarder) × spend_l × criticality_l × mode_weight × volume_l — a weighted
  ranking, not an optimizer.
Cohort gate. validASN_rate = ASNs passing all validation rules / ASNs received over the test
  window; activate the next cohort only if validASN_rate ≥ 0.95 . One cohort failed twice on a
  carrier timestamp format (invalid  excluded from valid count, so the rate fell below 0.95) —
  held.
Completeness. completeness_l = events received / events expected per lane (expected = shipments
  × milestones per mode). Canonical: 60% → 95%. Illustrative: 200 shipments × 4 milestones = 800
  expected; 480 received  60%; 760  95%. 997 acknowledgment rate. ack_rate = 997s received /
  documents sent per partner (pipe-alive proof; target ~100%; a drop is a transport failure, not
  a data failure).
Joinability. join_rate = 856/214 events with a matching PO#/tracking# in our data / events
  received ; unjoinable events are received but useless.
Freight result. Spend −10% ≈ $2M in 6 months  implied addressable base ≈ $20M/yr on the
  onboarded lanes (derived from the canonical pair, stated as such). Adoption 8×; high-value
  lanes 100% visible.
Sanity: S(k) is monotone non-decreasing; a gate on rate (not count) prevents a large cohort from
  hiding bad partners; completeness ≤ 100% (over-reporting = duplicate events  dedupe rule).

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured number is freight spend down ten percent, about two million dollars in six months on the five hundred ninety-seven lanes that carried eighty percent of spend, with completeness sixty to ninety-five percent and adoption eight x. Inside that two million I model roughly eight hundred thousand from catching off-guide carrier spend, six hundred thousand from mode discipline once the ETA was trusted, and six hundred thousand from expedites and detention that chase calls used to cause; those three are my split, the two million is the record. On top of it I model about four hundred thousand a year of document-handling labor that X12 replaced, and that piece is entirely mine."

If challenged — the full answers.

The split of the two million is the softest

It is a judgment on how the ten points divide; to firm it I would tag every invoice line on the onboarded lanes by cause, off-guide carrier, mode change, or expedite, for the six months and let the ledger set the shares.

Ninety thousand documents is a guess

Fifty shipments a lane and three touched documents each is illustrative; the ISA and GS control-number counts on the VAN for the first full quarter would give the exact document volume in an afternoon.

Labor saved is not cash

Correct, nine thousand hours is coordinator capacity moved from keying to exceptions; I would measure it as headcount held flat while lanes went from six hundred to a thousand, which is the number Finance can see.

S10 — Elementum: Austria — "the noise said carriers; the data said measurement" (routing-guide rules engine, 25 sites, ~$8M)

Amazon LPs: Are Right, A Lot (primary) · Dive Deep (backup) · Broad Responsibility (primary — one site's fix rolled to 25 sites) · Earn Trust (the Finance-signed method). Crosswalk: not in the loop packet — safe anywhere. Guardrails: say $8M, never "$1M"; "~99% in 3 months," never "two months"; 1–2 hrs/wk is the after, never the before.

Honesty label. I owned the analysis and the rules design and pulled the data myself; the data platform team built the engine; Finance signed the savings method before any dollar was claimed. Site-wave counts and dates beyond "Austria first, then 25 sites" are not in the record.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Austria transportation could not measure on-time delivery; reports said data quality was 40%; the team spent 8–10 hours a week reconciling by hand; everyone believed the carriers were late. Task. SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Transportation at the customer's Austria site could not measure on-time delivery. Reports said data quality was 40%; the team spent 8–10 hours a week reconciling promised vs actual dates by hand; the freight base was ≈$5M/yr with no enforceable OTD. Everyone — the site, the carriers' account teams, my own sponsor — believed the carriers were late.

T — I owned the analysis and the rules design.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

Transportation at the customer's Austria site could not measure on-time delivery; reports said data quality was 40%; the team spent 8–10 hours a week reconciling promised vs actual dates by hand; the freight base was ≈$5M/yr with no enforceable OTD. The goal: an OTD the carriers could not dispute, and savings claimed only on a method Finance signed first.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

I owned the analysis and the rules design; the data platform team built the engine. The challenge: everyone — the site, the carriers' account teams, my own sponsor — already believed the carriers were late, and the data had to overturn the room's explanation.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Blaming carriers off bad measurement — a scorecard on 55% data the carriers would dispute and win — plus 8–10 hours a week of manual reconciliation continuing at every site, and any savings claim disputed after the fact.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Are Right, A Lot?

“The noise said carriers; the data said measurement” — I pulled three years of shipment history in SQL myself, established true DQ was ~55%, not 40%, and the fix was a rules engine, not a carrier beating. Being right here meant the data overturning the explanation everyone held.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Are Right, A Lot?

S8 — measured lanes vs brochure — is the backup; S4, the wrong rule corrected right, is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. For the same LP in my current role, S4 is the record: my own rule, indicted by my own program's reports, fixed with promise math.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery — pulled three years of shipment history in SQL myself; ran the defect census (missing rates, wrong-grain lanes, five spellings of one carrier name, brochure transit times); established true DQ ~55% vs 40% reported; reframed the problem as measurement, not carriers. Exit bar: sponsor accepts the reframe on the evidence; defect classes named and counted.
  2. Plan & Design — rules-engine spec (lookup by ship-from/to, mode, volume, Incoterm, SLA; a no-hit pages a human, never a default carrier); reason-code alert design; the savings method signed with Finance before any dollar was claimed (contracted guide rate − paid rate, per shipment, executed only); the scorecard DQ gate (automated OTD = manual sample per lane cohort). Exit bar: spec signed by transportation; Finance method signed; scorecard gate published.
  3. Build & Test — held engineering to the spec; weekly defect burn-down by reason code; manual-sample comparison per lane cohort. Exit bar: DQ trending to ~99%; automated OTD matches the manual sample per cohort.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (Austria) — released the carrier scorecard only after the gate passed; retired the manual reconciliation (8–10 → 1–2 hrs/wk); stood up the savings ledger per shipment. Exit bar: a scorecard the carriers could not dispute; $500K/yr on the Finance-signed method.
  5. Hypercare / Close → scale — super-user model per region; one-page runbook (add a lane · read a no-hit · when to escalate); two shadowed rule reviews per site; rolled out site by site. Exit bar: 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes ≈ $8M; ~99% DQ; adoption cost per site = a rules file + one DQ cycle, in days.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

DQ = shipments with a valid promised date, a valid actual date and a resolvable lane, over all shipments; promised = ship date + transit(lane key), with Incoterm in the key on ~8% of lanes because it changes who owns the clock; a no-hit pages a human, never a default carrier. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned the analysis and the rules design and pulled the data myself; the data platform team built the engine; Finance signed the savings method before any dollar was claimed.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Pulling the three-year history myself — the room already had an explanation, and SQL was faster than a request queue and could not be argued with — and signing the savings method with Finance before the number existed: contracted guide rate minus paid rate, per shipment, executed only.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

A sponsor unhappy with the delay for weeks — answered with the defect classes trending down weekly and the manual-sample gate the scorecard had to pass; he was right to want speed, and the carriers never disputed the result. Incoterm semantics — put into the lane key rather than papered over.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the SQL pull, the defect census, the rules-engine spec, the reason-code alert design, the Finance-signed method and the scorecard gate. Theirs: the data platform team built the engine; regional super-users ran the rules files after two shadowed reviews each.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Evidence before enforcement — the manual reconciliation stayed alive until automated OTD matched the manual sample per lane cohort. Buy-in: transportation signed the spec, Finance signed the method, and scale went site by site at a rules file plus one DQ cycle each.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

The room's explanation that the carriers were late — overturned with three years of their own data. And I refused a scorecard on 55% data, a silent default carrier on a no-hit, and any savings number without a Finance-signed method.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: DQ ~55% → ~99% in 3 months; $500K/yr Austria → ~$8M across 25 sites (record); manual reconciliation 8–10 → 1–2 hrs/wk.

  • $ — $500K/yr at Austria — 10% of a ~$5M freight base on the Finance-signed, executed-only method — then ~$8M across 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes (record roll-up; ~$320K/site average, derived); plus ≈$0.5M/yr modeled analyst capacity.
  • # — 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes on the same rules file and one DQ cycle each; three years of shipment history pulled in SQL for the baseline (record).
  • % — Routing-guide data quality ~55% true (40% reported) → ~99% in 3 months (record).
  • Time — ~99% DQ in 3 months; adoption cost per site = a rules file + one DQ cycle, in days (record).
  • Customer — A carrier scorecard the carriers could not dispute — automated OTD matched the manual sample per lane cohort before release (record).
  • Quality — Manual reconciliation 8–10 → 1–2 hrs/wk per site; the savings method signed with Finance before any dollar was claimed; negotiated-but-not-executed never counted (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

DQ ~55 → ~99% in three months is the record that made everything else possible; $500K/yr at Austria and ~$8M across 25 sites are the Finance-signed consequences. The manual hours falling from 8–10 to 1–2 a week is what the team felt.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the $500K is 10% of a ~$5M freight base on the executed-only method (contracted guide rate minus paid rate, per shipment), and the ~$8M is the same ledger across 25 sites. Negotiated-but-not-executed never counted.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

Date for evidence — I delayed the scorecard weeks rather than ship it on bad data; scope was never cut. The sponsor was unhappy and right to want speed; the carriers never disputed the result.

Probing — I'm concerned about the ~$8M roll-up — tell me more.

It is the Finance-signed, executed-only method applied at each site, not a projection; the assumption underneath is that the ten percent Austria rate held elsewhere, and what firms it is the per-shipment ledger at each site — which is what Finance read.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Measure before you blame; sign the method before the number. Differently: start with the industry benchmark for lane transit times instead of proving the brochure wrong lane by lane — same answer, faster.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

This is the model for how a savings claim should be built — method signed with Finance before the number, executed-only, then scaled site by site with the adoption cost measured in days. The Broad Responsibility angle: one site's fix became 25 sites' instrument.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The site needed enforceable OTD; it got a scorecard the carriers could not dispute, DQ at ~99% in three months, $500K/yr on the signed method and ~$8M at network scale — with manual reconciliation down to 1–2 hours a week.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the three-year pull had every blank classified — missing rate, wrong grain, name variant, brochure time — and the sponsor accepted that true quality was ~55%, not 40%, and that the problem was measurement, not carriers.

Why pull the data yourself?

Because the room already had an explanation. Three years of history in SQL was faster than a request queue and could not be argued with.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Incoterm changing who owns the clock, a silent default carrier on a no-hit, and savings disputed after the fact. Mitigations: Incoterm in the key, page a human, Finance-signed method before any claim.

How did you handle the sponsor objecting to the delay?

I showed the defect classes trending down weekly and the manual-sample gate the scorecard had to pass. He was unhappy for weeks and right to want speed; the carriers never disputed the result.

Who signed the savings method and when?

Finance, before the first dollar was claimed: contracted rate minus paid rate, per shipment, per lane, executed only. Negotiated-but-not-executed never counted.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a lane rule?

It resolves without no-hits across the sample window and its automated OTD matches the manual sample for its cohort. Until then the manual reconciliation kept running.

How did you treat data defects?

As classes, not one-offs: alerts by reason code, trended weekly, burned down — root cause at scale. That is how 55% became ~99% in three months.

Cutover — What made you say go on the scorecard?

Automated OTD matching the manual sample per lane cohort. Anything less would have shipped a scorecard the carriers could dispute — and they would have won.

What was your rollback?

The manual reconciliation stayed alive until the match held. Reversibility was free because I didn't retire the old process early.

Hypercare — How did you decide Austria was closed and scale could start?

DQ ~99%, manual hours at 1–2 a week, the scorecard undisputed, and a regional super-user running the rules file after two shadowed reviews. Then 25 sites at a rules file plus one DQ cycle each.

What did you hand to the regional super-users?

Their rules file, the one-page runbook — add a lane, read a no-hit, when to escalate — and two shadowed rule reviews each. After that, adding a site cost days.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you refuse?

I traded date for evidence — delayed the scorecard weeks rather than ship it on bad data; scope was never cut. Refused: a scorecard on 55% data, a silent default carrier, and any savings number without a Finance-signed method.

How do you know the $8M is real? · Do differently?

Finance-signed, executed-only method applied across 25 sites; the $500K pilot is 10% of a ~$5M base; anything merely negotiated is excluded. Differently: start with the industry benchmark for lane transit times instead of proving the brochure wrong lane by lane — same answer, faster.

Where this lands at Amazon: the cleanest "used data to overturn the room's explanation" story — and the model for how a savings claim should be built: method signed with Finance before the number, executed-only, then scaled site by site with the adoption cost measured in days. Broad Responsibility angle: one site's fix became 25 sites' instrument.

If challenged. "How is the $8M real?" — "Because the method was signed before the number existed: contracted guide rate minus paid rate, per shipment, executed only. The math — full derivation, step by step

Data quality (true vs reported). DQ = shipments with a valid promised date AND a valid actual
  date AND a resolvable
lane / all shipments . Reported 40% (a field-populated proxy); SQL-measured ~55% (populated and
  valid). Defect classes: missing rate, wrong-grain lane, carrier name variant (five spellings),
  brochure transit time posing as a promise. Target ~99% in 3 months.
On-time delivery. OTD = deliveries with actual ≤ promised / deliveries with a valid promise ;
  only valid-promise rows count — measuring OTD on brochure promises penalizes carriers for our
  error. OTD accuracy ~97% post-fix (automated vs manual sample agreement per lane cohort).
Promised-date rule. promised = ship_date + transit(lane_key) with lane_key = (ship-from,
  ship-to, mode, volume band, Incoterm, SLA) . Incoterm changes who owns the clock: DDP promise
  runs to the consignee's door; EXW to the carrier pickup — ~8% of lanes needed Incoterm in
    the
  key. No-hit  page a human, never a default.
Savings method (Finance-signed). Per shipment saving_s = (contracted guide rate_s −
  counterfactual paid rate_s) for executed shipments only (never "negotiated"); annual
    = Σ_s
  saving_s . Pilot: freight base ≈ $5M/yr × 10% = $500K/yr; network: 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes ≈
  $8M (say $8M, never $1M).
Manual effort. 8–10 hrs/wk → 1–2 hrs/wk (−80%); across a team of illustrative 4 people  ~32
  hrs/wk recovered ("illustrative, not program data").
Sanity. 55% true < 40% reported? No — 55% > 40%: the report under-counted usable rows
    because it
  keyed on a single field; the
SQL measure was both broader and stricter. DQ ≤ 100%; OTD computed only on the valid-promise
  denominator; savings only on executed rows.

Sensitivity — low / base / high

InputLowBaseHigh

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured numbers are five hundred thousand a year at Austria, ten percent of a five million freight base on a method Finance signed before I claimed a dollar, contracted rate minus paid rate on executed shipments only, and about eight million when the same rules file and one data-quality cycle went to twenty-five sites and four hundred plus lanes. The roll-up is simple: eight million over twenty-five sites is about three hundred twenty thousand a site, which implies roughly eighty million of freight at ten percent, and Austria at five hundred thousand sits above that average because it was a bigger site. On top I model about half a million a year of analyst capacity from cutting manual reconciliation from eight to ten hours a week to one or two at each site; the eight million is record, the half million is my model."

If challenged — the full answers.

Is the eight million really measured

It is the Finance-signed executed-only method applied at each site, not a projection; the assumption underneath is that the ten percent Austria rate held elsewhere, and the thing that firms it is the per-shipment ledger at each site, which is what Finance read.

The half million of labor is the softest

It assumes all twenty-five sites carried Austria's eight to ten hours a week, which I cannot prove; a one-week time log at three sites before their rules file went live would replace that assumption with data.

Why not count negotiated rate cuts too

Because negotiated but not executed is a promise, not a saving; the method counts only shipments that moved at the guide rate, and that discipline is why the carriers never disputed the scorecard or the dollars.

S11 — Flexport: Shipment Visibility launch — 7 → 68 behind graduation gates, the WBR, and pre-prod measurement

Amazon LPs: Deliver Results (primary) · Ownership · Customer Obsession (graduation criteria per wave) · Earn Trust (the discarded 99.4% week) · Hire and Develop (backup — wave teams grown into owners). Crosswalk: loop packet MAIN + S18 + S26 — carries the §1.3 conflicts (automation 40→90 here vs 60→97 there; WBR hours 1.5–2 → ~4 here vs 30→6 there). Resolve before the loop; inside this packet, this page is canon. Never "10×" — say 7 → 68 (9.7×). Never "$1.00 → $0.03" or "70% baseline."

Honesty label. Program owner across nine teams / ~100 people; the WBR format was copied from the LSA review, not invented; week counts for the build are illustrative; the ~$1.5M pre-prod saving is a separate canonical figure, never merged with CTS.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Flexport's visibility product had public targets — 68 monthly active customers, 170 MAU, 32K shipments — off seven pilots at $0.66 a container; Sales wanted the whole pool boarded at once, and leadership said the program was not showing value. Task. As program owner across nine teams / ~100 people, I owned the boarding path, the measurement system, and the pre-prod proof. Action. Discovery: ~50-container calibration entry bar per client; WBR input/output OS copied from an existing review, seven metrics frozen. Plan & Design: six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1; launch gate 100 real containers × 7 checks; sample size 300 containers, ±1.6% at 95% confidence. Build & Test: weekly ops audit; WBR by hand 1.5–2 then ~4 hrs/wk; pre-prod ingest, per-hop monitors, kill switches. Cutover: wave 1 (2 clients) → wave 2 (4 of 7) → wave 3 (20+) held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss from one bad source → full rollout. Hypercare: discarded a flattering 99.4% three-touch week; the VP unlocked the 68-client rollout two months early off the WBR. Lesson: board no faster than automation matures; a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion. Result. Primary metric: cost-to-serve per container — $0.66 → $0.03 (−95.5%) across customers 7 → 68 (9.7×) with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount; automation 40% → 90%; the ~$1.5M pre-prod saving is a separate record figure."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Senior leadership told me the visibility program wasn't demonstrating value at the anticipated rate — it would drop from #3 to #14 of 16 initiatives.

T — I had been reporting activity, not evidence. Funding and the 68-customer rollout hung on whether value was legible.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

Senior leadership told me the visibility program wasn't demonstrating value at the anticipated rate — it would drop from #3 to #14 of 16 initiatives — and funding and the 68-customer rollout hung on whether value was legible. The public targets: 68 monthly active customers, 170 MAU, 32K shipments, off seven pilots at $0.66 a container.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

Program owner across nine teams / ~100 people, owning the boarding path, the measurement system and the pre-prod proof. Challenges: Sales wanted the whole pool boarded at once, and my own reporting was the problem — activity, not evidence.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Boarding faster than automation matured — a bad first impression at scale, with rollbacks — and the program losing its funding slot. The critique was about evidence, not effort, and I accepted that first.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Deliver Results?

Because both public targets landed two months early with zero rollbacks: CTS $0.66 → $0.03 across customers 7 → 68 on the same ops team. Results delivered behind graduation gates, not by boarding faster than the automation could carry.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Deliver Results?

S18 — 8 months, both hubs, gate cleared — is the backup; S2's comingle launch and S6's three-months-to-live are the thirds.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Flexport — recent, though not current role. The current-role Deliver Results record is S2: the comingle launch, zero incidents, +15% signed.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (canonical: WBR copied from the LSA review — Mon–Sun week, five-week window, WoW = (B−A)/A, ±5% green/red, every input tagged to an output; seven frozen metrics; run by hand 1.5–2 hrs/wk then ~4 for over a year; wave roster W1 Q4'21 (2 clients) → W2 Q1'22 (4 of a 7-pool) → W3 Q2'22 (20+, held on ETA accuracy) → full H2'22):

  1. Discovery — wrote the honest diagnosis (reporting activity, not evidence); inventoried what leadership actually decides on (rollout pace, funding); chose the LSA WBR as the pattern to copy rather than invent. Exit bar: leadership agrees on the questions the review must answer weekly.
  2. Plan & Design — froze seven metric definitions (coverage auto, coverage w/ human, tickets, tickets per active container, 4PL SLA %, 3PL SLA %, delta); input→output tagging; the ±5% WoW rule; the 15-day inactive rule as the honest denominator; the four-system pull procedure; six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1. Exit bar: definitions signed by the owners of each input; first Monday deck dry-run.
  3. Build & Test — built the deck manually every week (1.5–2 hrs → ~4); pre-decomposed every red with an owner and a dated fix; enforced the data-honesty rule (a truncated week is a data miss, not a result); pre-prod: 300-container measurement, per-hop monitors, kill switches. Exit bar: two consecutive Mondays where every red arrives with an owner and the number verifies the following week.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — the Monday WBR became the operating forum, with the rollout decision taken in the review; waves boarded only through the six graduation criteria; wave 3 held in public on ETA accuracy traced to one bad source. Exit bar: VP unlocks the 68-client rollout two months early.
  5. Hypercare / Close — handed the manual pull to the Snowflake pipeline (S16) with the definitions in code; left the critic steering by the numbers. Exit bar: automation 40% → 90%; CTS $0.66 → $0.03; the same critic uses the review to steer.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

The WBR arithmetic — Mon–Sun weeks, a five-week window, WoW = (B−A)/A at ±5% red/green, every input tagged to an output, honest denominators via the 15-day inactive rule — plus the six graduation criteria and the 300-container pre-prod measurement at ±1.6% at 95% confidence. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

Program owner across nine teams / ~100 people. The WBR format was copied from the LSA review, not invented — copying a trusted format was the point — and I ran it by hand every Monday for over a year, 1.5–2 hours and then about 4.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Making value legible: seven frozen metric definitions signed by their owners, input→output tagging, and the data-honesty rule — including discarding a flattering truncated 99.4% week and printing a data miss instead. The VP unlocked the 68-client rollout two months early off those numbers.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Sales wanting the whole pool boarded at once — answered with six graduation criteria published before wave 1; wave 3 held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss traced to one bad source — the rule fired, and the held customers became the cleanest wave. Bus factor one on the pull — retired by handing it to the Snowflake pipeline (S16).

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the honest diagnosis, the frozen definitions, the WBR built by hand every week, the graduation criteria, the hold decisions and the pre-prod measurement design. Theirs: nine teams built and operated; each input owner signed the definition they would be red against; the VP took the rollout decisions in the review.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Board no faster than automation matures — waves only through the six graduation criteria, every red pre-decomposed with an owner and a dated fix. Buy-in: leadership agreed the questions the review must answer weekly, and the Monday WBR became the operating forum where the rollout decision was taken.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

The “board everything now” push — challenged with published gates; and the temptation to print the 99.4% week — three touches is not a week, and a number that cannot survive the next Monday teaches the VP the wrong thing.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: CTS $0.66 → $0.03 (−95.5%); customers 7 → 68 (9.7×), zero rollbacks; accuracy 60% → 90%; both public targets two months early.

  • $ — CTS $0.66 → $0.03 per container (record); the ~$1.5M pre-prod measurement saving is a separate record figure, never merged with CTS; direct CTS dollars ≈$50K/yr modeled on an illustrative container count.
  • # — Customers 7 → 68 (9.7×) with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount; targets 68 customers / 170 MAU / 32K shipments (record).
  • % — CTS −95.5%; automation 40% → 90%; accuracy 60% → 90% (record).
  • Time — Both public targets hit two months early; the rollout unlocked two months early off the WBR (record).
  • Customer — Six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1 — including CSAT ≥ consignee average; wave 3 held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss, and the held customers became the cleanest wave (record).
  • Quality — A flattering truncated 99.4% week discarded and printed as a data miss; seven metric definitions frozen and later moved into code (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

CTS −95.5% across 7 → 68 with zero rollbacks is the unit-economics proof that scale came without headcount; automation 40 → 90% is the mechanism; two months early is the time result. The ~$1.5M pre-prod saving matters too — kept separate because it is a separate record.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the $0.66 → $0.03 is cost-to-serve per container, before vs after, and the direct dollars are honestly small: ≈$50K/yr modeled on an illustrative container count. The value is scale without headcount, and the ~$1.5M pre-prod figure is a separate record I never merge with CTS.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

New metrics traded for stability — a parking lot, the seven kept; and boarding speed traded for a rollout that never rolled back — wave 3 held publicly rather than take a bad first impression at scale.

Probing — I'm concerned about your containers-per-shipment assumption — tell me more.

It is a guess and I said so — the record gives me thirty-two thousand shipments, not containers; at one and a half to four containers per shipment the saving runs thirty to eighty thousand. I would firm it up by pulling the active-container count straight from the 15-day active rule in the WBR.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Board no faster than automation matures; a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion. Differently: start the WBR at kickoff, not after the critique.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

A WBR culture story told to the company that invented the WBR — copy the trusted format, freeze definitions, tag inputs to outputs, and let the review make the rollout decision; the graduation criteria are Amazon-shaped launch gates, published before the wave and held in public.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The public targets were 68 customers, 170 MAU and 32K shipments — both targets hit two months early, 7 → 68 with zero rollbacks, automation 40 → 90%, and the same critic who said value wasn't legible steering by the review.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When leadership had told me what they actually decide on — rollout pace and funding — and agreed the questions the review must answer weekly. Their critique was about evidence, not effort, and I accepted that first.

Why copy a format instead of designing one?

The LSA review already had leadership's trust — five-week window, WoW at ±5%, inputs tagged to outputs. Inventing a format would have spent credibility I didn't have.

Plan & Design — Who wrote the metric definitions and who signed?

I wrote the seven; the owner of each input — Data Services, Support, GTM — signed the definition they would be red against. Frozen after that; new metrics out of scope.

What was in your risk register?

Measurement-caused reds, a flattering wrong number, and bus factor one. Mitigations: decompose before the meeting, print data misses honestly, automate the pull — which became S16.

How did you handle the objection to freezing the list?

Stability over coverage — a number that changes definition can't steer a rollout. I offered a parking lot for new metrics and kept the seven.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a red week?

Decomposed before the meeting, an owner named, a dated fix, and the same number verified the following Monday. A red without an owner was my miss, not theirs.

Describe a hard call in the build.

Discarding a truncated 99.4% week. It flattered the program and would have taught the VP a number that couldn't survive the next Monday. I printed a data miss.

Cutover — When did the review become "live"?

The Monday the VP took the rollout decision in the room — unlocking the 68-client rollout two months early off the numbers, not a narrative.

Which wave did the review hold and how did Sales take it?

Wave 3, on ETA accuracy traced to one bad source. Sales had the six graduation criteria before wave 1, so the hold was the rule firing — and the customers we held became the cleanest wave.

Hypercare — How did you know the review would outlive your hands?

When the pipeline (S16) produced the same seven numbers within 1% for consecutive weeks and the critic who started this steered by them.

What did you keep doing after the review was credible?

Running it by hand every Monday for over a year — 1.5–2 hours, then about 4 — until the pipeline matched within 1%. A review that skips a week teaches leadership it can be skipped.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

New metrics for stability, and boarding speed for a rollout that never rolled back — held wave 3 publicly rather than take a bad first impression at scale. Escalated nothing outside the review — the review was the escalation forum, with the VP deciding in it every Monday.

Why discard 99.4%? · Do differently?

Three touches is not a week; printing it would have taught the VP a number that couldn't survive the next Monday. Differently: start the WBR at kickoff, not after the critique.

Where this lands at Amazon: this is a WBR culture story told to the company that invented the WBR — the respect move is that I copied a trusted format rather than inventing one, froze definitions, tagged inputs to outputs, and let the review make the rollout decision. The graduation criteria are Amazon-shaped launch gates: published before the wave, held in public when they fired.

Business impact model — KPI tree, the wave-machine math

Program value legible to leadership (funding + 68-client rollout)
+- Cost-to-serve per container $0.66 -> $0.03 (record)
|    +- Human interactions/container 3.7 -> 0.17 (record cross-walk: $0.18/interaction)
|    +- Automation coverage 40% -> 90% (record)
|         (loop) fewer touches -> more calibration data -> better ETA/DQ ->
|                fewer exceptions -> fewer touches
+- Customers boarded 7 -> 68 (9.7x), zero rollbacks (record)
|    +- Six graduation criteria published before W1 (gate)
|    +- W3 held publicly on ETA accuracy -> cleanest wave
+- Time-to-value: rollout unlocked 2 months early off the WBR (record)
+- Pre-prod measurement path ~$1.5M saved (record, SEPARATE canonical)
Wave-machine math: entry bar ~50 containers calibration per client (not a wave gate)
Six graduation criteria (ALL must pass): DQ >= 90% vs source of truth ·
  ETA/milestone accuracy >= 80% · verification SLA 90% (review <24h, max 48h) ·
  engagement >= 100 containers + 5 key features · stability · CSAT >= consignee average
Launch gate before first paying customer: 100 real containers x 7 checks;
  load proof 500 containers / 20 min / 99.6%
CTS cross-walk: $0.66/$0.18 ~ 3.7 residual interactions -> $0.03/$0.18 ~ 0.17
Pre-prod: n = 300 containers, expected accuracy 98% -> +/-1.6% at 95% CI;
  E2E accuracy 85% -> ~98%; rollout 1.5 months vs 6; ~$1.5M saved (separate canonical)
WBR instrument: 10,289 events; event-level human 19.4%; WoW = (B-A)/A; +/-5% red/green

  the value is scale without headcount, not the direct dollars

The point of −95.5% was never the direct dollars; it's that 7 customers became 68 with zero added ops headcount. The modeled figure is honest because the volume is; the scaling is the record." "Why ±1.6%?" — "n = 300 at an expected 98% accuracy gives a 95% confidence interval of about ±1.6 points — big enough to certify the gate, small enough to afford. That measurement path cut rollout from 6 months to 1.5 and carries the separate ~$1.5M record."

The math — full derivation, step by step

WBR arithmetic (copied from the LSA WBR pattern, not invented). Week = Mon–Sun; show the most
  recent 5 weeks plus monthly roll-ups. Week-over-week WoW = (B − A) / A where A = prior week, B
  = current; green/red at ±5% relative; every input metric tagged to the output line it may move
  ("RollUp").
Seven frozen metrics. coverage_auto = containers tracked with no human touch / active
  containers; coverage_with_human = tracked incl. human review / active; tickets = support
  tickets; tickets_per_active_container = tickets / active containers; 4PL_SLA% and 3PL_SLA% =
  milestones within SLA / milestones due; delta = 4PL − 3PL.
Honest denominators. Active container = touched within 15 days (the inactive rule) — a container
  that stops moving stops inflating coverage.
Cost-to-serve per container. CTS = (human interactions per container × loaded $/interaction) ;
  canonical $0.66 → $0.03  (0.03 − 0.66)/0.66 = −95.5% . Cross-walk to the platform's $0.18
    per
  eliminated interaction: $0.66/$0.18 ≈ 3.7 residual human interactions per container before;
  $0.03/$0.18 ≈ 0.17 after.
Automation grains (all real, different instruments). Event grain: 1,991/10,288 = 19.4% human;
  weekly stock automation 40.2% (13 Dec) · 40.0% (17 Jan) · 21.9% (31 Jan red week) · 69.2% (21
  Feb, active 287 → 546); ticket grain 538 auto / 456 human = 54.1%. Headline: automation 40% →
  90%; accuracy 60% → 90%. Why 99.4% was discarded. Three touches in a week  automation = 1 −
  h/N with N tiny; a 95% CI on N = 3 is wider than the metric ("Wilson interval ≈ [0.44,
    1.0]" —
  illustrative math on the real decision). A number that cannot survive the next Monday teaches
  the VP the wrong thing. Illustrative WoW ("not program data" for these two values). A
    = 40.0%,
  B = 21.9%  WoW = −45%  red; root cause a carrier format change (measurement), not operators.
Sanity. Rates ≤ 100%; WoW undefined if A = 0 (rule: show "n/a"); CTS reduction and
    automation
  rise move together.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Honestly, the cost-to-serve number is a unit-economics result, not a big dollar number: ninety-five percent off per container is measured, and at the thirty-two-thousand-shipment target that is only about fifty thousand dollars a year of direct cost, which is my model on an illustrative container count. What it bought is scale: sixty-eight customers boarded on the same ops team with zero rollbacks, about two and a half people we never had to hire, and a rollout unlocked two months early, which I model at roughly a quarter million of one-time revenue pull-forward. The million and a half on the pre-prod measurement path is a separate record figure and I keep it separate. So: the ninety-five percent and the seven-to-sixty-eight are record; every dollar figure except the one-point-five is my model."

If challenged — the full answers.

Your containers-per-shipment is a guess.

It is, and I said so. The record gives me thirty-two thousand shipments, not containers; I used two and a half FCL containers per shipment, and at one and a half to four the saving runs thirty to eighty thousand. I would firm it up by pulling the active-container count straight from the fifteen-day active rule in the WBR, which is the same denominator the review already used.

Fifty thousand a year is small — why lead with it?

Because it is honest, and because the point was never the dollars, it was boarding nine point seven times the customers without nine point seven times the ops headcount. The hardest part of that claim is the one-minute-per-interaction assumption behind the two-and-a-half FTE; I would measure it from the ticket-grain automation data, five hundred thirty-eight auto versus four hundred fifty-six human.

Where does the million and a half come from, then?

That is the pre-prod measurement path, a separate canonical: three hundred containers at plus or minus one point six percent let us prove the product in six weeks instead of six months. It is a record figure, it is not cost-to-serve, and I never add it to the fifty thousand. To firm it up I would want the finance sign-off memo that priced the four and a half months saved.

S12 — Flexport: FF App export launch — operator-first vs partner-first, CTS −25% vs −50%, the 8-week rebuild/reuse rule

Honesty label. The roadmap was my manager's; I owned the sequencing recommendation. Engineering produced the re-estimates; the ~$5M is the record's cost-avoidance figure, said once; the run-rate decomposition is my model.
60-second pitch

"Situation. The Freight Forwarding App export roadmap was operator-first, 18 months, already socialized, and my manager owned it; baselines 339.5 min/shipment and 2,200 operator interactions per departed shipment. Task. With no authority over the roadmap, re-sequence it on cost-to-serve evidence. Action. Discovery: time study × loaded rates.
