SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The customer's transportation team ran a carrier bid twice a year: hundreds of lanes, dozens of carrier rate files, constraints like incumbency floors and volume commitments — six weeks per cycle, mostly reconciliation, because origin/destination naming and mode codes differed per carrier. Everyone assumed it was an optimization problem.

T — Own the requirements, the constraint rules and the parallel run; transportation, procurement and engineering did not report to me.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

The customer's transportation team ran a carrier bid twice a year — hundreds of lanes, dozens of carrier rate files, constraints like incumbency floors and volume commitments — at six weeks per cycle, mostly reconciliation, because origin/destination naming and mode codes differed per carrier. The goal: a defensible award in half the cycle.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

I owned the requirements, the constraint rules and the parallel run — not the award; transportation, procurement and engineering did not report to me. The challenge: everyone assumed it was an optimization problem, and the diagnosis had to contradict the room.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Six weeks of analyst time, twice a year, spent reconciling lane names and mode codes instead of analyzing — and the proposed cure, a black-box solver, would have produced awards nobody could defend to an incumbent carrier.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Invent and Simplify?

Because the simplification beat the fancier tool: a lane master plus constraints written one per rule, with a <5% back-test gate against the last two awards before any optimizer ran. The invention was the diagnosis — a time audit that showed the problem class was reconciliation, not optimization.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Invent and Simplify?

S16 — four systems into one pipeline — is the backup; S3's de-aggregation design is the third, and the §3 E-designs carry the same muscle for design prompts.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. The more recent same-LP records are S16 at Flexport and S3's de-aggregation design in my current role.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery (one full bid cycle) — sat through a whole cycle and built the time audit by activity: the six weeks went to reconciling lane names and mode codes, not analysis; wrote the problem-class statement. Exit bar: Transportation agrees the cost is reconciliation. (Procurement not yet in the room — the miss.)
  2. Plan & Design — the lane-master spec (origin/destination/mode normalization); constraints written one per rule (volume commitments, incumbency floors, mode rules); the validation gate (<5% mismatch vs the last two awards); scenario comparison required as first-class output; the black-box solver rejected in writing. Exit bar: rules signed by Transportation; gate published before any optimizer run.
  3. Build & Test — held engineering to the spec; back-test against the two historical awards; the 10-day stale-data banner required. Exit bar: mismatch <5% against both awards; scenarios explainable to an incumbent.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (pilot region) — the parallel run — tool vs manual on one region's real bid; acceptance = the tool matches their defensible choices and beats cycle time. Exit bar: pilot region finished 3 weeks early; award defensible in carrier meetings.
  5. Hypercare / Close → adoption — weekly rate-file refresh cadence; Procurement brought into the constraint definitions (late — my miss); the tool handed over as a standing global tool. Exit bar: other regions adopted without a mandate; cycle ~3 weeks; procurement in discovery for the next cycle.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

The constraint-aware bid model — lane-carrier award shares minimizing rate × volume subject to coverage, volume commitments, incumbency floors and mode eligibility — with the scenario delta (constrained cost minus all-cheapest) shown as a number, and the <5% historical-mismatch gate validating the rules against defensible past decisions. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

I owned the requirements, the constraint rules and the parallel run — not the award; engineering built the model to the rules; transportation and procurement ran the award. Procurement joining late is my named miss.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Sitting through a full bid cycle logging hours — the time audit that contradicted the room — and the lane master plus explicit rules that turned six weeks of reconciliation into configuration. Explainability was the spec.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

Engineering wanted a cheapest-lane solver — answered by making scenario comparison a first-class output and gating the optimizer behind the <5% back-test. Constraint semantics differing across functions — “incumbency floor” meant different things to transportation and the category manager — fired because procurement wasn't in the room: my named miss.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the time audit, the problem-class statement, the lane-master spec, the constraint rules, the validation gate and the parallel-run design. Theirs: engineering built the model; the regional team's judgment was the acceptance test; transportation and procurement ran the award.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

The back-test read-out classified every mismatch against two historical awards — naming, mode code, constraint semantics — in one session, with owners. Buy-in: rules signed by Transportation, the gate published before any optimizer run, and adoption by envy — other regions came on without a mandate after the pilot finished three weeks early.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

The room's framing — “it's an optimization problem” — overturned by the hour log; the black-box solver, rejected in writing; and the dollar figure on the award, refused: transportation and procurement ran it.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: cycle 6 weeks → ~3 (~50%); pilot 3 weeks early; adopted without a mandate. ≈$190K/yr modeled analyst hours returned; no dollar on the award.

  • $ — ≈$190K/yr modeled analyst time returned (≈2,200 hrs/yr on illustrative team and region counts; range $35–420K); no dollar on the award — Transportation and Procurement ran it, and never savings on the award.
  • # — Hundreds of lanes, dozens of carrier rate files, twice a year; constraints written one per rule (volume commitments, incumbency floors, mode rules) (record).
  • % — Back-test mismatch <5% vs the last two awards before the optimizer could run — the published gate (record); cycle time down ~50%.
  • Time — Carrier-bid cycle 6 weeks → ~3; pilot region finished 3 weeks early; weekly rate-file refresh with a stale banner past 10 days (record).
  • Customer — Scenario comparison as a first-class output — the award defensible in carrier meetings; other regions adopted without a mandate (record).
  • Quality — The lane master normalized origin/destination/mode naming — the time audit showed reconciliation, not optimization, ate the six weeks; constraint definitions now written with procurement in the room (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Cycle 6 weeks → ~3 is the record, and the pilot finishing three weeks early is what drove adoption without a mandate. The <5% back-test gate is the quality result: an award that survives the incumbent meeting.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

There are no dollars on the award at all — deliberately: transportation and procurement ran it, and the record has no figure. The only dollars I model are ≈$190K/yr of analyst time returned, on illustrative team and region counts, range $35–420K.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

The trade was mine: a slower first cycle to write and validate constraints, for a tool that halved every cycle after. Nothing was traded on the bid itself.

Probing — I'm concerned about the three-analysts, three-regions assumption — tell me more.

They are guesses, which is why the range runs from about $35K to about $420K. I'd replace them with the actual bid roster per region and the hour log I kept in discovery — the same audit that found the problem class.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Diagnose the problem class from a time audit before building anything. Differently: procurement in discovery on day one — the constraint-semantics risk fired because they weren't.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Invent and Simplify with the diagnosis first — a time audit that contradicted the room, then a simplification (one lane master plus explicit rules) that beat the fancier tool; refusing the dollar I can't defend is what makes the ones I do defend credible.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The cycle was six weeks; it runs about three, twice a year, with the pilot region three weeks early, the award defensible in carrier meetings, and the mismatch gate below 5% against both historical awards.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (12). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the time audit from a full cycle showed the six weeks were reconciliation — lane names and mode codes differing per carrier — not analysis, and transportation agreed. It was not fully done: procurement was missing, which I learned later.

Why sit through a whole cycle instead of interviewing?

Interviews tell you what people think they do. Logging hours told me where the time actually went, and the answer contradicted the room.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Constraint semantics differing across functions, a black-box award, and stale rates. The first one fired — "incumbency floor" meant different things to transportation and the category manager — because I hadn't put procurement in the room.

How did you handle engineering objecting to the design?

They wanted a cheapest-lane solver. I made scenario comparison a first-class output and required <5% mismatch against past awards before the optimizer could run. Explainability was the spec.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for the model?

<5% mismatch against the last two award cycles, every scenario explainable in one sentence, and a stale-data banner past 10 days. Below that bar the optimizer did not run.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The back-test read-out: every mismatch against two historical awards classified — naming, mode code, constraint semantics — in one session, with owners. That is where most of the reconciliation logic got fixed.

Cutover — What was your go criterion for the pilot?

The tool matched the regional team's defensible choices on their real bid and beat the cycle time. Their judgment was the acceptance test, not mine.

What was the rollback?

The manual process ran in parallel through the pilot. If the tool had disagreed indefensibly, they would have awarded by hand — never needed.

Hypercare — How did other regions come on?

Without a mandate — the pilot finished three weeks early and the tool carried each region's own constraints as rules. Adoption by envy; the refresh cadence kept it honest.

What kept the tool honest after the pilot?

The weekly rate-file refresh with the stale banner past 10 days, and constraint definitions now written with procurement in the room. A model with stale rates or unowned rules decays into a spreadsheet with a logo.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you refuse?

Nowhere on the bid itself. The trade was mine: a slower first cycle to write and validate constraints, for a tool that halved every cycle after. Refused: a black-box solver — and a dollar figure; transportation and procurement ran the award.

What did it save? · Do differently?

I don't have a dollar figure and won't invent one; the cycle halved and the pilot finished three weeks early — the savings claim belonged to the negotiation. Differently: procurement in discovery on day one.

Where this lands at Amazon: Invent and Simplify with the diagnosis first — a time audit that contradicted the room ("it's an optimization problem") and a simplification (one lane master + explicit rules) that beat the fancier tool. The refusal to claim the award dollar is the credibility move interviewers remember.

The math — full derivation, step by step

Bid model (constraint-aware; engineering built it to the rules). Decision variable x_{l,c} ∈
  [0,1] = share of lane l awarded to carrier c. min Σ_l Σ_c rate_{l,c} × volume_l × x_{l,c}
  subject to: Σ_c x_{l,c} = 1 ∀ l (every lane covered); volume commitments Σ_l volume_l ×
  x_{l,c} ≥ V_min,c for committed carriers; incumbency floors x_{l,inc(l)} ≥ f_l where the rule
  applies; mode rules x_{l,c} = 0 if carrier c not eligible for mode(l). Scenario output: Δcost
  = cost(all-cheapest) − cost(constrained) = the price of service/relationship risk, shown as a
  number.
Historical mismatch gate. mismatch = lanes where the model's award ≠ the actual award in the
  last two cycles / lanes evaluated ; the optimizer runs only if mismatch < 5% . The gate
  validates the rules (constraints) against defensible past decisions, not the prices.
Time audit. Cycle = 6 weeks; measured composition (my hour log): reconciliation of carrier files
  (naming, mode codes) dominated; analysis was the minority. Post: ~3 weeks (~50%); pilot region
  3 weeks early. Illustrative worked example ("not program data"). Three lanes, two
    carriers.
  Lane volumes 100/200/300 loads; rates (A,B): L1 (900, 950), L2 (1,100, 1,000), L3 (800, 850).
  All-cheapest: L1→A, L2→B, L3→A  90,000 + 200,000 + 240,000 = $530,000.
Constraint: B has a volume commitment ≥ 250 loads  must also take ≥ 50 loads of L1 or L3;
  cheapest way: 50 loads of L3 to B at +$50 each = +$2,500  $532,500. Scenario line:
    "honoring
  B's commitment costs $2,500 (0.47%)." The award survives the room because the delta is
  explicit.
Stale-data banner. age = today − rate_file_date ; banner if age > 10 days ; weekly refresh.
Sanity. Σ shares = 1 per lane; constrained cost ≥ unconstrained cost always; mismatch gate on
  rules never admits an unexplainable award.

60-second pitch

"Situation. Two DCs on a legacy WMS, no documentation, an eight-month deadline, ~$1.8M budget, a $20M ROI pipeline gated behind go-live — about $2.5M a month of slip; I had never run a WMS migration. SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Two DCs on a legacy WMS; zero inherited documentation; a hard eight-month deadline with a $20M ROI pipeline of four programs gated behind go-live (~$2.5M/month of slip); budget ~$1.8M; and I had never run a WMS migration. Inventory accuracy across two DCs could not wobble at cutover.

T — Own the full lifecycle — discovery charter, design sign-off, the ERP ↔ WMS ↔ 3PL ↔ carrier interface inventory, UAT, cutover, hypercare — accountable to the sponsor and Finance.

Probing — Why is this important? What was the goal?

Two DCs on a legacy WMS with zero inherited documentation, a hard eight-month deadline, a ~$1.8M budget, and a $20M ROI pipeline of four programs gated behind go-live — about $2.5M a month of slip. The goal, written in the charter in week one: on the date, inside a published reconciliation tolerance, zero accuracy incidents.

Probing — What was the initial scope of the project? What were the challenges?

Full lifecycle — discovery charter, design sign-off, the ERP ↔ WMS ↔ 3PL ↔ carrier interface inventory, UAT, cutover, hypercare — accountable to the sponsor and Finance. The honest challenge: I had never run a WMS migration, and inventory accuracy across two DCs could not wobble at cutover.

Probing — What were the risks and potential consequences if nothing happened?

Undocumented interface surprises, conversion mismatch above tolerance, and 3PL/carrier freeze non-compliance — each with a trigger, a data-first mitigation and an owner. The one I missed: RF conversion sat on the log as a task, not a risk.

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

Because the standard was published before it could be met: the 0.5% tolerance signed with Finance before mock 1, so the number belonged to Finance and me before the test. Mock 1's 7% was the price; mock 3's 0.3% was worth it — and the tolerance was never renegotiated.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

S2 — the robotics gate held at 94 vs 98 — is this LP's primary; S9's ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, held twice, is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is a Sephora record, and its discipline is what the current UDC program reuses — S2's three mock cutovers run against the same 0.5% tolerance. S2 is the most recent same-LP record.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (team 14 core + 6 DC Ops; month markers illustrative unless stated):

  1. Discovery (month 1) — sponsor charter written in week one (objectives, assumptions, success criteria); phase entrance/exit criteria; interface inventory v0 from message logs; the systems-impacted table with LOE per owner. Exit bar: charter signed by sponsor and Finance; every interface has a named owner; I could walk receive → pick → ship unaided.
  2. Plan & Design (months 2–3) — design sign-off pack; interface inventory v1 with a test cell per contract; the conversion approach (masters → open orders → on-hand → in-transit as its own batch); the mapping-table standard (data, not code); the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock 1. Exit bar: council sign-off; tolerance signed; stories slotted by business criticality × effort.
  3. Build & Test (months 4–7) — build tracking; the QA demo schedule to DC Ops; the UAT entrance memo (function testing done, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod + perf ready); three mock cutovers with written root causes; after the RF miss, the training plan and device readiness table. Exit bar: mock 3 ≤ 0.5% (0.3%) → written go-live recommendation; training sign-off (slipped 1.5 weeks, date held).
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (month 8) — the cutover runbook; the 72-hour transfer freeze; conversion batches; post-load reconciliation; the legacy read-only switch; the go/no-go call. When ~100 of ~400 RF devices failed the conversion script, I invoked the rehearsed paper-pick fallback rather than roll back. Exit bar: post-load mismatch inside tolerance; ship confirms posting; zero accuracy incidents.
  5. Hypercare / Close (4 weeks planned) — blockers/day read daily; the 30-day failback watch; budget closed; the exit memo with the single-point-dependency lesson. Exit bar: <2 blockers/day sustained → exit week 3; ~3% under budget; pipeline programs started the next month.

Probing — Deep probe: functional expertise / core competency.

The reconciliation math — aggregate mismatch M = Σ|Δ| / Σ ERP on-hand, gated at 0.5% with a per-SKU band — the conversion order (masters → open orders → on-hand → in-transit as its own batch), and the 72-hour freeze sized to exceed max lane transit so the in-transit set was static. The phase-by-phase bank below and the full derivation under RESULTS carry the depth.

Probing — Were you the key driver or project owner?

Full lifecycle owner, accountable to sponsor and Finance — I wrote the charter in week one and learned the floor and the vendor docs in month one; the team was 14 core plus 6 DC Ops. The RF miss is mine: I scored the task by effort when impact was what mattered.

Probing — What was your biggest contribution? What unique value did you bring?

Writing the success criteria before anyone asked — the date, the tolerance, zero accuracy incidents, the numeric hypercare exit — and the three-mock discipline read by cause class: 7% → 1.8% → 0.3%, each mock removing a named defect category.

Probing — What were the most significant obstacles you faced? How did you overcome them?

No documentation — the interface inventory was built from message logs, every interface with a named owner and a test cell. My own inexperience — the floor walked unaided by the end of month one. And ~100 of ~400 RF devices failing at cutover — answered by the rehearsed paper-pick fallback, not a rollback, with the training criterion slipping 1.5 weeks while the date held.

Challenge — What did you do specifically, versus the team?

Mine: the charter, the entrance/exit criteria, the interface inventory, the conversion approach, the mapping-table standard (data, not code), the tolerance, the go/no-go recommendation and the fallback call. Theirs: 14 core plus 6 DC Ops built, converted, tested and ran the floor; Finance co-owned the tolerance.

Challenge — How did you set priorities and get buy-in?

Stories slotted by business criticality × effort; UAT entrance criteria written in the charter — function testing done, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod and perf ready; QA could fail a build and DC Ops confirmed because QA had demoed during build. Buy-in: sponsor and Finance signed the charter week one, so nobody negotiated the exit later.

Challenge — What decisions did you challenge? Why? How did you influence the right outcome?

Rolling back when the RF devices failed — I challenged it because inventory was inside tolerance and the failure was device conversion, not data: the rehearsed fallback fired instead, and leadership heard it the same day with the slip priced and my name on the cause.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: mock variance 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% vs the 0.5% tolerance; on time; ~3% under ~$1.8M (~$54K); the $20M pipeline unblocked the next month.

  • $ — ~3% under the ~$1.8M budget (~$54K, record); the $20M ROI pipeline unblocked the next month — ~$2.5M/month of slip avoided, not banked; ≈$45K modeled paper-pick fallback cost paid deliberately to hold the date.
  • # — ~100 of ~400 RF devices failed the conversion script — the rehearsed paper-pick fallback fired; team 14 core + 6 DC Ops across two DCs (record).
  • % — Mock variance 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% against the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock 1 (record).
  • Time — Eight-month deadline held; 72-hour in-transit freeze; legacy read-only for 30 days; training slipped 1.5 weeks while the date did not move; hypercare exited week 3 of 4 (record).
  • Customer — Zero accuracy incidents at cutover; ship confirms posting; post-load mismatch inside tolerance (record exit bars).
  • Quality — Mocks read by cause class, each removing a named defect category — units of measure, location names, order-status vocabularies, then in-transit timing; failback rehearsed, never used (record).

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

The mock trajectory against the published tolerance — 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% vs 0.5% — is the record that made “go” a written recommendation rather than confidence in the room; on time, ~3% under budget and the $20M pipeline unblocked the next month are what the sponsor and Finance measured.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the ~$54K is ~3% under the ~$1.8M budget, and the ~$2.5M/month is slip exposure avoided, not banked; the pipeline's value belonged to the four programs it gated.

Probing — What trade-offs did you have to make? (quality, cost, time)

Sequence, not scope: training slipped 1.5 weeks so the go-live date held, told to leadership the same day with the slip priced; and ≈$45K of modeled paper-pick productivity was paid deliberately to protect the critical path.

Probing — I'm concerned about the $875K avoided-slip figure — tell me more.

It is record arithmetic on a slip that did not happen, so I never bank it — it is the price that made the fallback the obvious call. What I'd measure is the pipeline's first program start date against plan, which was the next month.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Any single-point dependency in front of an exit criterion gets a what-if plan, regardless of estimated effort. Differently: dual-path device conversion on day one, and a device model × OS × script pass/fail table before training week.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Cutover discipline in its most literal form — a tolerance published with Finance before the first rehearsal, mocks read by cause class rather than headline, a freeze, a tested failback, and a numeric hypercare exit nobody can negotiate.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The charter asked for the date, the tolerance and zero accuracy incidents — delivered on time, 0.3% against 0.5%, zero accuracy incidents, ~3% under budget, and hypercare exited week 3 of 4 under the charter's own blockers-per-day number.

ACTION DEEP-PROBE BANK — PHASE Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — You had never run a WMS migration; how did you know discovery was done?

When I could walk receive → put-away → pick → ship on the floor unaided and every interface I saw in the message logs had a row, an owner and a test cell. That took month one; the charter had been written week one.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote them in week one — on the date, inside a published reconciliation tolerance, zero accuracy incidents, hypercare exit under two blockers a day — and the sponsor and Finance signed the charter.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Undocumented interface surprises, conversion mismatch above tolerance, and 3PL/carrier freeze non-compliance — each with a trigger, a data-first mitigation and an owner. RF conversion was on the log as a task, not a risk; that was my miss.

Why publish the tolerance before you knew you could hit it?

So the number belonged to Finance and me before the test, not after. Mock 1's 7% was the price; mock 3's 0.3% was worth it.

Build & Test — How were UAT entrance criteria set and who could fail a build?

Function testing done, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod and perf ready — written in the charter. QA could fail a build; DC Ops confirmed in UAT because QA had demoed to them during build.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The three mocks. Everyone in one room reading the mismatch by cause class — units of measure, location drift, status vocabularies, then in-transit timing. Nothing left the room without a class, an owner and a data fix.

How did you read a mock?

Never by the headline number alone. Each delta got a root-cause class; if the total shrank without a named cause, the mock was not evidence. 7 → 1.8 → 0.3 each removed a category.

Cutover — What made you say go?

A written recommendation off mock 3 at 0.3% against 0.5%, the freeze agreed in writing, failback tested, training signed off — 1.5 weeks late, inside the window.

What were your rollback triggers and did any fire?

Post-go-live mismatch above 0.5% for two days, or ship-confirm posting failing beyond two hours — rollback per interface, the legacy system read-only for 30 days as the failback. Rehearsed; never used. The paper-pick contingency, also rehearsed, did fire — for the RF miss.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over?

Blockers per day under two, sustained — exited week 3 of 4. The number was in the charter before go-live, so nobody negotiated the exit.

What did you hand over at hypercare exit?

The interface inventory with owners and test cells, the mapping tables owned by DC Ops and ERP, the failback plan still armed for the rest of the 30 days, and the device readiness table. The RF lesson went into the sponsor read-out with my name on it.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

Nowhere on scope — I traded sequence: training slipped 1.5 weeks so the go-live date held, and I told leadership the same day with the slip priced. Escalated the RF miss as a priced decision with my name on the cause. The rule I left: any single-point dependency in front of an exit criterion gets a what-if plan, regardless of size.

You'd never run a WMS migration — why you? · Do differently?

I wrote the success criteria before anyone asked and learned the floor and the vendor docs in month one; the mocks were where I proved it. Differently: dual-path device conversion on day one; a device model × OS × script pass/fail table before training week.

Where this lands at Amazon: the cutover discipline in its most literal form — a tolerance published with Finance before the first rehearsal, mocks read by cause class rather than headline, a freeze, a tested failback, and a numeric hypercare exit nobody can negotiate. The RF miss is the honest beat: a rehearsed contingency fired, the date held, and the lesson became a rule.

The math — full derivation, step by step

Reconciliation tolerance. Per SKU-location j: Δ_j = WMS_onhand_j − ERP_onhand_j . Aggregate
  mismatch M = Σ_j |Δ_j| / Σ_j ERP_onhand_j ; gate M ≤ 0.5% plus a per-SKU band ( |Δ_j| / ERP_j
  ≤ b or |Δ_j| ≤ k units for low-count SKUs). Canonical trajectory: mock 1 = 7% → mock 2 = 1.8%
  → mock 3 = 0.3% vs 0.5%.
Worked example (illustrative units on canonical percentages). Total ERP on-hand across two DCs =
  2,000,000 units. Mock 1: Σ|
Δ| = 140,000 → 7.0% (UoM mappings: a case-vs-each factor of 12 on 1% of SKUs alone creates 11 ×
  those units of error). Mock 2: 36,000 → 1.8% (in-transit snapshot timing). Mock 3: 6,000 →
  0.3% ≤ 0.5%  go-live recommendation. Sanity: each fix class must remove a category of Δ; if Δ
  shrinks without a named root cause, the mock is not evidence.
In-transit freeze logic. Transfers in flight at snapshot time t_s are counted at neither end or
  both unless frozen: freeze window F = 72 h ≥ max lane transit  in-transit set is static;
  converted as its own batch with Σ in-transit_legacy = Σ in-transit_JDA .
Slip economics. Pipeline $20M gated  ~$2.5M/month of slip  ~$83K/day; a 1.5-week training slip
  that does not move go-live costs 0 pipeline dollars; the same slip on the critical path would
  have cost ≈ $875K. Budget: ~$1.8M, ~3% under ≈ $54K.
Hypercare exit. blockers/day < 2 sustained  exit; exited week 3 of 4.
RF-device miss. Failure rate on newer hardware ≈ 100/400 = 25%; effort-scored "3–4
    days" vs
  impact = a go-live exit criterion (training sign-off). Rule left behind: any single-point
  dependency in front of an exit criterion gets a what-if plan regardless of estimated effort.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The record numbers first: on time, about $54K under a $1.8M budget, and the $20M pipeline started the next month, where every month of slip was worth about $2.5M. When a quarter of the RF devices failed, the same slip on the critical path would have cost about $875K, so I paid roughly $45K of paper-pick productivity instead and told leadership the training criterion moved, not the date. The modeled part is smaller and I'll label it: landing at 0.3 percent against a 0.5 tolerance meant about $100K less inventory in dispute at cutover on an illustrative $50M on-hand, and at mock one's 7 percent that would have been $3.5M. The date, the budget, the variance and the pipeline are measured; the on-hand value and the fallback cost are my model."

If challenged — the full answers.

Where does $50M of on-hand come from?

It's illustrative, two million units at $25, chosen so the percentages become dollars; the record gives me 0.3 percent, not the balance sheet. I'd firm it up with the actual ERP on-hand valuation at the freeze snapshot, which Finance already had for the reconciliation.

The $875K avoided slip is a counterfactual.

Yes, it's record arithmetic on a slip that did not happen, so I never bank it; it's the price that made the fallback the obvious call. What I'd measure is the pipeline's first program start date against plan, which was the next month.

Paper-pick cost is a guess.

It's a benchmark, 15 to 40 percent slower on a hundred devices for a week and a half, so somewhere between $23K and $84K. I'd measure it from the WMS: picks per labor hour on the paper lanes versus the RF lanes during those ten days.

3. The design round — twelve designs from the Elementum years, each with the math and the probes

The GFiber packet's Part 3 carried twelve system-design prompts. Here they are told as what they were underneath: design engagements from the Elementum client work — supply-chain design for manufacturers and network operators running SAP, 3PLs, EDI and field fleets. Use these for Amazon's functional and "how would you design / approach…" questions; they are design stories, not behavioral stories. Every design is answered in the same six moves, said out loud so the interviewer can follow the map: (a) stocks and flows — every bucket a unit can sit in, every flow between them, with the driver and the document that records it; (b) first principles — the invariants (every unit is in exactly one stock; every flow has exactly one document; every number has exactly one owner); (c) the design — components, the data model at its true grain, integration events, controls and kill switches; (d) sequencing — phases ranked Reach × Impact × Confidence ÷ Effort, with the invariants deciding what may ship at all (shown in full on E1–E2; folded into the design elsewhere); (e) the deep-dive probes; (f) one metric with a dollar — illustrative, labeled. Every dollar in this section is illustrative — a sizing method with stated assumptions. Where a pattern wasn't run end-to-end in production, the honest line says so.

3.0 — How I'd run programs at Amazon (the one shared section — methodology lives here, never inside a story)

Sequencing convention used throughout (illustrative): Reach = units, orders or people touched per month; Impact on the 0.25 / 0.5 / 1 / 2 / 3 scale; Confidence 50–100%; Effort in person-months. Score = Reach × Impact × Confidence ÷ Effort. The score ranks; the invariants decide what is allowed to ship at all. If interrupted at minute two: clarify the book of record and the primary metric in twenty seconds, then draw the stocks.

E1 — Elementum design engagement: contractor / truck-stock inventory for a field-install fleet

Use for: "design field/mobile inventory," "stock you own but don't hold," ambiguous-ownership questions. Record behind it: the owner-partitioned pool and audited acts (S1, S2), lower-of-two when two books disagree (S1, S18), reason-coded reconciliation (S14).

Honest line. A design engagement for a network-operator client whose contractors' trucks carried client-owned install equipment; I owned the inventory model, the event contract and the controls — the client's SAP team configured. I have not run a full van program end-to-end; the volumes here are illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. Contractors' trucks carry client-owned equipment and consumables; issues from the 3PL are posted, installs post late or not at all, counts are sporadic, and Finance sees "unaccounted" growing. Design the inventory model, the events, and the controls. Book of record: the client's SAP inventory. Primary metric: unaccounted units per contractor per month, and their value.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[3PL SLoc] --issue (311, scanned)--> [Van SLoc] --install (601 by serial)--> [Installed]
                 |                        |-- swap-out (311) --> [Returns-untested]
                 |                        |-- van-to-van (311, both scan)
            weekly count --> [Adjust 701/702 + reason] --> [Chargeback only from a posted count]
Unaccounted(van) = issued - installed - returned - counted_on_hand
Classify like out-of-balance: timing -> unposted install -> serial mismatch -> true loss

Invariants. Conservation: every owned unit is in exactly one stock at every instant — today the van is an unmeasured stock; "unaccounted" is not theft yet, it is a missing document. One document per flow: issue = scanned transfer; install = work-order close that posts the goods issue; count = a physical-inventory document — if a flow has no document, it does not exist and the design adds one before adding a report. One owner per number: Inventory Control owns the book; Field Ops owns counts; Finance owns the chargeback method; the contractor owns the physical; I own the definitions and gates.

(c) Design. A van is a storage location under the market plant (not a spreadsheet); serialized issues with forced scan on every flow; min/max replenishment per van by tech type, released only when last week's count is posted (count-as-gate); a reconciliation service computing issued − installed − returned − counted per van, reason-coded; governed screens for count, adjustment (role + reason) and chargeback review; the CRM reads only the assigned tech's van availability. Data grain: SKU × plant × SLoc(van) × serial × state, with an append-only serial-history ledger; every event idempotent on document + line, replays never double-post, reversals reference the original. Controls: no new issue to a van with an unposted weekly count (hard gate); days-of-cover cap with audited override; a serial issued to van A cannot be installed from van B without a posted transfer — the install is held, not lost; two weeks over tolerance ⇒ count-every-issue mode; kill switch = freeze issues to a contractor, 3PL fulfills directly.

(d) Sequencing (illustrative). P1 van-as-SLoc + serialized issue + count-as-gate (score 300) → P2 field-app install/swap events posting by serial (175) → P3 reason-coded reconciliation + Finance chargeback method (267 — ranks above P2 but needs its events; the invariant sequences it after) → P4 min/max + cap → P5 CRM same-day availability read. Rejected: a spreadsheet outside SAP — a second book.

(f) Metric. Unaccounted units per contractor per month → near zero, from posted documents only, published weekly per van with the reason mix. Illustrative dollar: 500 vans × 2 unaccounted/month × $150 average unit ≈ $150K/month ≈ $1.8M/yr of stock with no owner; the design targets the unexplained share — Finance sets the chargeback threshold, not me. Supporting: install events posted same-day ≥ 99%; vans with a posted weekly count = 100%; "van said yes, book said no" reschedules → 0.

Deep-dive probes.

Why a storage location and not consignment or subcontracting special stock?

The client owns the unit until install, so it is their stock at the contractor's location — a storage location under the market plant. Subcontracting stock is a manufacturing pattern (components consumed into a finished good); customer consignment implies the customer holds it. Neither describes a truck.

Movement types?

311 for 3PL → van (scanned both ends), 601 on the install (or 201 to a cost object if not order-based — decide once), 311 van → RMA for swap-outs, 701/702 for count differences with a reason code. 561 only for the initial load — never for "fixing" a count.

The field app is offline half the day — how does the book stay right?

Capture with a client-generated idempotency key and timestamp; replay in order per serial; reconciliation counts "captured, not posted" as its own reason code so timing is never read as loss.

Failure modes?

Install posted from the wrong van — hold, never auto-transfer. Duplicate install replay — idempotency key. Count with a phantom serial — quarantine the line. Chargeback from an unposted count — impossible by design; chargebacks read only physical-inventory documents.

Trade-offs?

Count-as-gate slows issues in week one and finds every uncounted van — accepted. Per-serial postings raise volume — sized. The days-of-cover cap frustrates long routes — override with a reason code, audited.

What if a contractor has no scanner?

Stage 1: SLoc + signed paper count uploaded; stage 2: scanning. Never skip the SLoc for the scanner.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

[Salesforce [BoR customer/order]]        [BI / BigQuery: van recon, aging]
  WorkOrder close (install/swap)                 ^  MATDOC + install/count
  | event, real-time                             |  events, hourly
  v                                              |
[Field app: tech / van / install_event / return_event / count]
  offline capture, client idempotency key, replay in order per serial
  | install event -> 601 (or 201 cost ctr) by serial, real-time
  | swap event    -> 311 van -> RMA SLoc by serial, real-time
  | van count     -> PI document -> 701/702 + reason, weekly
  v
[SAP CPI / Integration Suite]  --retry / DLQ per hop, idempotent-->
  v
[SAP S/4HANA [BoR stock + finance]]
  plant = market · SLoc per contractor x truck · serials (MARC-SERNP)
  311 3PL SLoc -> van SLoc · 601/201 install · 651 customer return
  701/702 count adjust -> chargeback report (Finance)
  ^
  | 940 pick / 945 ship-confirm for the van issue, event
[3PL WMS [BoR bin]]  -- replenishment to van min/max, weekly wave

E2 — Elementum design engagement: serial-number tracking, vendor → warehouse → technician → customer → return

Use for: traceability, serialized inventory, state-machine design questions. Record behind it: state machines for inventory buckets with one owning system per transition (S1, S2); two books reconciled to a published tolerance (S18).

Honest line. I designed and governed the state model and reconciliation; the client's SAP team configured the serial profile — I own the state list and which transitions are legal, not the IMG screens. Volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. Every serialized unit the client ships — network hardware to customer premises — must be traceable by serial across its whole life, in SAP and in every system that touches it (3PL WMS, field app, CRM asset, RMA/refurb). Book of record for the serial state: SAP, with the 3PL and field app as event sources. Primary metric: serial-state accuracy — serials whose SAP state matches a physical or system observation.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[Vendor FG] --856 ASN w/ serial list--> [In-transit] --receipt by serial (101)-->
[3PL unrestricted] --issue (311)--> [Van] --install (601 + CRM asset)--> [Installed base]
[Installed] --return/disconnect--> [Returns-untested] --test--> refurb / RTV / scrap (E10)

Invariants. A serial never appears from nowhere — its first legal appearance is the ASN; a receipt for a serial the ASN did not carry is an exception, not a receipt; an install for a serial not in that van is a hold. Every flow has a reversal that references the original, so a replay or correction never manufactures a phantom serial. Minimal design forced: serial profile with stock check; the ASN carries serials; every downstream event carries the serial; one append-only serial-history ledger; a daily three-way reconciliation (SAP vs 3PL vs field app/CRM asset).

(c) Design. Serial profile with stock check on the serialized materials, so a goods issue for a serial not in that location fails loudly instead of posting silently; EDI/ASN ingest validating serial lists against the PO; receipts by serial; install/swap events by serial creating the CRM asset; an RMA module with test-result events and grade — refurb keeps the same serial, same material, with a grade attribute: never re-number, so the asset history stays continuous. Grain: serial × SKU × state × location × owning-system × as-of, plus the serial-history ledger (one row per transition: document, source, actor, timestamp, idempotency key). Controls: ASN serial count = PO quantity or the ASN quarantines; a receipt for an unknown serial goes to an exception queue with a 24-hour vendor SLA — never auto-create; installed serials with no CRM asset after 24 h alert; corrections only through a governed screen with role + reason — never a manual table edit, and never "fix SAP to match the 3PL" — lower-of-two for promising while the cause is found.

(d) Sequencing (illustrative). P1 serial profile + ASN serials + receipt by serial (~50K serials/mo, score 24,000) → P2 install/swap by serial + CRM asset (10,500) → P3 three-way daily reconciliation + governed correction screen (28,333 — after P1/P2; needs the events) → P4 RMA/refurb states → P5 scrap/RTV with certificates.

(f) Metric. Serial-state accuracy ≥ 99.5%, measured by the daily three-way reconciliation with a reason-coded exception list. Illustrative dollar: each mis-stated serial costs a truck roll or a lost unit; at 200K installed serials, 97% → 99.5% removes ~5,000 mis-states/yr × ~$120 blended ≈ $600K/yr — before warranty-recovery value (in-warranty failures returned to vendor by serial). Supporting: ASN-to-receipt serial match ≥ 99.5%; installs with an asset record within 24 h = 100%.

Deep-dive probes.

How do serials travel on EDI?

The 856 ASN carries serials in its hierarchical loops at item/pack level; the 945 ship advice carries serials for the van issue; adjustments must carry serials or they quarantine.

Idempotency?

Key = document + line + serial; the ledger rejects a duplicate transition and logs it; reversals reference the original document. A replayed install never creates a second asset.

The 3PL receives a serial the ASN didn't list?

Exception queue, 24-hour SLA to the vendor; the unit sits blocked and unpromisable until resolved. Never auto-create the serial.

Refurb identity — same serial, new material?

Same serial, same material, a grade attribute or refurb location — never re-number; the asset history stays continuous.

Failure modes?

Duplicate serials from a vendor — block at ASN validation. Typo serial from the field — scan-only with check digits. State fixed by a table edit — forbidden; correction screen with reason and role. Reconciliation that "fixes" SAP to match the 3PL — forbidden; lower-of-two while the cause is found.

Trade-offs?

Serialization slows receiving (a scan per unit) — offset by ASN pre-load. Three return states add screens — but an untested return that is promisable is a truck roll.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

[Vendors / EDI]  --856 ASN + serial list (SLN/REF) via AS2 -> CPI-->

                                    |

   |  944 receipt by serial -> 101 (103/105 if QI) -> EQUI created, event
   |  945 ship advice + serials -> 311 to van SLoc, event
   |  947 adjust must carry serials or quarantine, event
   v
[SAP S/4HANA [BoR serial state + stock]]

   ^                         ^                        |

E3 — Elementum design engagement: vendor EDI + VMI for long-lead network equipment

Use for: vendor integration, consignment/VMI, inbound-reliability design questions. Record behind it: 597 lanes of X12 EDI behind a ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate (S9); lead times measured as distributions (S8).

Honest line. The EDI onboarding is run experience (S9); the VMI/consignment mechanics are the SAP pattern, designed in the engagement but not run by me as a program — labeled.

The problem as I restate it. Long-lead equipment from a few strategic vendors; the client wants EDI for POs/ASNs/invoices and a VMI arrangement where the vendor replenishes to agreed min/max at the central 3PL. Design the transaction set, the stock-ownership model, and the controls. Book of record: SAP MM (PO, goods receipt, consignment stock). Primary metric: confirmed-date reliability — the vendor's receipts measured against its own confirmations — and valid-ASN rate.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[Vendor inventory] --850 PO out / 852 product activity (VMI)--> [Vendor committed (855 ack)]
  --ship (856 ASN; 214 status)--> [In-transit] --receive (101 K if consignment / 101 if own)-->
[Consignment K at 3PL] --consume/transfer (411 K)--> [Own unrestricted] -> settlement (MRKO)
Reorder point ~ demand over p90(LT) + z x sigma_L  -- from measured distributions (S8),
  refreshed monthly with a drift flag; brochure lead times are inputs to nothing

Invariants. A confirmation without a date is not a confirmation. A receipt without an ASN is an exception, not a receipt. Ownership is explicit at every instant: vendor-owned consignment on our floor is special stock K, visible to planning, settled at consumption — or the vendor replenishes our own stock under blanket POs; who carries the working capital is a Finance decision, modeled both ways, not a default.

(c) Design. Transaction set: 850 PO out · 855 acknowledgment (confirmation, with date and quantity) · 856 ASN · 810 invoice · 997 functional acknowledgment on every interchange · 852 product activity for VMI · 861 receiving advice for discrepancies. Confirmation discipline: the 855's date becomes the planning date; "acknowledged" with no date = unconfirmed, a planner exception. ASN gate per vendor: ≥95% valid ASN before activation (the S9 gate, reused); vendors with weak data onboard on portal templates and graduate. Min/max set from measured lead-time distributions per vendor × lane, refreshed monthly, drift-flagged. Controls: gateway de-duplication on interchange control numbers; document idempotency on PO + line + confirmation sequence; out-of-order 855 after 856 — the ASN wins quantity, the ack updates only the date; a mis-parsing pack hierarchy quarantines, never partially posts.

(f) Metric. Confirmed-date reliability ≥ 95% — receipts on or before the vendor's own confirmed date, measured from month one (baseline unknown, so the metric starts honest). Illustrative dollar: if late equipment idles a build crew at ~$50K/week and confirmation discipline avoids ~20 such weeks/yr ≈ $1M/yr; plus working capital if consignment carries ~$2M at the vendor's expense (≈$160K/yr at 8%). Supporting: valid-ASN ≥95% per vendor before activation; receipts without ASN → 0.

Deep-dive probes.

Consignment vs own stock for VMI?

Special stock K: vendor-owned on our floor, visible to planning, settled at consumption. Alternative: vendor replenishes our own stock under blanket POs — simpler accounting, but we carry the inventory. Choose by who should carry the working capital; I model both and let Finance decide.

Idempotency and ordering?

Control numbers de-duplicate at the gateway; document-level idempotency on PO + line + sequence; an ASN received twice creates one inbound delivery.

Failure modes?

An 855 with "acknowledged" but no date — treat as unconfirmed. An ASN with an unparseable pack hierarchy — quarantine, not partial post. Unit-of-measure mismatch — a conversion table at the map (the classic mock-1 defect from S18). A looping 997 on a failed interchange — dead-letter after three.

How do you set min/max?

From measured lead-time distributions per vendor × lane: reorder point ≈ demand over the p90 lead time plus safety stock from σ_L, refreshed monthly with a drift flag. Brochure lead times are inputs to nothing.

Trade-offs?

Confirmation control adds planner exceptions in month one — that is the point; it reveals the unreliable vendors. The ASN gate delays go-live for weak-data vendors — they onboard on the portal floor and graduate, which keeps the long tail inside the standard.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

[SAP S/4HANA MM [BoR PO, GR, consignment K]]
  MRP -> EBAN -> EKKO/EKPO/EKET; 855 -> EKES (planning date)
  101 / 101 K at GR · 411 K consume -> MRKO settle · 122 RTV · 810 3-way
   | 850 PO out, on release        ^ 855 ack -> EKES, event
   | 852 product activity, daily   | 856 ASN -> inbound LIKP/LIPS, event
   | 830 forecast (VMI), weekly    | 810 invoice -> RBKP/RSEG, event
   v                               | 997 on every interchange, <4h
[SAP CPI / Integration Suite: Integration Advisor maps, Trading Partner
 Mgmt, ISA/GS/ST de-dup, DLQ per hop]  <--861 receiving advice, exceptions
   ^                               |
   | AS2 / SFTP                    v
[Vendors / OEM ERP]          [Carriers] --214 status, optional, event-->
   reads 852/830, ships to min/max
                                   |
[3PL WMS [BoR bin]] --944 receipt by serial -> 101 / 101 K, event-->  SAP
                    <--inbound delivery expected receipts, event--    SAP
                                   |
[BI / BigQuery] <-- EKES vs GR dates, ASN validity, K on-hand vs vendor
                    statement, LT samples per lane (p50/p90), daily

E4 — Elementum design engagement: CRM order → SAP fulfillment integration with status sync

Use for: order-orchestration, system-integration, "orders are dropping" design questions. Record behind it: the OMS held to "read published availability, never compute" and the event chain across WMS/OMS/POS/S4 (S1, S3); scheduler and status-mapping failures lived through (S13, S14, and a ~$2.8M status-mapping root-cause at Flexport).

Honest line. I have not implemented the client's CRM; I owned the integration contract — the event chain, idempotency, status truth and reconciliation — and the client's teams built both ends.

The problem as I restate it. A customer signs up in the client's CRM; the order must create the SAP fulfillment (a kit delivery from the 3PL or van), reserve stock, and keep status flowing back so the CRM, the technician and the customer see the same state — without orders dropping at month-end or double-shipping on retries. Book of record: CRM for the customer order; SAP for stock and fulfillment; status truth per hop is the system that performed the hop. Primary metric: orders reconciled across every hop — sent = created = delivered = status returned — 100% daily.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[CRM order created] --order.created (correlation id)--> [SAP order created]
  --ATP against PUBLISHED availability (never computed in the CRM)--> [Reserved]
  --install date - lead--> [Delivery created] --pick/ship/PGI--> [Shipped]
  --install event--> [Complete] ... status flows BACK per hop, one writer per field

Invariants. One creation path: deliveries are created only from the SAP order, never from the inbound event — a duplicate event cannot make a second delivery. One availability truth: the CRM reads published availability; it never computes its own. One writer per status field; a status the map does not know defaults to "unknown — investigate," never to "shipped." Every message carries a correlation id that lives on the SAP order, so a retry finds its own order.

(c) Design. Durable messaging with retry and dead-letter queues per hop; an outbox table on the CRM side so order-create is at-least-once with no lost orders; idempotency at create (correlation id unique on the order — a second create returns the first order); version checks so an update arriving before its predecessor holds rather than overwrites; availability published to a cache with a staleness flag ("hold last-good, flag it") so CRM promising survives an SAP outage; daily hop-count reconciliation with an unreconciled queue cleared by 9 AM; a heartbeat on every scheduler in the chain (the S13 lesson — detect in one interval, not by a VP at 4 AM).

(f) Metric. Orders reconciled across every hop daily → 100%, unreconciled count → 0 by 9 AM. Illustrative dollar: if 0.5% of 50K orders/month stall and each stalled install costs ~$75 in rescheduling plus a day of deferred revenue, ≈$19K/month ≈ $225K/yr direct — before churn on a bad first impression, which is the number that matters and which Finance, not I, should size. Supporting: dead-letter age p95 < 1 h; duplicate deliveries = 0; status latency p95 < 5 min.

Deep-dive probes.

Durable documents or APIs?

Durable, monitorable documents (IDoc-class) for order create/change — retry with visible status; APIs for status back where latency matters. Either way the correlation id lives in a reference field on the order so a retry finds its own order.

How do you stop a retry from double-shipping?

Idempotency at create — the correlation id is unique on the order, so the second create returns the first order — and deliveries are created only from the SAP order, never from the inbound event.

Orders drop at month-end — where do you look?

Count reconciliation across hops for the last three month-ends; the message monitor and dead-letter queue by error class; job overlaps and lock waits at the drop times — close jobs locking customer or material masters, availability runs in the same window; the release calendar. Fix: retry with backoff plus idempotency immediately; re-sequence or throttle the contention as the cure; counters on a dashboard so it is visible before close.

Status mapping — what breaks?

A new partner status not in the map (default to "unknown — investigate," never "shipped"); a mapping changed without regression (release train only); two hops writing the same CRM field (one writer per field). A ~$2.8M root-cause I ran at Flexport was exactly a status-mapping defect.

Failure modes?

Version 3 arrives before version 2 — version check holds the older. SAP created but the confirmation back was lost — reconciliation finds "created, unconfirmed" and re-emits. A ship-confirm for a cancelled delivery — exception; physical stock to be found. A scheduler dies silently — heartbeat page.

Trade-offs?

The CRM reading SAP availability adds an uptime dependency — mitigated by the published cache with a staleness flag. The outbox adds a table and a relay — the price of at-least-once with no lost orders.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

[Salesforce [BoR customer + order]: Account, Order/OrderItem, WorkOrder,
 Asset, status field (one writer per field)]
   | order.created/changed/cancelled via outbox, at-least-once, real-time
   | correlation_id = SF order + line + version
   v                                   ^ order.confirmed (SAP VBELN)
[SAP CPI / event bus]                  | status.changed(hop, ts, corr id)
   ORDERS/ORDCHG IDoc or API ·         | p95 < 5 min, per hop
   DLQ per hop · per-hop counters ·    | availability read (never
   status_map table (versioned) -------+ computed), cached + stale flag
   v
[SAP S/4HANA [BoR stock + fulfillment]]
   VBAK/VBAP (corr id in reference field) -> ATP -> VBBE requirement
   -> LIKP/LIPS delivery (install date - lead time) -> PGI 601 (MATDOC)
   | 940 pick, daily wave        ^ 945 ship confirm -> 601, event
   v                             |
[3PL WMS [BoR bin]] --or-- [Field app: van pick / job.completed]
                                 | job.completed -> SAP install posting
                                 | + SF Asset(SerialNumber), real-time
[BI / BigQuery] <-- hop_counter, orders-by-state aging, DLQ age, daily
   received = processed + failed + in_flight per hop, balanced by 9 AM

E5 — Elementum design engagement: 3PL inventory reconciliation — two books, one truth, Finance protected

Use for: reconciliation design, "two systems disagree," inventory-integrity questions. Record behind it: the lower-of-two basis and out-of-balance sign rule (S1), the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance and read by cause (S18), reason-coded reconciliation as a standing control (S14).

Honest line. This is the seam I have owned repeatedly; the design is the generalized version of run experience. Volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. The 3PL's WMS and SAP disagree on on-hand by SKU (and by serial); the delta drifts, promising reads SAP, Finance closes on SAP, and adjustments are posted ad hoc. Design the reconciliation so the two books converge, the customer is protected while they disagree, and Finance can trust the close. Books: SAP at storage-location grain; the 3PL WMS at bin/serial grain. Primary metric: reconciled delta — rows outside tolerance after reason-coding, trending to inside tolerance.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[In-transit (ASN)] --receive (944 -> 101)--> [3PL bin] = [SAP unrestricted]
[SAP unrestricted] --pick/ship (940 -> 945 -> PGI 601)--> [Shipped]
[WMS bin moves] -- internal, MUST NOT change SAP qty
[Damage/hold] --947 status adjust--> [SAP blocked] · [Count diff] --947 w/ id--> [701/702]
Daily: snapshot(WMS by bin, rolled to SLoc) vs MB52(SAP) per SKU x SLoc x state
Delta classified: timing (in-flight ledger) | mapping | status | physical -> only
  "physical" ever becomes an adjustment

Invariants. Neither side wins automatically: SAP is the book of record for Finance and promising; the 3PL is the physical truth; promising reads the lower of the two until the reason is known. Grain alignment is a contract: bins roll up to the storage location; WMS statuses map to unrestricted/QI/blocked through one owned table; units of measure and pack factors per SKU; serials compared as sets — any row that cannot be aligned is a mapping defect, not a variance. Adjustments only from documents: a 947 with an adjustment id, posting a referenced 701/702 — never a retroactive snapshot that rewrites history.

(c) Design. A daily snapshot comparison against the in-flight ledger (so timing never reads as shrink), reason-coded into timing / mapping / status / physical with an owner per class; a tolerance per value band published with Finance before the first comparison; serialized equipment at zero tolerance — every unexplained serial is a unit whose state is unknown, which is exactly the work; idempotent snapshots (date + warehouse) and adjustment ids so replays never post twice; the delta report read before every close, trending from "pipe" causes to "physical" causes as the pipes get fixed.

(f) Metric. Reconciled delta: SKU × location rows outside tolerance after reason-coding → 0 on consecutive days; serialized equipment: 0 unexplained serials. Illustrative dollar: with ~$20M of equipment at 3PLs and a 2% unexplained delta, ~$400K of inventory has no reliable owner; converging to 0.5% frees ~$300K of buffer Finance no longer reserves against — before the truck rolls avoided by not promising phantoms. Supporting: unposted ship-confirms >48 h → 0; adjustments with reason code = 100%; month-end close with zero silent true-ups.

Deep-dive probes.

Which side wins?

Neither, automatically. SAP is the book for Finance and promising; the 3PL is the physical truth. Promising reads the lower until the reason is known; Finance sees the reason-coded delta; only the "physical" class becomes an adjustment.

How do you align grains?

Bins roll to the storage location; statuses map through one owned table; unit-of-measure and pack factors per SKU — the classic mock-1 defect class; serials as sets. Unalignable rows are mapping defects, not variances.

Idempotency?

Snapshots idempotent on date + warehouse; adjustments carry the 3PL's id — a replay never posts twice; the book adjustment references the 3PL document.

Failure modes?

An inventory snapshot arrives before overnight ship-confirms post — timing; compare against the in-flight ledger, not zero. A status the map does not know — "unknown, hold," never unrestricted. The 3PL "fixes" a count by re-sending corrected history — reject retroactive snapshots; adjustments go through documents.

Trade-offs?

Lower-of-two under-promises while the delta stands — a planning variance accepted over a customer defect. Zero tolerance on serials creates more exceptions — each is a unit whose state is unknown, which is exactly the work.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

[Vendors / EDI] --856 ASN -> CPI -> inbound delivery LIKP/LIPS, event-->
                                                                |
[3PL WMS [BoR bin / serial scan]]                               v
   bins, WMS status, cycle counts, holds      [SAP S/4HANA [BoR SLoc stock
   | 944 receipt -> 101, event                  + finance]]  (#2: IM grain)
   | 945 ship confirm -> PGI 601 + serials       MARD / MCHB / MSKA by
   | 947 adjust (qty or status) -> quarantined    MATNR x WERKS x LGORT
   |     until reason-coded -> 701/702 or 344     x stock type; EQUI serials
   | 846 stock snapshot at SKU x whse x status    MATDOC movement trail
   |     x serial, daily by 06:00 local           ^ 940 pick, daily wave
   v                                              |
[SAP CPI / Integration Suite]  ---- IDoc status WE02 (51 / 64) --------+
   idempotent on date + warehouse (846), 947 adjustment id, 945 id
   |                                              |
   v                                              v
[BI / BigQuery: recon_fact] <-- MARD/MCHB + MATDOC + EQUI, daily 06:30 --
   align bin->SLoc, status->stock type, UoM/pack; Δ by reason;
   in-flight ledger (940 w/o 945, ASN w/o 944, 945 not posted)
   | min(SAP, WMS) per SKU x node while Δ != 0, hourly
   v
[Salesforce / promising] reads the lower basis  ·  [Finance] reads Δ list

E6 — Elementum design engagement: PLM ECN → SAP material/BOM handoff, with obsolete-part disposition

Use for: engineering-change, BOM-integrity, obsolescence questions. Record behind it: the contract manufacturer's BOM made safe to plan on — phantoms, revision overlaps, one supply equation, per-site reconcile gate (S7) — the same problem from the SAP side.

Honest line. I have not implemented a PLM system; I have run the part of the PLM→ERP handoff that hurts — effectivity, phantoms and where-used are what break planning. Volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. Engineering releases change notices in the client's PLM (new revision, substitute component, end-of-life). SAP must get the material-master and BOM changes with correct effectivity; planning must not buy the obsolete part; stock of the obsolete part — say 5,000 units — needs a disposition; and the field must know which revision to install. Books: PLM for engineering design; SAP for the planning/costing BOM and inventory. Primary metric: ECN-driven exceptions — POs placed for obsolete parts, and revision overlaps in the planning BOM → 0.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[PLM in-work] --ECN released--> [Released revision] --transfer (change master,
  valid-from)--> [SAP material + BOM, new rev] --effectivity switch--> [MRP re-plans]
[Old-part stock, 5,000 units] --disposition--> run-out | rework | return-to-vendor | scrap
Purchasing BLOCKED at ECN approval, not at effectivity -- the gap between the two is
  where obsolete buys happen

Invariants. Two revisions are never valid on the same date — reject at transfer. Effectivity is never set in the past — planning would re-plan history. The BOM never arrives before its material — order the transfer or stage the BOM. A phantom assembly explodes through to components, so the reconcile gate must explode through phantoms or it will pass a wrong structure (the S7 lesson). Disposition is a signed sequence — Engineering → Planning → Finance — and purchasing is blocked at approval while issuing continues for run-out.

(c) Design. The ECN transfer carries the change master with valid-from; date effectivity for planning (MRP needs one switch), serial or lot effectivity for the field only where revisions are not interchangeable; a where-used check drives the disposition decision tree: still consumable in a valid assembly → run-out (push valid-from to the run-out date, if not safety-related); small modification → costed rework order; vendor accepts → return against the PO; otherwise scrap with Finance's write-down — approved before effectivity, with the workflow posting the status change itself so "approved but not blocked" cannot exist. Field protection: asset-record effectivity plus a bulletin so an old revision cannot be installed after the switch.

(f) Metric. ECN exceptions — POs placed for obsolete parts plus revision overlaps in the planning BOM → 0 per month, measured from open-PO data against material status and from the reconcile-gate log. Illustrative dollar: one avoided obsolete buy of 5,000 units × $40 = $200K; at ~10 obsoletes/yr with even 20% previously mis-bought ≈ $400K/yr, plus the write-down Finance no longer takes by surprise. Supporting: ECN → SAP lead time ≤ 1 business day; dispositions approved before effectivity = 100%; field installs of a superseded revision → 0.

Deep-dive probes.

Date vs serial effectivity?

Date for planning — MRP needs one switch; serial or lot for the field when old and new revisions must be distinguished at install. Both recorded on the ECN; the planning BOM uses the date.

5,000 units on hand — how do you decide?

Where-used first: is the part still consumable in any valid assembly? Run-out if yes and the change is not safety-related; rework if it's a small costed modification; return if the vendor accepts; scrap with the write-down otherwise. The signature order is Engineering → Planning → Finance, and purchasing is blocked at approval, not at effectivity.

How do phantoms behave?

A phantom explodes through to its components in planning; an ECN on a phantom changes the parent's effective components — the reconcile gate must explode through phantoms or it passes a wrong structure.

Failure modes?

Two revisions valid the same date — reject at transfer. Effectivity in the past — reject; planning would re-plan history. BOM before material — stage it. Disposition approved but status unchanged — the workflow posts the status itself. Field installs the old revision after effectivity — asset effectivity plus the bulletin.

Trade-offs?

Blocking purchasing at approval can starve a run-out if Planning misjudged — mitigate by allowing issue while blocking purchase, with a dated review. Serial effectivity costs field data discipline — use it only where revisions are not interchangeable.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

[Siemens Teamcenter [BoR engineering design]]
   Item / ItemRevision / BOMView / ChangeNotice (ECN), release status
   | ECN released -> T4S (or CPI/API, #4): MATMAS then BOMMAT, ordered,
   | with change master AENR + valid-from; per ECN, <= 1 business day
   v
[T4S / SAP CPI]  -- staging queue: hold BOM until material exists;
                    reject two revisions valid on one date; idem = ECN id
   v
[SAP S/4HANA [BoR planning BOM, stock, cost]]
   MARA/MARC (MMSTA/MSTAE status) · MAST/STKO/STPO under AENR
   CS15 where-used · MD04/MD06 MRP · EKPO open POs · MARD on-hand
   | where_used.snapshot per ECN (BOMs, POs, on-hand, installed), event
   | disposition approved (Eng -> Planning -> Finance) -> status change
   | PO cancel / convert -> 850 change -> 855 from vendor, per ECN
   | 122 RTV · 551 scrap · rework order · run-out (valid-from pushed)
   v                                    v
[Vendors / EDI] <- 850/855 ->     [Field app + Salesforce Asset]
                                    serial effectivity -> which rev to
                                    install; service bulletin, per ECN
[BI / BigQuery] <-- ECN log, reconcile-gate results, EKPO vs status,
                    revision overlaps, daily

E7 — Elementum design engagement: equipment supply & demand forecasting for a new-market build

Use for: forecast-to-buy, cold-start demand, launch-planning design questions. Record behind it: forecast-accuracy measurement and input integrity (MAPE/bias, 70→85%, the both-errors release rule, S6); lead-time distributions (S8); need floors so one channel cannot starve another (S1).

Honest line. I have not owned a market-launch forecast end to end; the forecast number belongs to Planning — I own the equation, the measurement and the triggers. Newsvendor and safety-stock formulas are the MIT MicroMasters toolkit, labeled. Volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. The client launches market N: build sites ramp by month from the build plan; demand follows adoption curves with no local history; kits and long-lead equipment have vendor lead times of weeks to months. Design the forecast-to-buy chain so installs are never delayed by stockouts and stranded stock stays inside a band. Books: the project system for the build plan; Planning for the adoption curve; SAP MRP for supply. Primary metric: install delays caused by stockouts → 0, with days-of-cover inside band.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) the math

[Build plan] --milestones--> [Sites passed] --adoption curve x marketing-->
[Install demand] --kit BOM explosion--> [Component need by SKU x week, as PIRs]
--MRP (measured lead times, confirmed dates)--> [Planned orders -> PRs -> POs]
--856/101--> [Central 3PL pool] --STOs as sell-through proves the curve--> [Markets]
Newsvendor first buy: CR = Cu / (Cu + Co)
  missed install ~ $300 (re-roll + deferred revenue); holding ~ $3/mo
  CR ~ 0.99 -> order the 99th percentile of month 1-3 demand for the core unit;
  lower-Cu extras sit at the 70th (illustrative inputs; the method is the toolkit)
Re-forecast trigger: actual take-rate vs curve +/-20% for two consecutive weeks ->
  new version, re-explode kits, MRP on confirmed dates; bias sign published weekly

Invariants. Demand enters planning exactly once, through the engine — manually loaded requirements beside it are blocked. Lead times are measured distributions, never brochures (the S8 rule): the planned delivery time is the measured p50 with a drift flag, and the confirmed date drives the receipt, not the requested date. The first buy is held central: under maximum uncertainty one pool covers several markets' variance (risk pooling); stock forward-deploys only as sell-through proves the curve. Launch reserves carry need floors with hold-until dates — one market cannot steal another's launch; a floor breach is a governed screen with a reason code, not an email.

(f) Metric. Install delays caused by stockouts → 0 in the first 90 days of a launch, measured from reschedule reasons joined to the supply view; days-of-cover inside band per SKU × market; MAPE and bias published from month 2. Illustrative dollar: 2,000 installs/month × 3% stockout-caused reschedules × ~$300 ≈ $18K/month ≈ $216K/yr per market, plus stranded-stock avoidance — holding 20% of a $500K first buy central rather than in-market keeps ≈$100K from stranding. The client's take-rate and unit costs belong to its Planning and Finance — never invented.

Deep-dive probes.

Why central-first?

The first buy happens under maximum uncertainty; holding it central collapses the distribution — one pool covers several markets' variance — and stock transfers forward-deploy as the curve proves out.

Show the newsvendor logic.

Critical ratio = underage cost over underage plus overage. A missed install at ~$300 against ~$3/month of holding gives ~0.99 — order the 99th percentile of early-month demand for the core unit; accessories with a lower miss cost sit at the 70th. Illustrative inputs; the method is the toolkit, labeled.

Re-forecast trigger?

Actual vs curve ±20% for two consecutive weeks → new forecast version, kits re-exploded, planning on confirmed dates. Bias sign publishes weekly so Planning sees direction before magnitude.

How do you stop one market stealing another's units?

Need floors and hold-until dates on launch reserves; only the uncommitted pool is reallocatable; a floor breach is a screen with a reason code.

Failure modes?

Requirements loaded manually beside the engine — block. A brochure lead time in the master — replace with measured p50, flag drift. Kit BOM effectivity undated — the ECN process (E6). An adoption curve borrowed from a non-analog market — archetype review with Marketing.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

+------------------+  homes_passed.updated    +--------------------+
| SAP PS (WBS)     |  WBS milestone, weekly   | Planning tool      |
| PROJ/PRPS        |------------------------->| curve x build_plan |
+------------------+                          | forecast_version   |
+------------------+  orders + install dates, +---------+----------+
| Salesforce       |  reschedule reasons,     ^         | forecast.published
| Order/Account    |  daily ------------------+         | -> MD61 PIRs
+------------------+                                    |  PBED/PBIM, weekly
+------------------+  kit BOM STKO/STPO + AENR          v
| Teamcenter / T4S |  effectivity, event -----> +----------------------+
+------------------+                            | SAP S/4HANA [BoR]    |
| Vendors / EDI    |  850 out / 855 -> EKES AB  | MRP nightly: PLAF -> |
