E8 — Elementum design engagement: the monthly release / regression strategy for SAP + integrations

Use for: release management, regression strategy, "a release broke production" questions. Record behind it: run experience — the 4-week cadence with the QA lead owning the gate (S15); node × channel × transition regression matrices as the gate (S1); rule changes riding the cadence with a kill switch (S4).

Honest line. This one is experience generalized, not adjacency. Volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. SAP configuration, custom objects, integration maps, the CRM, the 3PL interface and the field app all change; a release broke MRP last month. Design the release train and the regression strategy so changes ship monthly with no surprise on the floor. Book of record: the transport/change log and the release manifest. Primary metric: release-caused Sev 1–2 incidents per release → 0, with the cadence held.

(a) The train · (b) first principles

[Backlog] --commit (freeze T-15d; definition of ready cites a regression cell)-->
[Release scope] --build--> [DEV transports/packages] --promote T-10d--> [QA integrated]
--regression matrix + E2E chain, T-5d--> [QA verified] --go/no-go T-2d (QA lead owns
the gate; Blocker+Critical = 0; rollback plan; freeze check)--> [PROD] --hypercare,
numeric exit--> [closed]
Rules: scope swapped, never absorbed · config and code in separate transports ·
emergency changes counted and 100% retrofitted · freeze around peak and close

Invariants. A change rides the train or does not ship. The regression matrix at the true grain is the gate, and every cell has an owner. The manifest is frozen at T−15; new work is estimated and swapped, never absorbed. Transports import to QA in the same sequence as production; nothing goes "just this once" outside the manifest. Hypercare exits on a number, never a calendar.

(c) Design. The MRP-protection cell: run MRP in QA on a production-like snapshot before and after the transport, diff the exception counts and planned-order totals by plant — unexplained deltas fail the build. Integration maps regression-tested with golden files per transaction set replayed against the new map version, payloads asserted field by field, contract tests against the partner's staging endpoint, dead-letter queue empty after replay. The CRM and field app ride the same manifest and the same go/no-go; if an app-store lag decouples them, SAP-side changes deploy dark and enable when the app reaches the field.

(f) Metric. Release-caused Sev 1–2 per release → 0, cadence held (12 trains/yr, no skipped month), measured from the incident ledger joined to the deploy log by object. Illustrative dollar: one MRP-breaking release that mis-plans a week of equipment can idle a build crew (~$50K/week) and strand or delay ~$200K of POs; avoiding two such incidents a year ≈ $300–500K/yr — before the trust cost of a floor that stops believing the calendar. Supporting: emergency changes ≤1 per release, 100% retrofitted; hypercare exit ≤ T+5; regression cells owned = 100%.

Deep-dive probes.

Why did the release break MRP, and what cell catches it?

Typical causes: a changed MRP type, lot size or planning strategy; a BOM effectivity landing early; a planning-file change; a job variant. The cell: run MRP in QA on a production-like snapshot before and after, diff exception counts and planned-order totals by plant; unexplained deltas fail the build.

How do you regression-test integration maps?

Golden files per transaction set replayed against the new map version; the produced payload asserted field by field; contract tests against the partner's staging endpoint; the dead-letter queue empty after replay.

Transport discipline?

A managed change list; QA imports in production sequence; configuration and code in separate transports so a config rollback doesn't drag code.

Failure modes?

Scope creep after freeze — swap, never absorb. Regression passed on synthetic data missing the failing case — production-like snapshot, anonymized. A transport imported out of order — sequence check. Hypercare exited on the calendar — numeric exit only. Emergency changes not retrofitted — counted in the postmortem, retrofit ticket mandatory.

Trade-offs?

A 4-week train delays small fixes — the emergency path exists and is counted. Automation is expensive up front — build the manual matrix first, automate the cells that fail most.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

The "data" here is the release artefact: transport → regression cell → interface test cell → go/no-go (LRR)

→ PROD, plus the interfaces under test.
+---------+ scope.frozen T-15  +-----------------------+ manifest v1..vN:
| Jira    |------------------->| Release manifest      | release x item x
| backlog |                    | [BoR: change log]     | transport / pkg id
+---------+                    +---+-----+-----+---+---+
   transport E070/E071 | CPI pkg  |     |     |   |  SF change set | build
  +--------------------------------+     |     |   +-----------------+
  v                    v                 v     v                    v
+---------------+  +---------------+  +---------------+  +---------------+
| SAP S/4       |  | CPI / Int.    |  | Salesforce    |  | Field app     |
| DEV -> QA     |  | Suite maps    |  | change sets   |  | build         |
+-------+-------+  +-------+-------+  +-------+-------+  +-------+-------+
        v import T-10 (same sequence as PROD) v                  v
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| QA (integrated): matrix cells MRP type x proc type x plant; MRP diff    |
| cell MD04/PLAF before vs after; interface cells = golden 850/855/856/   |
| 945/947 -> IDoc/API asserts, 3PL staging endpoint, DLQ empty; SF status |
+-----------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
   regression.completed T-5 per cell v   gonogo.decided T-2 (LRR: B+C=0)
+---------------+ deploy T, flags off +---------------+ monitors T+1..T+5:
| LRR / go-no-go|-------------------->| PROD S/4 [BoR]|-> hop counters, DLQ,
| QA lead owns  | emergency->retrofit | +CPI+SF+app   |   IDoc 51, MRP excp.

E9 — Elementum design engagement: the ERP cutover from a legacy WMS — tolerance, mocks, freeze, one promise publisher

Use for: cutover/migration design questions — the designed generalization of S18's run experience. Record behind it: the 0.5% tolerance published before mock 1, mocks 7 → 1.8 → 0.3 read by cause, the 72-hour freeze, the RF fallback (S18); zero-then-publish (S2); book-of-record discipline (S1, S3).

Honest line. The design is the generalized playbook of a migration I ran; mechanics from the record, volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. A DC (or a 3PL site) runs a legacy WMS with its own inventory book; the client wants the ERP as book of record, with no double-promising during the switch and a Finance-grade reconciliation at go-live. Book of record: the legacy WMS until the switch instant; the ERP after. Primary metric: cutover reconciliation variance vs a published tolerance, and zero double-promises.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[Legacy on-hand, bin x status] --extract + map (UoM, location, status vocab)-->
[Staging, SKU x SLoc x stock type x serial] --load (561 / migration cockpit,
control totals)--> [ERP initial stock]
[In-flight receipts/shipments] --72-h freeze + their own batch--> [ERP in-transit]
[Open orders] --convert / re-create at cut-off--> [ERP deliveries]
[Both books] --reconcile by cause vs published tolerance--> go/no-go
Promise switch: zero the legacy availability FIRST, then publish the ERP's --
  never the reverse; consumers read one publisher at a time

Invariants. The tolerance is published with Finance before the first mock, so "good enough" is arithmetic, not negotiation. A mock is read by cause class, never by headline — a total that shrinks without a named cause is not evidence. The initial load has one door (migration postings with control totals); nothing is "fixed" afterwards outside the governed path. In-transit gets its own batch behind the freeze — the mock-2 lesson. Serials load only after their masters exist, at zero tolerance.

(c) Design. Three mocks on full production data, each a rehearsal of the real cutover including the failback; mapping sets as data (units of measure, location names, status vocabularies — the mock-1 defect classes); the cut-off list that catches late receipts posted against legacy orders; device readiness as a program row with a rehearsed manual fallback (the S18 RF lesson); hypercare with a numeric exit (<2 blockers/day sustained). If the 3PL keeps its WMS, the ERP integrates at storage-location grain over the standard document set (E5); if the client runs the floor, an embedded warehouse module with bin mapping — decided by who runs the floor, not by preference.

(f) Metric. Cutover reconciliation variance vs the published tolerance — e.g., 0.3% against 0.5% at go-live, 0 unexplained serials — with zero double-promises during the switch, measured by the reconciliation-by-cause report at mock 3 and at T+1. Illustrative dollar: the cost of missing the date is the program's own number — in my record it was a $20M pipeline at ~$2.5M/month of slip; for a new client I ask Finance for the equivalent rather than invent it. Supporting: hypercare exit ≤ T+3 weeks; manual stock corrections post go-live outside the governed path = 0.

Deep-dive probes.

Load mechanics?

Migration cockpit or initial-entry postings with control totals; batch and serial masters pre-loaded before balances; value flows to the migration clearing account and Finance reconciles it. The migration posting is the only door — never "fix" with count adjustments afterwards without a cause.

What produced the 7% in mock 1 and how did it get to 0.3%?

Units of measure, location names that mapped to nothing, and order-status vocabulary — fixed in the mapping set (1.8%); then in-transit timing — fixed with the 72-hour freeze and a separate batch (0.3%). Each cause had an owner; the number was read by cause every time.

How do you avoid double-promising during the switch?

Zero the legacy promise source first — legacy availability reads zero to the order systems — then publish the ERP's availability. Never the reverse; consumers read one publisher at a time.

Failure modes?

Control totals match but value doesn't — valuation/price mapping, the Finance row. Late receipts posted against legacy orders — the cut-off list. Serials without masters — pre-load. Devices on old screens — readiness row, rehearsed fallback. The one extract expert on vacation — documented, rehearsed by a second.

Trade-offs?

Three mocks cost weeks — they are the weeks that would otherwise be spent live. A 72-hour freeze delays receipts — priced with operations, scheduled off-peak. Zero tolerance on serials produces exceptions — each is a real unit whose state is unknown.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

Before the switch instant the legacy WMS is [BoR] ; after it, S/4 is. The diagram shows the load path once

(per mock and live) and the two interface re-points.
+-------------------+ extract file + control totals   +--------------------+
| Legacy WMS        | (count, qty, value by class),   | Staging + mapping  |
| [BoR until T0]    | per mock / live, 72-h freeze -->| sku_xref, site_map |
| item/bin/status   |                                 | status_map uom_map |
+---------+---------+                                 | serial_xref        |
          | in-transit list (receipts/shipments       +---------+----------+
          | in flight), freeze batch, once            | staging.validated
          v                                           v LTMC / 561 by SKU x
+-------------------+                        +---------------------------+
| Cut-off runbook   | open orders -> convert | SAP S/4HANA [BoR from T0] |
| freeze.started    |----------------------->| MATDOC BWART 561 (+serial |
| device readiness  |                        | EQUI/SER03), MARD, MBEW   |
+-------------------+                        +------+-----------+--------+
+-------------------+ 940/945/947/846 via CPI,      |           | stock by
| 3PL WMS (if kept) | re-pointed at T0, event/daily |           | SKU x SLoc
| bin grain         |<------------------------------+           | x type, 2 h
+-------------------+                                           v after load
+-------------------+ availability.zeroed(legacy) then  +--------------------+
| Salesforce / OMS  | availability.published(S/4) --->  | Recon engine (BI)  |
| promise consumer  |<-- S/4 ATP / snapshot, event      | delta by cause/tol |
+-------------------+                                   | -> go/no-go pack   |
| Finance (FI/CO)   |<-- clearing acct value by class -- signs tolerance first

E10 — Elementum design engagement: the returns / RMA flow — premise → test → refurb / return-to-vendor / scrap

Use for: reverse-logistics, warranty-recovery, disposition design questions. Record behind it: bucket state machines with one owning system per transition (S1, S2), reason-coded reconciliation (S14), serial-grain reconciliation at cutover (S18).

Honest line. No RMA program run end to end; the design generalizes the serialized state-machine discipline. Volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. Customers disconnect or swap; techs bring units back; some go straight to a returns bin and are never tested; refurb yield is unknown; warranty recovery from vendors is leaking. Design the reverse flow so every returned serial is tested, graded and dispositioned, and in-warranty failures go back to the vendor. Books: SAP for serial state and stock; the RMA system for reason and test result. Primary metric: returned units dispositioned within SLA, and the warranty recovery rate.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[Installed base] --disconnect/swap (RMA w/ reason code)--> [Return-in-progress]
--pickup / tech return (651 by serial)--> [Returns-untested, market RMA SLoc]
--weekly consolidation (STO)--> [Central test center] --test-->
  [Refurb-good A/B (321)] | [Repairable] | [Failed (344 blocked)]
Failed + in-warranty --> RTV w/ vendor RMA number (122 / return PO) -> credit
Failed + out-of-warranty --> scrap (551, with certificate)
No test, no grade -- an untested return that is promisable is a truck roll

Invariants. Every returned serial passes through test — no grade, no availability. Refurb identity: same serial, same material, a grade attribute — never re-numbered, so the asset history stays continuous. Warranty eligibility computes from the serial's own receipt or install event per the vendor's contract term — never from a person's memory of a PO date. A return-to-vendor without the vendor's RMA number is blocked — the credit would be denied. Scrap of an in-warranty serial is prevented by rule; the exception needs Finance approval.

(c) Design. Return order with reason code at initiation; receipt by serial into returns-untested; weekly consolidation to one central test bench — one grading standard beats faster-but-unreliable grading on the truck; test-result events drive the state machine; graded availability published with the grade so the order system can decide which customers receive grade B (policy, not technology); the RTV credit reconciliation as a standing report. Idempotency: serial + event type + document; a repeated test result replaces only if newer and flags the earlier; a repeated return receipt for the same RMA is rejected.

(f) Metric. Dispositioned within SLA ≥ 95% within 14 days; warranty recovery on in-warranty failures ≥ 80%. Illustrative dollar: 48K returns/yr × 75% refurb yield × $120 replacement cost avoided ≈ $4.3M/yr of new-unit purchases displaced (net of ~$25 test cost on all returns ≈ $3.1M net); warranty recovery lift 40% → 80% on 8K failures × 60% in-warranty × $150 ≈ $290K/yr. Supporting: returns-untested aging p95 < 14 days; refurb field-failure rate ≤ new-unit rate + margin; scrap with certificate = 100%.

Deep-dive probes.

Movement types and documents?

Return delivery to returns stock (651; to QI if inspecting at receipt — never straight to unrestricted); QI → refurb location on pass; unrestricted → blocked for fails; stock transfers for consolidation; return against the original PO for RTV; scrap with reason; a write-off for a non-return only with a case reference.

Warranty start — ship or install?

Contract-dependent; store the term and start event per vendor and compute eligibility from the serial's receipt or install event. Never a person's memory.

Failure modes?

A tech "returns" a unit that never posts — that's E1's unaccounted; the return is a swap event on the van. A test result for a serial not in returns-untested — hold; the state machine refuses. RTV shipped without a vendor RMA number — blocked. Scrap posted for an in-warranty serial — rule prevents it.

Trade-offs?

Centralized testing adds a hop and days — but one grading standard. "No test, no grade" means refurb stock lags during a surge — better than a truck roll on a bad unit.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

+----------------+ Case/ReturnOrder (reason,  +----------------------------+
| Salesforce     | expected serial), event -->| SAP S/4HANA [BoR]          |
| Account/Asset/ |<- serial state + grade,    | serial state: EQUI/SER03   |
| Case/RetOrder  |   availability, event/daily| return order VBAK/VBAP     |
+-------+--------+                            | MATDOC 651/655/351/101/    |
        | rma_id + serial_expected  +-------->| 321/344/161/122/551/702    |
        v                           |         | return PO EKKO/EKPO        |
+----------------+ return_event ->  |         +--+------+-----+------+----+
| Field app      | 651 by serial,   |            |      |     |      |
| van RMA SLoc   | daily ------------+  STO 351   |      |     |      | 810
+----------------+                     ->101,    |      |     |      | credit
+----------------+ rma_receipt -> 651/655, evt   |      |     |      | memo
| 3PL WMS        |<----------------------------+ |      |     |      | RBKP
| market RMA SLoc| 846 RMA SLoc snapshot, daily   |      |     |      v
| + central test | test_result -> grade ->        |   +-------------------+
|   cell         | 321/344, same day ------------+   | Vendors / EDI     |
+----------------+                                   | RTV 161/122 +     |
+----------------+ rtv 856 out / 214; prepaid  ----> | vendor RMA no.;   |
| Carriers       | label for customer returns        | 810 credit, month |
+----------------+                    +-------------+-------------------+
 aging by state, yield by grade,      | BI/BigQuery: serial_state, rma, |
 credit expected vs received, weekly->| test, disposition, rtv          |

E11 — Elementum design engagement: direct procurement of long-lead equipment — PO lifecycle, confirmations, ranked expediting

Use for: procurement design, vendor-reliability, long-lead-time questions. Record behind it: SAP purchasing data domains and EDI confirmations (S6, S9); lead-time distributions (S8); the carrier bid's constraint rules (S17).

Honest line. The indirect-procurement platform boundary is a knowledge pattern — I have not configured one; the boundary design is how I would frame the decision, not a claim. Volumes illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. Core equipment carries 12–40-week lead times, allocation risk, and multi-site delivery; today POs are placed off spreadsheets, confirmations live in email, and expediting is heroic. Design the procure-to-receive lifecycle in SAP for direct materials, define the boundary with the indirect-spend platform, and make confirmed-date reliability a managed metric. Primary metric: on-time-to-confirmed-date receipts, and PO lines without a vendor confirmation → 0.

(a) Stocks and flows · (b) first principles

[Demand: build plan / MRP need by week] --requisition (release strategy)--> [PR approved]
--source (source list / info record / contract)--> [PO sent, 850 out]
--confirm <= 48 h (855 -> confirmation record, MRP-relevant)--> [PO confirmed]
--at risk? expedite ranked by milestone-at-risk, not by who shouts--> [re-confirmed]
--ship (856) / receive (101) / invoice (810, three-way match)--> [closed]
OTIF measured BOTH ways: to-request (our planning discipline) and to-confirmation
  (vendor reliability) -- managed to confirmation; the gap published separately

Invariants. A confirmation without a date is not a confirmation — after 48 hours the line is a planner exception. Planning runs on the confirmed date, not the requested one, so the plan reflects reality rather than hope. Every urgent buy goes through a requisition — spreadsheets creeping back is a failure mode, blocked by the release strategy. A PO changed after confirmation without re-confirmation is flagged.

(c) Design. Confirmation control made MRP-relevant so the vendor's date replaces the schedule line in planning; expedite list ranked by the milestone at risk × value, refreshed weekly; for allocated long-lead items: outline agreements with scheduled releases, dual sourcing where qualified, and a strategic buffer sized by the p99 lead-time tail against the build plan's variance (toolkit, labeled). The direct/indirect boundary as a category table owned with Procurement and Finance: items that become stock or project stock → SAP MM; services, consumables expensed at receipt, tools, software → the indirect platform, which posts to the GL via the standard integration and never creates stock.

(f) Metric. Receipts on or before the vendor's confirmed date ≥ 95%; PO lines lacking a confirmation after 48 h → 0 — measured per line, monthly per vendor. Illustrative dollar: a late shipment that idles a build week costs ~$50K in crew and deferred activations; confirmation discipline plus ranked expediting avoiding ~15 such weeks/yr ≈ $750K/yr; the measured-lead-time buffer that prevents one allocation stockout protects a quarter's plan — the client Finance's number, not mine. Supporting: requested-vs-confirmed gap published; receipts without ASN → 0 for EDI vendors; spreadsheet POs → 0.

Deep-dive probes.

Confirmation control — how does it change planning?

With an MRP-relevant confirmation category, the confirmed date and quantity replace the schedule line for planning; the supply view shows the confirmed receipt. A PO with no confirmation shows the requested date — which is why the 48-hour exception exists.

OTIF to confirmation or to request?

Measure both, manage to confirmation: it isolates vendor reliability from our own over-asking. The requested-vs-confirmed gap is its own planning-discipline metric.

Allocated items with 40-week lead times — what do you do?

Outline agreements with scheduled releases; dual source where qualified; a strategic buffer sized from the p99 lead-time tail and the build plan's variance — toolkit, labeled; expediting ranked by the milestone at risk, not by who shouts.

Where exactly is the direct/indirect boundary?

Items that become stock or project stock live in SAP MM; services, expensed consumables, tools and software live on the indirect platform, which posts accruals to the GL and never creates stock. The boundary is a category table owned with Procurement and Finance — decided once.

Failure modes?

"Acknowledged" with no date — unconfirmed. A PO changed after confirmation without re-confirmation — block or flag. A receipt posted to the wrong plant or project — account-assignment check. A confirmation for a closed PO — exception. Spreadsheets creeping back for urgent buys — the release strategy requires a requisition.

Trade-offs?

Confirmation discipline creates exceptions in month one — that is the diagnostic; it reveals the unreliable vendors. Project stock improves site accountability but fragments the pool — use it only when the site is fixed.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

Direct (becomes MM or project stock) lives entirely in S/4; Coupa handles indirect and never creates stock

(clarifier #7).
+---------------+ WBS need / reservation +-------------------------------+
| SAP PS (WBS)  | RESB, weekly --------->| SAP S/4HANA MM [BoR: direct]  |
| PROJ/PRPS     |<- 101 Q project stock  | MD01 -> EBAN -> EKKO/EKPO/EKET|
+---------------+   by WBS, event        | EORD, EINA/EINE, MARC-PLIFZ   |

+---------------+ 850 PO out, event      | LIKP/LIPS inbound, MATDOC 101 |

E12 — Elementum design engagement: smart vending machines — the consignment ledger, and why a tech van is a vending machine

Use for: open-ended system design, event-ledger design, consignment/working-capital questions — the showpiece design. Record behind it: the real-time decrement ledger with idempotent events and daily reconciliation to a settlement book (S1); one unit in one bucket, counts change the book only through posted documents (S1, S14); lower-of-two when ledger and physical disagree (S1, S18).

Honest line. I have not built a vending network or run a consignment program; the par-level and safety-stock formulas are the MicroMasters toolkit, labeled; every number is illustrative.

The problem as I restate it. A network of connected vending machines sells product with no human at the counter: slots with a planogram, dispenses on payment, trucks on refill routes, machines sometimes offline. Design the inventory ledger, the payment flow, the replenishment and the reconciliation so the operator, the supplier and Finance agree on what was sold, what is on the shelf, and who owns it. Books: the dispense-event ledger for units, the ERP as the settlement book; the payment processor for money. Primary metric: fill rate (dispenses served / attempted) with shrink % inside tolerance.

Why this design matters beyond vending. It is the consignment question in its purest form: if the supplier owns the product on our shelf until it is dispensed, that is special stock K — receipt creates custody, not liability; the dispense is the consumption that creates the liability; month-end is a settlement, not an invoice. And a field tech's van is a vending machine: the van is the machine, the install is the dispense, the weekly restock is the refill, the van kit is the planogram. E1 is the own-stock version of this design; E12 is the version where the vendor owns the unit until the dispense — for a capital-hungry operator, a working-capital lever: hundreds of vans of equipment stop sitting on the balance sheet until the day of install.

(a) Clarifiers · stocks and flows · (b) the math

Clarifiers first (five): scale (default 500 machines x 40 slots x ~12 dispenses/day
  ~ 6,000 events/day) · ownership (own stock vs consignment K -- K is the interesting
  case) · connectivity (assume a machine can be dark for hours) · payment (offline auth
  allowed to a small cap) · replenishment (our drivers, fixed weekly routes, handheld)
[Depot (own or K)] --load route truck--> [In-transit per route x stop] --refill event
(counted_before + qty_added + planogram version)--> [Slot on-hand] --dispense (payment
auth -> motor -> capture; event_id = machine x slot x seq)--> [Sold / consumed (411 K)]
Monthly: settlement = Sigma consumption x agreed price (MRKO -- a settlement, not an
  invoice), three-way matched: consumption vs delivery notes vs refill counts
Par per slot = mu_d*(R + L) + z*sigma_d*sqrt(R + L), capped at slot capacity C
  e.g. mu=1.2/day, sigma=0.8, R=7, L=1, z=1.65 -> 9.6 + 3.7 ~ 13; capacity 15 holds;
  if capacity were 10, the slot is capacity-bound -> the ROUTE INTERVAL moves, not the par
Refill trigger: on_hand < Par - mu_d x (days to next scheduled visit)
Route ranking: Sigma_slots P(stockout before next visit) x margin per dispense
Shrink_slot = ledger_before_refill - counted_before; shrink % reason-coded per route
Worked network: 20,000 slots · ~6,000 events/day (~250/hr, 10x lunch peak ~2,500/hr --
  correctness is the hard part, not throughput) · weekly routes -> ~71 visits/day ->
  3 trucks x ~25 stops · shrink 1.5% of ~42,000 refilled units/wk x $1.50 ~ $50K/yr

Invariants. Every unit is in exactly one stock; the dispense event is minted at the edge (journaled before the motor turns) so a crash between dispense and send still produces exactly one event. Money is stricter than units: auth is synchronous or offline-capped; capture is at-least-once with idempotent ids; a refund is a compensating event referencing the original — never an edit. The count changes the book only through a posted refill count; the settlement is reproducible from the event store or it is not trusted.

(f) Metric. Fill rate ≥ 98% with stockout slot-hours published per route; shrink ≤ 1.5% (illustrative tolerance). Illustrative dollars: 500 machines × 12 dispenses × $2.00 ≈ $12K/day of revenue; a 2-point fill-rate lift on a network stocking out 5% of the time recovers ≈ $85K/yr; the van-consignment analogue defers cash on ~500 vans × 30 units × $150 ≈ $2.25M of stock off the balance sheet until install — Finance's number to confirm, not mine. Supporting: dispense → ledger latency p95 < 60 s online, backlog drained < 10 min after reconnect; payment success ≥ 99%; offline exposure loss < the margin offline enables.

Deep-dive probes.

Event ordering with clock skew?

Order by the machine's monotonic sequence number per machine × slot, never by device wall-clock; carry both device and received timestamps. Cross-machine order doesn't matter — each machine is its own stream; device time is corrected from the heartbeat offset for reporting only.

Idempotency key design?

machine × slot × sequence, minted at the edge and persisted in the journal before the motor turns — a crash after the dispense and before the send still produces one id. The server keeps a per-machine high-water mark plus a short dedupe window.

Offline authorization limit?

Per machine: a cap on count and amount per offline hour, decaying as the outage lengthens; per card: one offline auth per machine per day; a floor limit on price. Set the cap where expected decline loss < the margin the offline window enables.

How do you detect a jammed slot from data?

Auth without a capture on the same slot; dispense events with a jammed outcome; a slot whose ledger never moves while neighbours do; a refill count equal to the ledger plus a run of voided auths. Two together raise the alert before the customer calls.

Reconciliation cadence?

Per dispense: auth ↔ capture ↔ event, continuous. Per refill: count vs ledger per slot — the physical inventory. Daily: captures vs the processor settlement file. Monthly: consumption vs supplier delivery notes vs refill counts — the three-way match behind the settlement. Unit-ledger tolerance zero; shrink tolerance negotiated in the consignment agreement.

Consistency model and scale?

Eventually consistent projections over a strongly ordered per-machine event log; partition by machine id; consumers idempotent on event id; projections rebuilt from the log on demand. Six thousand events a day is small — the design holds the same shape at 50,000 machines; only the partition key count changes.

Fraud?

Repeated offline auths on one card across machines; refund-outcome clusters on one machine; shrink concentrated on one route or driver; door-open events without a refill event. Controls: offline caps, remote lock, driver-level shrink reporting, refund rate per machine on the daily dashboard.

Solution flow — the system map (proposed)

and settlement (special stock K, MRKO ), the payment processor is [BoR] for money. In the GFiber analogue the machine is the tech van and the dispense is the install.

3.99 — Cross-cutting: how I open any "design X" prompt (say this in the first minute)

  1. Book of record and metric first — "SAP is the book; the metric is unaccounted units per contractor per month — agree?"
  2. Stocks on the whiteboard — every bucket a unit can sit in; then the flows, each with the document that records it.
  3. The invariant that is broken today — a unit in two stocks, a flow with no document, a number with two owners.
  4. Minimal design the invariants force — then components, grain, events, controls, NFRs.
  5. Sequence the phases (Reach × Impact × Confidence ÷ Effort) — and say which invariant overrides the score.
  6. One metric, one dollar — illustrative, labeled, measured from posted documents.
  7. How I'd run it — point to §3.0 once; never re-explain the method per prompt.

4.2 Never-say list (one glance before every conversation)

  • Never "I built the model" as a day job — "I owned the requirement/definition/rule; the data team or engineers built; the toolkit is from MIT."
  • Never "I configured S/4," "I deploy SAP," or "I wrote ABAP" — "I led the integration program and owned the data contract and definitions; the SAP team / SI configured and coded to the spec I signed." The only platform I configured myself is Manhattan DOM.
  • Never claim Salesforce, Coupa, Teamcenter, VMI or serial-profile implementation experience — "adjacent, bridge honestly," and name the pattern. The §3 designs carry their honest lines for exactly this reason.
  • Never: "6 NA sites" · "500+ product families" · 60→95% inside the CM-ingest story · a $ figure for the carrier bid · "$1M routing guide" · "$1.00 → $0.03" · "10×" · "400 devices failed" · cohort sizes · $2M + $1.5M added together · $5M "avoidance" and "annual reduction" as two results.
  • Never present a modeled number as record: the SNB drift dollars (S3), the bid cycle-time value (S17), the S1 oversell dollars, the S2 labor dollars are modeled — say "modeled from these inputs" and give the basis. Numbers with no basis stay uninvented: direct-report headcounts, carrier-bid award savings, realized dollars on the $10M initiative. Say "I don't have a canonical figure and won't invent one; what I can defend is…"
  • Never "we" for an action that was mine; name the team's part once, explicitly.
  • Never volunteer only successes — S4 (the rule I got wrong), S18 (the RF miss), S16 (the security-review miss) and the UAT-crash bench story are the failure stories; Amazon explicitly probes for lessons from mistakes.
  • Amazon-specific: never tell the same story to two interviewers — the §1.1 map exists so each LP has a primary, backup and third. Never carry a §1.3-conflicted number into the room before resolving which document is right. Every modeled dollar gets its basis label out loud; the loop packet's "my model, Finance's rates" discipline applies to every figure here.

Final rule of the packet. This document and the loop packet are one system: this one carries the depth (gated STARs, phase probes, impact models, the design round); the loop packet carries the loop mechanics (interviewer tracker, the 128-question AIQB bank, writing defense, video execution). Rehearse from both; resolve the §1.3 conflicts before the loop; and in the room, say less than you know — the pitch ends on the primary metric, and the probes are how the interviewer fills their worksheet in your favor.
